# Adversarial review of the brief and the baseline

Stage 1 of `prompts/01-execution-prompt.md`. This review changes nothing outside `docs/review/`. The brief, the code, the tests and the settings are exactly as committed in `4823b09 Package as delivered` and `bfcb188 Lock dependencies`.

## Ground

**This review ran on Linux, not on the Windows 11 work computer the execution prompt assumes.** Everything about Windows was checked against Microsoft's documentation, or against psutil's Windows code shipped in `.venv` and its published C sources, and is marked accordingly. Nothing here was run on Windows. What only that machine can confirm is listed under **Not checked**, most urgent first within each lens, and in R137.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Operating system | Ubuntu 24.04.5 LTS, kernel 7.0, 8 logical cores, about 450 processes, cgroup v2 (`cpu memory pids` delegated to the user, no `io`), systemd 255, time zone UTC-5 |
| Shell | bash 5.2.21 |
| Python | 3.14.5, chosen by uv because nothing pins a version (`pyproject.toml` allows 3.12 and later; see R124) |
| uv | 0.11.14 |
| Packages of note | psutil 7.2.2, SQLite 3.50.4, ruff 0.17.6 |
| Reviewer | Claude Code 2.1.322. The ground work and the first lens run used Fable 5.2. The lens run, the verification and this report were finished on Opus 5.5 after Fable's usage limit stopped the first run (see `00-working-notes.md`) |
| Git | `main` at `bfcb188`. `origin` is https://github.com/therealestmatthew/submeter-pc-monitor and answers `git ls-remote`. **Pushing `main` was refused by the session's permission checks twice and is still owed.** |

Baseline checks, with full output in `docs/review/ground/`:

| Check | Result |
| --- | --- |
| `uv run ruff check` | All checks passed |
| `uv run ruff format --check` | 51 files already formatted (26 Python, 25 Markdown) |
| `uv run mypy src tests` | Success: no issues found in 25 source files |
| `uv run pytest` | 20 passed |
| `uv run python scripts/platform_check.py` | 6 results, 0 failed. 452 processes, 298 restricted, 0 without a readable command line. One full snapshot: median 114.3 ms, worst 119.3 ms over 20 runs |

Measurements taken on a quiet machine before the lenses ran (`docs/review/probes/measure/`):

| What | Result |
| --- | --- |
| Baseline psutil snapshot, 20 runs | median 111.2 ms, worst 121.8 ms for 447 processes. That is 74.6 ms per 300 processes (7.5 times the 10 ms switching rule) and 11.1% of a core at a 1 s interval, against limits of 3% active and 1% idle |
| Direct `/proc` reader, stat only | median 11.2 ms for 448 processes, 7.5 ms per 300 |
| Direct `/proc`, stat + statm + io | median 23.2 ms for 449 processes, 15.5 ms per 300, 2.3% of a core at 1 s |
| Whole baseline tick without storage | median 125.9 ms, 12.6% of a core at 1 s |
| Collector-shaped process, 120 ticks at 1 s | resident 39 MB, unique 35 MB, 14.1% of a core |
| Raw store | 12.6 process rows and 3 workload rows a tick; 120 bytes a row; 0.36 GB per 48 h |
| CPU time no process accounts for, under polling | 1.7% of capacity when quiet, 13.7% with one spawning loop, 51.6% with four |

How the findings were produced:
1. Eight read-only lens reviewers (A Windows, A Linux, B to G) wrote probes under `docs/review/probes/<lens>/` and returned 145 leads. A ninth reviewer (H, scope) worked over those leads. The raw lens reports are in `docs/review/lenses/`.
2. The leads were grouped into distinct findings. For each finding, an adversarial verifier re-read the cited lines, re-ran the probes or wrote new ones (`docs/review/probes/verify-*/`), and re-fetched the vendor documentation behind each critical and high finding.
3. A second pass then audited every critical and high finding again (`docs/review/probes/audit/`). It lowered six severities, merged four duplicates and corrected several proposed changes so that they no longer conflict. It also checked that each of the lead reviewer's own 38 leads reached a finding (`docs/review/probes/coverage/`). Its notes are the **Audit** lines under each finding. The records behind this report are in `docs/review/verify/`.

No lead was rejected outright. Many were merged, narrowed or lowered in severity. Every lead that went in was checked again by code, probe or documentation, and two findings remain suspected.

## Summary

**Counts.** 116 findings, R38 to R153: 102 defects and 14 scope findings.

| | Critical | High | Medium | Low | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Defects | 6 | 11 | 68 | 17 | 102 |
| Scope (lens H) | 0 | 2 | 9 | 3 | 14 |

114 findings are verified and 2 suspected. Recommended dispositions: 97 `fix now`, 13 `plan` and 6 `defer`. 42 findings need your decision.

**The ten that matter most**

1. **R38 and R39: gaps.** Any interval over 30 s zeroes all CPU, including `submeter top --seconds 31..60` today. A gap is also stored as covered, zero-use time, so after every sleep an hour reads 0.29 cores instead of 2.00 and still counts as well covered. Write the two fixes as one specification, because each alone loses the CPU the other keeps (see their Audit lines).
2. **R40: child-time recovery double counts.** `docs/17`'s rule for recovering reaped children's CPU from the parent's counters double counts every child that polling saw. On Linux the same holds for I/O in the shipped baseline today.
3. **R41: the Windows Idle process.** On Windows the provider reads pid 0 as a process whose CPU is the machine's idle time, which then lands in Restricted and breaks reconciliation.
4. **R42: nothing can be "unavailable".** Unreadable values are stored as 0, against AGENTS.md. This needs a decision on what a partial workload total means.
5. **R43: fixed rule 1 on Linux.** Tagging a run the usual systemd way, with a delegated scope, switches on the cpu controller and changes how the user's CPU is shared.
6. **R45: reconciliation.** System and Idle are never defined, the baseline test checks only conservation, and the measured CPU that polling misses (13.7% to 51.6% under spawn loads) has nowhere honest to go.
7. **R44 with R142: hot and cold sets.** These cannot be built on the sampler as designed: cold CPU is lost and exits are invented, or it lands as 10x spikes. The scope lens's cut and the defect's option (a) are the same decision.
8. **R46: memory.** On Linux workload memory is a sum of RSS, overstating multi-process workloads, and the record has one memory field for the two units the design needs.
9. **R53: bucket rules.** The raw reader selects samples by their end and the roll-up assigns them by their middle, so per-minute roll-ups lose CPU.
10. **R54: one process can stop storage.** A single process with a non-UTF-8 argument or name stops every raw write while it lives.

**What blocks planning.** The plan's phase list depends on the scope rulings (R140 to R153), above all:
- which operating system gets the platform layer in version 1 (R151);
- whether tagged runs move to phase 1 (R141);
- whether the minute tier, the dashboard and API, the MCP server and the guest collector stay.

Four structural decisions also shape the work breakdown:
- the sampler model (R44 and R142);
- what reconciliation means (R45);
- where the program loops sit in the layer rule (R108);
- the dependencies AGENTS.md requires you to approve (R109).

Finally, every timing in this review is from Linux. The plan's Windows estimates, including the Python against Rust decision, need the probes in R137 run on the owner's machine first.


## Findings

Numbered from R38. Defects come first, ordered by severity; the scope findings of lens H follow, in that lens's own ranking. Every finding was re-checked by an adversarial verifier that re-read the cited lines, re-ran or wrote probes (under `docs/review/probes/verify-*/`) and re-fetched the vendor documentation behind critical and high findings. Each finding's **Verification** line says what that check found. The lens reports these were merged from are in `docs/review/lenses/`, and the verified records in `docs/review/verify/`. A second adversarial pass then audited every critical and high finding, merged duplicates and checked that each of the lead reviewer's own 38 leads reached a finding; its notes are the **Audit** lines.


## Critical (6)


### R38. The fixed 30 s gap rule zeroes all CPU at cadences the settings accept, and `submeter top --seconds 31..60` prints zeros

- **Lens** B, C, E · **Severity** critical · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** sampler.py:39 gap_after_s=30.0; :55 gap = elapsed > gap_after_s or wall-elapsed > 2; :67 cpu_busy_s=0.0 if gap; :78-79 every process cpu_s=0.0. config.py:46-51 idle 1s-60s, cold 5s-60s; :92 on_battery_multiplier up to 10; no gap setting. docs/08:70-71 multiplier stretches every interval; overhead guard lengthens intervals (docs/17:230). cli.py:79 top max=60.0, :87 Sampler() default. docs/14:244 states the fixed 30 s rule. docs/17:75 'a change of cadence never loses or invents CPU time'. verify-1/gap_rule.out.txt: part 1 'idle 60s x on_battery_multiplier 10  accepted; 600.0 s between samples'; part 2 '29 s: gap=False ... credited 14.5' vs '31 s: gap=True, process used 15.5 CPU-s, credited 0.0; machine busy recorded 0.0'; part 3 'top --seconds 2 ... CPU-s summed 0.470' vs '--seconds 31 ... 448 processes, interval_s 31.1, CPU-s summed 0.000'.
- **Impact.** Any interval over 30 s, whether set in the config, stretched by the battery multiplier or lengthened by the overhead guard, records zero CPU for every workload and zero machine busy time. Attribution totals, trends and the reconciliation read zero while the machine is busy. The documented `top` command prints 0.00 cores for every workload when --seconds is 31 to 60. A stall in which both clocks keep running also loses its CPU, although it is not a suspend.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/collector/sampler.py: ingest() takes the expected interval for this tick. A tick is a gap only when elapsed > 3 x expected + slack, or when the suspend test fires (see G9). Do not zero CPU or I/O deltas in a gap batch: the counters are cumulative, so the deltas are exact, and a suspended process accrues nothing. The flag controls coverage only (G2). src/submeter/cli.py top: pass --seconds as the expected interval. docs/14:244 and docs/05 step 3: state the rule. Tests: a 60 s interval is not a gap; a 35 s stall at a 1 s cadence is a gap that keeps its CPU. Second problem, from C-storage#2: rollup.py:110 puts a sample whole in the bucket that holds its midpoint, so once 60-600 s samples are legal, roll_up must split a sample across bucket edges in proportion to time, and docs/17:123 finality must be at least the longest interval.
- **Cost.** About 15 lines in sampler.py and cli.py, 2 tests, a sentence each in docs/05 and docs/14. The proportional split in rollup.py is about 15 more lines.
- **Verification.** I re-read sampler.py:39-69, config.py:43-92, cli.py:77-105 and docs/08:57-71, and found no gap setting, no caller that passes gap_after_s, and no cross-validation of the intervals. I wrote verify-1/gap_rule.py, which feeds the real load_settings and Sampler and runs the real `top` through typer's CliRunner, with sleep replaced by a clock offset so nothing was re-timed. The results match the leads exactly, including a 29 s vs 31 s boundary that the leads did not show. Refutation attempt: the collector loop is not built, so a builder could pass a larger gap_after_s. But docs/14:244 states 30 s as fixed behaviour and nothing ties it to cadence, and `top` is shipped baseline code that is wrong today, so critical stands. The cluster holds a second, distinct problem (C-storage#2 part 3: samples of 60 s or more overflow minute buckets and arrive after finality). I verified it by reading rollup.py:110 and kept it as a secondary note under the main finding.
- **Audit.** Kept at critical. Re-read sampler.py:39 (gap_after_s=30.0), :55 (gap test), :67 and :78-79 (CPU and busy zeroed in a gap), cli.py:79 (top accepts --seconds up to 60) and :87 (default Sampler()), and config.py:46-51 and :92 (idle up to 60 s, cold up to 60 s, battery multiplier up to 10). Re-ran verify-1/gap_rule.py parts 1, 2 and 4 (logic only), and the output matched the stored file: '29 s: gap=False ... credited 14.5' against '31 s: gap=True, process used 15.5 CPU-s, credited 0.0; machine busy recorded 0.0'. Shipped baseline code shows a wrong number today: `submeter top --seconds 31..60` prints 0.00 cores for every workload. docs/14:244 states the fixed 30 s rule as designed behaviour, and docs/17:269 tells the builder to put the collector loop 'around the existing sampler', so a builder is not steered away from the rule. Critical holds on the top defect alone.
- **Audit.** Correction: (1) The fix reverses behaviour that a test pins and a document states: tests/test_baseline.py:226-233 asserts that a gap batch has cpu_s == 0.0 and cpu_busy_s == 0.0, and docs/14:244 says 'deltas are zero'. Both must change with it, so the change is a reversal of a pinned behaviour, not just a new threshold. (2) The fix cannot ship apart from G2's. When a gap sample keeps its CPU, roll_up's midpoint rule (rollup.py:110) puts it in a bucket in the middle of the suspend. Under G2's add() rule, docs/review/probes/audit/g1g2_fix_compose.out.txt shows that bucket then either 'close() returned None: the 4.0 CPU-s G1 kept are lost' or 'close() raised ZeroDivisionError'. The fix should place a gap sample's sums in the bucket that holds taken_at, and should never split a gap sample across suspended buckets. (3) G1's proportional split for samples of 60-600 s conflicts with G2's 'reject a non-gap sample whose interval_s exceeds width_s'. idle_interval = 60s alone gives a 60.05 s interval (same probe, part 2). Choose the split, or cap the effective cadence below the minute width.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#1, C-storage#2, E-baseline#6. Earlier findings touched: R08


### R39. A gap is stored and rolled up as covered zero-use time: one minute bucket claims 3601 covered seconds and the hour passes well_covered

- **Lens** B, C, E · **Severity** critical · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** sampler.py:59 gives a gap batch interval_s=max(elapsed, wall). rules.py:219-221 copies batch.interval_s into every WorkloadSample, which has no gap field (models.py:135-147). raw.py:97-129 writes the gap row AND the system_raw and workload_raw rows for the gap batch. rollup.py:49-53,60,68-71 add the interval to covered_s, the memory mean, active_s and both histograms. :110 puts the sample in the bucket that holds its midpoint. :171-173 well_covered means covered >= 0.5 x width. verify-1/gap_rule.out.txt part 4 (real Sampler, aggregate, RawFile, roll_up; 2.00 cores, 1 h suspend): 'batch interval_s 3601, workload row interval_s [3601.0]'; 'minute bucket at +2400 s: covered_s 3601 of 60, cpu_s 0, well_covered True'; 'hour bucket at +0 s: covered_s 4201, cpu_s 1200, mean cores 0.29, well_covered True' (truth: observed 600 s at 2.00 cores). docs/06:38 and docs/17:120 say covered seconds exist 'so a gap is never read as zero use'.
- **Impact.** After every sleep, one minute bucket claims more coverage than its width with zero use. The hour that holds it reads 0.29 cores instead of 2.00, and it passes well_covered, so baselines, the slow-growth check and trends take sleep as idle use (docs/06:102, docs/17:132). system_raw is stored the same way, so the machine busy share is understated too (C-storage#1 measured 12.5% vs 50%). The fixes listed for R08 and R24 are not present in the baseline.
- **Proposed change.** core/models.py: add gap: bool to WorkloadSample. attribution/rules.py aggregate(): pass batch.gap. store/rollup.py SeriesAccumulator.add(): for a gap sample, add cpu_s, I/O and started to the sums, but add nothing to covered_s, samples, active_s, the memory weight or either histogram. Reject a non-gap sample whose interval_s exceeds width_s. store/raw.py: store the flag (a gap column on workload_raw and system_raw), and have workload_samples() return it. Test: Sampler to bucket across a suspend gives covered_s <= width_s and well_covered False. Do not use C-storage#1's interval_s=0 variant: rollup.py:50 drops a row with interval 0 whole, which would lose the CPU that G1's fix keeps.
- **Cost.** About 15 changed lines in four modules, plus one test
- **Verification.** I re-read every cited line and grepped src/ for any reader of the gap table: only raw.py writes it, and nothing subtracts it from coverage. tests/test_baseline.py:404-418 checks only that a gap row exists. I drove the full chain myself in verify-1/gap_rule.py part 4 and got the same 3601-of-60 minute and a well-covered hour. Searched docs/05, 06, 14 and 17: docs/14:298 says only that a gap batch is written in full. The verdict is 'with changes' because one lead's fix (interval_s 0) conflicts with G1's fix, so I chose the gap-flag form that two of the three leads proposed.
- **Audit.** Kept at critical. Re-ran verify-1/gap_rule.py part 4 through the real Sampler, aggregate, RawFile and roll_up: 'minute bucket at +2400 s: covered_s 3601 of 60, cpu_s 0, well_covered True' and 'hour bucket at +0 s: covered_s 4201, cpu_s 1200, mean cores 0.29, well_covered True' (truth 2.00 cores over 600 s). The code agrees: sampler.py:59 sets interval_s=max(elapsed, wall) for a gap, rules.py:221 copies it, WorkloadSample (models.py:135-147) has no gap field, and rollup.py:53,60,68-71 add the interval to coverage, the memory weight, active time and both histograms. This happens after every real sleep, a daily event on a Windows laptop, and the stored hourly figures that feed baselines and trends are wrong (docs/06:38 and docs/17:120 promise the opposite). It is not covered by R08 or R24, whose fixes are not present in the baseline.
- **Audit.** Correction: The proposed rollup change is incomplete and conflicts with G1's in two places. (a) It says where a gap sample's sums do not go, but not which bucket they do go to. Under the midpoint rule (rollup.py:110) they land in a bucket in the middle of the suspend that nothing else covers. close() then returns None and loses the CPU, or divides by zero (rollup.py:90 memory_weighted / covered_s). merge() divides by the summed covered_s at :153. docs/review/probes/audit/g1g2_fix_compose.out.txt shows both. Specify that a gap sample goes to the bucket that holds taken_at, and that close() and merge() accept covered_s = 0. (b) Drop 'Reject a non-gap sample whose interval_s exceeds width_s'. Settings already accept idle_interval = 60s, which yields 60.05 s samples (same probe, part 2), and G1 proposes splitting such samples instead. Write G1's and G2's changes as one specification.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#2, C-storage#1, E-baseline#1. Earlier findings touched: R08, R24


### R40. docs/17's 'recover reaped children's CPU from the parent's child-time counters' double counts every child that polling saw, and drops a long-lived child's whole lifetime into one interval

- **Lens** A, B, E · **Severity** critical · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:71 gives the sentence with no subtraction rule. man7 proc_pid_stat(5), re-fetched, cutime: 'Amount of time that this process's waited-for children have been scheduled in user mode' (a lifetime total). verify-2/reap_counters.out.txt: 'baseline sampler, interval 1: child cpu 0.27 s' / 'this process's cutime+cstime rose at reaping by 0.27 s' / 'CPU if the child-time increase is added as the brief says: 0.54 s for 0.27 s used'. a-linux/child_times.out.txt: 'truth, from wait4 inside the parent: 13.23 s' / 'polling plus the parent's counter: 19.85 s (+50%)' / 'less the last sighting of each child that ended: 13.22 s'. child_times_tree.out.txt: the result is exact (6.15 vs 6.15) only when each ended process's last child-time counter is subtracted as well. Limits: reap_counters 'grandchild last seen with 0.15 s own CPU; this process's child-time rose by 0.07 s (the launcher only)'; child_times, SIGCHLD ignored: '0.00 s'. ProcessCounters has no child-time field (models.py:71-84).
- **Impact.** Built as written, polled spawn-heavy workloads on Linux are charged 50% more or worse, and 2x for a child that was seen until it ended. When a long-lived polled child exits, its whole lifetime of CPU lands in its parent's workload in one interval. Counters of 24,108 to 87,945 s exist on this machine. The spike reaches the live view and the trends. Orphans' time is recovered into the subreaper's workload (systemd --user), not the agent's.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:71, replace the sentence with: 'On Linux, also read each process's child time (cutime + cstime) and credit its increase to that process. Subtract, for every seen child that ended since the last tick, that child's last-seen own CPU and last-seen child time. This recovers descendants reaped inside the tree. Orphans re-parented to a subreaper are charged to the subreaper. Children of a parent that ignores SIGCHLD, and children not yet reaped, are not recovered. Never recover more than busy time less credited time in the interval.' core/models.py: add children_cpu_s: float | None to ProcessCounters now. In the P1 procfs reader (build list 05), add a test fixture with nested reaping.
- **Cost.** Three sentences in docs/17 and one model field now. The subtraction, about 30 lines, belongs to the P1 /proc reader task.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/17:71, docs/05:65, sampler.py:80-87 and models.py:71-84. Wrote and ran verify-2/reap_counters.py: the unmodified PsutilProvider and Sampler credited a child 0.27 s while it was alive, then this process's cutime+cstime rose by 0.27 s at reaping, giving 0.54 s for 0.27 s used. Re-fetched man7 proc_pid_stat(5). Read the A-linux probe scripts. Their subtraction is exact, so the method works and the defect is the missing rule. R01 covers polling's blindness, not this recovery step. Merged the three leads. B's busy-time arithmetic (48.98 + 6.76 = 55.74 > 53.96) is only a bound, so I kept the direct measurements. Corrected one claim: orphans are not unrecoverable when every process's counter is read. They are recovered into the subreaper (child_times: the systemd user manager's counter rose 1.02 s), which is the wrong workload. Kept critical because following the literal instruction gives wrong numbers.
- **Audit.** Kept at critical. Re-ran verify-2/reap_counters.py through the unmodified provider and sampler. The child was credited 0.27 s while alive, this process's cutime+cstime rose by 0.28 s at reaping, and the run printed 'CPU if the child-time increase is added as the brief says: 0.55 s for 0.27 s used'. docs/17:71 gives the recovery rule with no subtraction, and the naive reading, crediting the increase, double counts every child that polling saw. A builder would not obviously avoid that. The a-linux tree probe shows the subtraction makes the result exact. The defect is in the specification only (models.py:71-84 has no child-time field), but following it as written gives wrong stored CPU on Linux, so critical stands. Absorb R2: same mechanism and same fix.
- **Audit.** Correction: The subtraction must be tied to the reaping, not to the child's last sighting. The baseline drops zombies (psutil_provider.py:102-103; see G8), so a child that has exited but is not yet reaped vanishes from the snapshot one or more ticks before its parent's child time rises. 'Subtract for every seen child that ended since the last tick' then lands in the wrong interval. The clamp swallows it there, and the next tick double counts. State that a zombie stays in the snapshot until its /proc entry is gone (G8's fix is a prerequisite), and that a pending subtraction is carried to whichever process's child time rises: the last-seen parent, or the subreaper if that parent has ended. The limits sentence overlaps R15, which edits docs/10:10 and docs/17:221.
- **Audit.** Absorbed 'On Linux a parent's /proc/PID/io absorbs the I/O of children it reaps, so polled workload I/O double counts every child polling saw'. Its evidence: verify-2/reap_counters.out.txt: 'child last seen: ... write_bytes 4.0 MiB' / 'this process's /proc/self/io rose at reaping by write_bytes 4.0 MiB, wchar 4.0 MiB' / 'baseline sampler, interval 1: child ... io 4.0 MiB' / 'interval 2: this process io 4.0 MiB (child gone)' / 'I/O a workload holding both is charged: 8.0 MiB for 4.0 MiB written'. a-linux/io_children.out.txt shows the same at 32 MB, and +32 MB after reaping a shell that only waited. Code: psutil_provider.py:77-78 calls io_counters (psutil reads /proc/PID/io); sampler.py:82-83 takes a plain delta; rules.py:208-209 sums it. Brief: docs/09-telemetry.md:12 lists '/proc/<pid>/io' with no caveat, and docs/17:71 has no rule. Its proposed change: docs/09-telemetry.md, Disk I/O row, Linux cell: '/proc/<pid>/io, which also includes children the process has reaped'. docs/17:71, next to R1's sentence: 'The same holds for /proc/<pid>/io: when a seen child ends, subtract its last-seen I/O counters from its reaper's next increase.' Add a test with this probe's shape to the P1 procfs reader (build list 05). The baseline sampler can apply the same subtraction from batch.exited and parent_pid. Audit reason for merging: The defect is real and in the baseline today. The re-run of verify-2/reap_counters.py printed 'this process's /proc/self/io rose at reaping by write_bytes 4.0 MiB' and 'I/O a workload holding both is charged: 8.0 MiB for 4.0 MiB written', and psutil_provider.py:77-78, sampler.py:82-83 and rules.py:208-209 take and sum the plain delta. It is the same kernel mechanism as R1: on reaping, the parent's signal struct absorbs the child's CPU (cutime/cstime) and its I/O accounting (/proc/PID/io). The fix is the same: subtract the last-seen counters of each seen child from its reaper's increase. It goes in the same docs/17:71 sentence (R2's own text says 'next to R1's sentence') and the same P1 procfs task (build list 05). One finding with one subtraction rule for CPU and I/O avoids two separately worded rules for one mechanism. Correction: The merged finding must keep R2's distinct facts: the I/O double count happens in the shipped psutil baseline now, not only in the planned /proc reader, and docs/09:12's Linux Disk I/O cell needs the 'includes reaped children' caveat. The same reap-timing correction given under R1 applies to the I/O subtraction.
- **Leads.** A-linux#5, B-measurement#11, E-baseline#5; A-linux#4. Earlier findings touched: R01


### R41. On Windows the provider reads pid 0 (System Idle Process) as a process whose CPU is the machine's idle time, and that time lands in Restricted

- **Lens** A, E · **Severity** critical · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** psutil_provider.py:44 maps every process_iter() entry and excludes no pid. In .venv psutil 7.2.2: _pswindows.py:636 'pids = cext.pids'; :736-737 name() returns 'System Idle Process' without opening the process; :938-948 cpu_times() falls back to _proc_info() user_time and kernel_time on a permission error; :789-811 memory falls back the same way. psutil release-7.2.2 proc_utils.c, re-fetched: 'if (pid == 0) { // otherwise we'd get NoSuchProcess return psutil_oserror_ad("automatically set for PID 0"); }'. proc_info.c, re-fetched: UserTime and KernelTime come from the SYSTEM_PROCESS_INFORMATION entry, with a pid 0 case only for create_time ('create_time = 0'). Its cmdline helpers open a handle through psutil_handle_from_pid, so cmdline is denied, restricted becomes True (psutil_provider.py:68-71), and the process goes to Restricted (rules.py:156). Microsoft Learn, 'Analyzing Processor Activity', re-fetched: 'The Idle process runs a thread on each processor... System Monitor and Task Manager both use the Idle process to calculate time when the processor is not busy. You can see processor time for the Idle process on the Processes tab in Task Manager (called the System Idle Process)'. a-windows-docs/windows_shaped_inputs.out.txt, stand-ins fed through the real provider and sampler: 'System Idle Process cpu_s 6.80 restricted True', 'sum of per-process CPU 7.84 vs busy 1.44'.
- **Impact.** On an idle 8-core machine, Restricted shows about 7 cores every interval and process CPU exceeds the machine's busy time. Reconciliation (docs/17:98) fails by the idle share, and the 'how much is restricted' check (windows-priorities.md:51) is swamped. The planned bulk snapshot reads the same SystemProcessInformation entries and inherits the problem.
- **Proposed change.** psutil_provider.py snapshot(): skip pid 0 on Windows (one condition). docs/17:71, Bulk polling: 'Exclude pid 0, the Idle process, whose time is idle time. Pid 4 (System) and the Registry, MemCompression and Secure System processes belong to System.' docs/reference/windows-priorities.md item 3 and scripts/platform_check.py: check that summed per-process CPU over 5 s does not exceed machine busy time by more than 5%.
- **Cost.** Two lines of code, one sentence of brief, and one platform check of about 10 lines.
- **Verification.** Read the .venv psutil 7.2.2 _pswindows.py fallbacks for pids, name, cpu_times, memory, create_time and io_counters. Re-fetched psutil release-7.2.2 proc_utils.c, proc_info.c and the Microsoft page, and all quoted text is there. The leads disagreed on the bucket: cmdline needs a process handle, which pid 0 cannot get, so pid 0 is restricted and goes to Restricted (A-windows#2), not Unattributed (E-baseline#4). In 7.2.2, create_time for pid 0 is 0: the C comment says Python converts it to boot time, but _pswindows.py:910-922 does not. So the first sighting credits nothing, and every later interval credits the idle delta. Searched docs/, scripts/ and src/ for 'idle process' and 'pid 0': nothing. Status is verified from the psutil code and Microsoft's text. The exact size on a real machine still needs one Windows run.
- **Audit.** Kept at critical. Read .venv psutil 7.2.2 _pswindows.py myself. :636 'pids = cext.pids' (EnumProcesses includes 0). :736-737 name() returns 'System Idle Process' without a handle. :938-948 cpu_times() falls back to _proc_info() user_time and kernel_time on a permission error. :910-922 create_time falls back the same way. :763-781 cmdline needs a handle in both branches, so it raises AccessDenied, psutil_provider.py:68-71 sets restricted, and rules.py:156 sends the process to Restricted. grep of docs/, docs/reference/, scripts/, src/ and tests/ for 'idle process', 'pid 0' and 'system idle' finds nothing that excludes it. On Windows, the first platform, the shipped `top` would show roughly the machine's idle cores as Restricted CPU. A shown number is wrong and the reconciliation fails, so critical stands. It was not run on Windows. The code path is verified, and the exact size needs the windows-priorities section 3 run.
- **Leads.** A-windows#2, E-baseline#4. Earlier findings touched: R07


### R42. Records cannot say 'unavailable': unreadable I/O is summed as zero and labelled polled, and the Restricted bucket shows 0 bytes of I/O

- **Lens** E, F · **Severity** critical · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `plan` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** verify-2/attribution_checks.out.txt: 'workload docker: source=polled io_read=4096 io_write=8192 (dockerd's I/O was None)'. e-baseline/interface_cases.out.txt: 'WorkloadSample with io_read_bytes=None: refused'; 'ProcessCounters with cpu_s=None: refused'. Code: rules.py:208-209 'io_read_bytes or 0'; rules.py:216 labels every workload except Restricted POLLED; models.py:142-145 are int fields; raw.py:43-44 are NOT NULL; rollup.py:56-57 sums them. verify-2/linux_reads.out.txt: '297 restricted ... I/O missing: 297, command line missing: 0, CPU above zero: 185'. AGENTS.md:44: 'A metric the system cannot deliver is reported as unavailable, never as zero'; docs/09:5 says the same.
- **Impact.** On Linux, any workload holding another user's process, such as a docker rule or a service, shows a partial I/O sum as a complete polled figure, and Restricted shows 0 B/s of I/O. Stored rows and rollups carry the zeros forward.
- **Proposed change.** models.py: make the I/O fields Optional on WorkloadSample and Bucket, and add a count of members with a field missing. aggregate: when any member's field is None, set the source to PARTIAL and fill the count. raw.py and rollup.py: nullable columns, and sums that keep None. cli prints n/a. Provider: read CPU and memory, the command line, and I/O in separate try blocks, and set restricted only when CPU or memory is unreadable. Record the change in docs/14.
- **Cost.** More than fifty lines across models, aggregate, raw, rollup, cli and tests. Every later provider and reader follows the same contract.
- **Decision.** When some members of a workload lack a field, is the workload figure (a) unavailable, or (b) the sum of the readable members plus a count of unreadable ones, labelled partial? Recommendation: (b), matching docs/17:78 'with whatever is visible'.
- **Verification.** Wrote verify-2/attribution_checks.py: a rule-matched process with I/O None next to a readable one gives a polled workload holding only the readable bytes. Ran verify-2/linux_reads.py: every restricted process lacks only I/O. Re-read the cited lines. One change: the path that zeroes CPU and memory (psutil_provider.py:65-66, 104-110) is rare. On Linux, /proc/PID/stat and statm are world-readable. On Windows, psutil falls back to SystemProcessInformation for cpu_times, memory_info and io_counters (.venv _pswindows.py:789-811, 938-948, 1022-1037). That path fires only when name()/exe() or create_time is denied, which is unverified on Windows. The I/O case is verified and is the main problem. Severity rests on the wrong number; the AGENTS.md line is a standing rule, not one of the three fixed rules. Moved to plan because the change passes fifty lines once raw and rollup are included.
- **Audit.** Kept at critical. Confirmed by reading the code. rules.py:208-209 'io_read_bytes or 0'. rules.py:216 labels every non-Restricted workload POLLED. models.py:144-145 has int fields. raw.py:43-44 has NOT NULL I/O columns. rollup.py:56-57 sums them. verify-2/attribution_checks.out.txt: 'workload docker: source=polled io_read=4096 io_write=8192 (dockerd's I/O was None)'. AGENTS.md:44 and docs/09:5 say unavailable is never shown as zero, and models.py:5 calls these classes the contract later providers build on, so the zero spreads. On Linux, other users' /proc/PID/io is unreadable, so Restricted shows 0 B of I/O and rule-matched daemon workloads show a partial sum labelled as complete. A shown number is wrong on a supported platform, so critical stands. On Windows, psutil's fallback (_pswindows.py:1022-1037) reads I/O for denied processes, so this is Linux-only today.
- **Audit.** Correction: The proposed provider change, 'set restricted only when CPU or memory is unreadable', would change who lands in Restricted on both platforms. On Windows, psutil's fallbacks make CPU and memory readable for nearly every process (_pswindows.py:789-811, 938-948), and on Linux /proc/PID/stat and statm are world-readable. Restricted would empty, and other users' processes would move to Unattributed, against docs/02:147-148 ('14 processes owned by other users') and the windows-priorities.md section 3 'how much is restricted' check. Keep restricted tied to unreadable attribution inputs (command line or owner), and record each field's availability separately through the Optional fields and missing-member count R8 already proposes. W6's change of threads to int | None is the same contract change and should be done with it.
- **Leads.** E-baseline#3, F-consistency#13. Earlier findings touched: R07


### R43. Tagging a run with a Delegate=yes systemd scope switches on the cpu controller for every sibling app group and changes how the user's CPU is shared (fixed rule 1)

- **Lens** A · **Severity** critical · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** Probe a-linux/cgroup_side_effects.out.txt: 'Delegate=yes: scope has [cpu memory pids]; app.slice passes down [cpu memory pids] while it runs; ... cpu.weight True'; 'Delegate=memory pids: ... app.slice passes down [memory pids]'; 'cpu controller not passed down to X and Y: X gets 20%' / 'passed down: X gets 50%'. Re-read now (verify-4/linux_platform_readonly.out.txt): app.slice passes down 'memory pids'; 22 groups directly below app.slice; user@N.service DelegateControllers=cpu memory pids. systemd 255 man page and https://www.freedesktop.org/software/systemd/man/latest/systemd.resource-control.html (re-fetched): Delegate=yes means 'all supported controllers are enabled for the unit'; '...any controllers that are delegated will be enabled for the parent and sibling units of the unit with delegation'; units with a controller enabled 'may be subject to resource control even if they do not have any explicit configuration'. https://docs.kernel.org/admin-guide/cgroup-v2.html (re-fetched) on cpu.stat: 'This file exists whether the controller is enabled or not.' Brief: docs/17-build-prompt.md:23 allows cgroup placement and forbids only limits; :42 sends the builder to 'cgroup v2 delegation' docs; :69 'Set no limits'. Nothing names which controllers.
- **Impact.** While any tagged run is live, the 22 sibling app groups switch from per-thread to per-group CPU sharing (one busy process in X against four in Y: 20% becomes 50%). That changes the scheduling of processes Submeter did not launch, which fixed rule 1 forbids, and it changes the tagged run's own CPU share, i.e. the figure being measured. It also exposes cpu.weight/cpu.max on those groups, 'a setting that enables' a priority change. The run's accounting needs none of it: cpu.stat exists without the cpu controller.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md after line 69: 'On Linux, delegate to the run's scope only controllers its parent slice already passes down (read the parent's cgroup.subtree_control first), normally memory and pids. Never enable the cpu or io controller anywhere and never write cpu.weight or cpu.max: enabling a controller turns it on for every sibling group, and cpu.stat is present without it.' docs/09-telemetry.md row 1, Linux column: 'cgroup v2: cpu.stat always (no cpu controller needed); memory and pids files when the parent slice passes them down'.
- **Cost.** Two sentences in two documents.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/17:23,42,62,69 and grepped docs/ for cgroup/delegat/systemd/cpu.weight: no controller guidance anywhere, so not handled; no R-number covers it. Did NOT re-run the probe: both parts would themselves enable cpu on app.slice or burn a CPU and so disturb the other verifiers' timing. Instead re-checked the state read-only with my own probe (app.slice 'memory pids', 22 siblings, user@ delegates cpu memory pids, no probe cgroups left) and confirmed the mechanism in the local systemd 255 man page and the re-fetched freedesktop and kernel pages (quotes above). The 20%/50% split matches per-thread vs per-group fairness exactly, which the man page's example also describes ('the cpu controller would not be enabled for units in those slices and CPU resources would be further allocated using other mechanisms, e.g. based on nice levels'). Severity kept critical: the brief authorises cgroup placement, points the builder at delegation docs whose default is Delegate=yes, and only forbids limits, so the natural build breaks rule 1. Changed the proposal from a fixed 'memory pids' list to 'only what the parent already passes down', because on a distro whose app.slice does not pass memory down, Delegate=memory would also enable memory on every sibling.
- **Audit.** Kept at critical. I re-checked this in the local systemd 255 man page (man systemd.resource-control). Under Delegate=: 'If true, delegation is turned on, and all supported controllers are enabled for the unit' and 'any controllers that are delegated will be enabled for the parent and sibling units of the unit with delegation'. Under 'Enabling and disabling controllers': 'Units for which a controller is enabled may be subject to resource control even if they don't have any explicit configuration.' The probe output (a-linux/cgroup_side_effects.out.txt) agrees: app.slice passes down cpu while a Delegate=yes scope lives, and a process's share moves from 20% to 50%. I did not re-run it, because it burns CPU and changes cgroup state. The brief sends the builder to 'cgroup v2 delegation' docs (17:42). Delegation is needed for any holder/payload layout that keeps counters after the payload empties, which is L2's own design. And Delegate=yes is the default form. So the natural build breaks fixed rule 1. Enabling cpu on 22 sibling groups changes their CPU distribution, and it exposes cpu.weight and cpu.max on them: literally 'a setting that enables' a priority change or cap (AGENTS.md rule 1). A broken fixed rule is critical by definition.
- **Audit.** Correction: The impact line 'changes the tagged run's own CPU share, i.e. the figure being measured' describes an observer effect, not a wrong number; cpu.stat still measures what was used. The rule-1 breach alone carries critical. The man page confirms that the fix works where the parent passes nothing down: 'Assigning the empty string will enable delegation, but reset the list of controllers'. So say 'Delegate= with the parent's pass-down list, or an empty list'. On systemd 254 or later, DelegateSubgroup= can also place the payload without moving processes by hand.
- **Leads.** A-linux#1. Earlier findings touched: none


## High (11)


### R44. The hot and cold sets cannot be built on the sampler as designed: cold CPU is lost and exits and starts are invented, or the cold CPU lands as 10x spikes

- **Lens** B, E · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** sampler.py:47-49 keeps only the last snapshot; :65 calls everything absent 'exited'; :84-87 credits 0 to an old process seen again; :104-112 hot_keys() is documented as 'Processes worth sampling at the active interval'. models.py:122-132 has one interval_s per batch. docs/05:45 'the hot set every 1 s and the cold set every 10 s'; docs/08:61,63; docs/17:71; R02. Nothing says which fields the cold set skips. verify-1/hot_cold.out.txt (1 hot process at 0.90 cores, 9 cold at 0.02, 30 ticks): 'A: ... cold credited 0.00 of 5.40; exits reported 27, starts reported 27 (truth 0 and 0)'; 'B: ... cold credited 5.40 of 5.40 ...; cold set's highest one-tick rate 1.80 cores (truth 0.18)'.
- **Impact.** Build-list item 3 has two possible readings, and both give wrong numbers on the existing sampler. Partial snapshots lose all cold-set CPU and inflate the started count, which is a cost metric. Carrying cold counters forward keeps the totals but puts 10 s of CPU into a 1 s interval, which corrupts per-tick cores, percentiles and spike detection. The phase-1 gate 'totals reconcile within 2%' would fail under the first reading.
- **Proposed change.** Decision first. Under option (a): docs/05 step 2, docs/17:71 and the docs/08 rows for active_interval and cold_interval should say that every tick reads cumulative CPU, memory and I/O for every process (the Windows bulk call returns them all), and that the hot and cold sets decide only which costly extras are read (USS, handles, working folder, command line). Reword the sampler.py hot_keys() docstring to match. Under option (b): Snapshot gains complete: bool, ProcessSample gains its own interval_s, and the Sampler keeps the last counters of processes absent from a partial snapshot.
- **Cost.** (a): about four sentences in three docs and one docstring. (b): about 30 lines in models.py and sampler.py, plus tests.
- **Decision.** Do the hot and cold sets decide (a) which costly per-process extras are read, with CPU, memory and I/O read for every process every tick, or (b) which processes appear in a snapshot at all, with per-process intervals added to the model? Recommendation: (a). The Windows bulk snapshot returns every process anyway, and (a) keeps one interval per batch and the sampler unchanged.
- **Verification.** I re-read the sampler and models, and I searched docs/05, 08, 14 and 17 and R02 for which fields a cold read skips: none says. I wrote verify-1/hot_cold.py, which feeds the real Sampler both ways, and reproduced both failure modes on a smaller synthetic set. I downgraded severity from critical (B) to high: the numbers are wrong only once someone builds the cold set on this sampler, the design is silent rather than wrong, and the phase-1 reconcile gate would catch reading (b). I did not re-time the /proc stat-only read cost that B cites for option (a), so that figure is the lead's.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. Confirmed by reading the code. sampler.py:49 replaces _known on every tick. :65 reports every process absent from the snapshot as exited. :84-87 credits nothing to an old process seen again. :99 together with rules.py:211 counts it as started. The hot_keys docstring (:105, 'Processes worth sampling at the active interval') describes reading A, partial snapshots. The verify-1/hot_cold.out.txt figures follow from those lines: 'cold credited 0.00 of 5.40; exits reported 27, starts reported 27' under A, and a 1.80-core one-tick rate against a true 0.18 under B. docs/05:45, docs/17:71, docs/08:61,63 and R02 never say what a cold read skips. Under reading A the P1 gate 'figures are within 5% of kernel counters' (docs/15:13) fails, which meets high. It is not critical, because no shipped path builds a cold set yet.
- **Audit.** Correction: G3's option (a) and H3 (batch-9) rewrite the same rows (docs/05:45, docs/17:71, docs/08:61,63, the hot_keys docstring) in incompatible ways. G3 (a) keeps cold_interval and redefines it as the cadence for costly extras. H3 deletes cold_interval, hot_cpu_threshold and hot_keys. Adopting both recommendations would give conflicting document edits, so present them to the owner as one decision. H3's 1 s to 2 s default change is a separate question.
- **Audit.** Overlap: H3 (scope) proposes the same change as option (a) and deletes cold_interval, which option (a) redefines. Decide them together.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#3, E-baseline#7. Earlier findings touched: R02


### R45. The reconciliation invariant leaves System and Idle undefined, the baseline 'reconciliation' test checks only conservation, and with System taken from counters the invariant fails under polled spawn loads

- **Lens** B · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/17:98: 'attributed plus unattributed plus system plus restricted plus idle CPU time equals total capacity within 2%'. Neither docs/12 nor docs/17 defines System or Idle, and grep shows rules.py:36 SYSTEM is never assigned. tests/test_baseline.py:268-271 asserts only that the sum over workloads equals the sum over processes. verify-2/attribution_checks.out.txt: the test's check '... holds' while 'workloads 1.00 + idle 1.00 = 2.00 of capacity 4.00; the missing 2.00 has no term in the baseline'. Gates: docs/01:8, docs/15:13 and docs/17:269 require 'totals reconcile within 2%'. b-measurement/reconcile_terms.out.txt, quiet machine: busy time no process accounts for is 4.98 CPU-s = 2.04% of capacity, and System from counters is 0.34%. measure/reconcile_under_spawn.out.txt: one spawner 'missing 18.55 CPU-s = 13.7% of capacity', four spawners 'missing 71.28 CPU-s = 51.6% of capacity'. docs/10:10 accepts about 70% undercount of polled children on Windows.
- **Impact.** If System is the remainder, the invariant cannot fail, and unseen child CPU appears as System beside a 'Reconciled' bar (docs/03:43). If System comes from counters, the P1 gate fails whenever polled workloads spawn children, which docs/10 accepts as normal on Windows, where no recovery exists. Either way the gate does not test what R07 intended.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17:98 and docs/12: define Idle as the per-core idle counters and System as kernel threads plus the interrupt and DPC counters. Add a sixth always-visible term, 'not seen by polling' = busy - (processes + System), shown beside polled workloads. State the check as: the six terms sum to capacity, and 'not seen' stays under a stated share on the quiet P1 fixture. Rename the baseline test as a conservation test and add a reconcile check that uses SampleBatch.cpu_busy_s and logical_cores. Show the new term in docs/03's 'Reconciled' line.
- **Cost.** Four sentences in docs/17 and docs/12, one line in docs/03, one test.
- **Decision.** Where does CPU time that polling never saw go? (a) Its own visible term, 'not seen by polling': the invariant holds and the gap is shown. (b) System: the invariant holds trivially, the gap is hidden, and System stops meaning kernel and interrupts. (c) Nowhere, and the 2% check may fail under polled spawn loads. Recommendation: (a), with R4's cgroup residual naming the unit on Linux.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/17:94-98, docs/01:8, docs/15:13, docs/17:269, tests/test_baseline.py:237-272 and rules.py. grep shows SYSTEM assigned nowhere. Wrote verify-2/attribution_checks.py: the baseline's assertion holds on a batch where half of capacity is unaccounted for. Read reconcile_terms.py: busy is the /proc/stat fields less idle and iowait, processes are credited as the sampler does, and the arithmetic is consistent. Did not re-time anything. R07's fix says 'a test enforces it', and that test is not in the baseline, so R07 does not justify a rejection. The Linux spawn figures were taken without R1's recovery, which would shrink them on Linux. On Windows, the first platform, there is no recovery, so the failure stands.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. grep finds rules.py:36 SYSTEM defined and never assigned anywhere in src/ or tests/. tests/test_baseline.py:268-271 checks only conservation (the sum over workloads equals the sum over processes), and verify-2/attribution_checks.out.txt shows that check holding while 'the missing 2.00 has no term in the baseline'. The gates (docs/15:13 P1 'totals reconcile within 2%', docs/15 P3, docs/17:269) require the invariant. docs/10:10 accepts a 70% polled undercount of short-lived children on Windows, the product's main use case (docs/17:60), so with System taken from counters the gate fails under real spawn loads. With System as the remainder, docs/03:43's 'Reconciled ... = 100%' hides the gap. Either way a gate or the attribution display fails, so high fits.
- **Audit.** Correction: The record overstates the gap: 'neither docs/12 nor docs/17 defines System' is not quite true. docs/17:96 names it 'System (kernel, interrupts)', and docs/02:137-143 describes it as 'Kernel time, interrupts, deferred calls and non-process memory' from 'counters' (docs/02:44,140). What is missing is the concrete counters and any code that produces it. The proposed definition, 'kernel threads plus the interrupt and DPC counters', must also say that those rows leave the processes term, or they count twice. On Linux, kernel threads (children of pid 2) are processes the poller already reads. On Windows the same applies to pid 4 and the rows R5 lists. B9's System item duplicates this finding's core and should defer to it.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#10. Earlier findings touched: R07, R01


### R46. On Linux workload memory is a sum of per-process RSS, overstating multi-process workloads, and the record has one memory field for the two units the design needs

- **Lens** E · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `plan` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** e-baseline/memory_sum.out.txt: 'chrome 17 processes: summed RSS 3115 MiB, PSS 1599, USS 1369, x1.95'; 'Web Content 3: 223 / 43 / 23, x5.14'; 'all 155 own processes: RSS 11327 MiB, PSS 6659 MiB, x1.70'. a-linux/smaps_cost.out.txt, an independent probe: 'RSS 11.36, PSS 6.80, USS 6.02' GB, and 'the 25 largest hold 87% of the proportional memory'. Code: psutil_provider.py:63 sets memory_bytes to private bytes (Windows) or RSS; rules.py:207 sums it; cli.py:101 prints it in top; models.py:82 has a single memory field. Brief: docs/17:81 and docs/06:83 'Memory for totals: USS or PSS'; docs/08:66-67 uss_top_n = 25 and uss_interval 30s; docs/14:225 states only the per-process unit.
- **Impact.** On Linux, the memory column of submeter top and workload_raw.memory_bytes count shared pages once per process, 1.4x to 5x per workload. The design itself does not say what the total is for members outside the 25 largest processes. Adding a second field later changes every provider, the raw schema and every reader.
- **Proposed change.** core/models.py: split memory into memory_growth_bytes (private bytes, or RSS plus swap) and memory_total_bytes: int | None, with the time it was read. aggregate sums memory_total_bytes and marks the workload partial, with a count of members that lack it. top labels the figure. docs/06:83 and docs/08:67 state what a workload total is when USS or PSS is read only for the uss_top_n largest processes.
- **Cost.** A doc sentence now. The model split touches models, provider, sampler, aggregate, the raw schema, rollup and cli: more than fifty lines with tests.
- **Decision.** When USS or PSS is read only for the 25 largest processes, what is a workload's memory total? (a) The sum over members that have a reading, shown as partial with the count measured. (b) PSS for every readable process at the cold interval on Linux, and USS for the top N on Windows. (c) Private bytes, labelled as such. Recommendation: (a) now, and measure the cost of (b) in phase 0. smaps_cost shows the 25 largest hold 87% of PSS, at about 0.8% of a core every 30 s.
- **Verification.** Re-read psutil_provider.py:63, rules.py:204-211, cli.py:94-103, models.py:71-84, docs/06:82-83, docs/14:225 and docs/17:81. memory_sum.out.txt and smaps_cost.out.txt are two independent probes, and they agree that RSS is about 1.7x PSS for this user. One change: on Windows, private bytes are not double counted, because private pages add up across processes. The wrong number is therefore Linux only. On Windows the figure is a different unit (commit) from the USS the brief names. R02 and R06 chose the units but say nothing about the summed total, so they do not cover this. Moved to plan because the change exceeds fifty lines.
- **Audit.** Severity changed from critical to high. The probes are sound: memory_sum.out.txt gives 'chrome 17 processes ... x1.95' and 'all 155 own processes ... x1.70', and psutil_provider.py:63 with rules.py:207 sums RSS on Linux. But the 1.4-5x figure comes from the baseline `top` summing the growth unit, which docs/14:225 documents as RSS on Linux, the second platform. The brief already specifies a different unit for totals (docs/17:81 'Workload totals use USS (or PSS on Linux)', docs/06:83), so a builder following it does not ship RSS sums. On Windows, private bytes add up across processes without double counting (as the verifier noted). What remains is a real design gap: no rule gives the total for members outside uss_top_n, and the model holds one field for two units. smaps_cost says the 25 largest processes hold 87% of PSS, so the gap mostly hits workloads made of small processes, whose RSS overstates most. That makes job 1's per-workload memory wrong under real conditions on Linux, which is high. No fixed rule is broken, and the brief as written does not produce the multiple-fold error, so it is not critical.
- **Audit.** Correction: The 'wrong number' part is a baseline placeholder that the brief already replaces. The finding's lasting content is the decision about members outside uss_top_n and the two-field model (overlaps B5, models cannot express the docs/06 catalogue).
- **Leads.** E-baseline#2. Earlier findings touched: R02, R06


### R47. An inherited workload is lost when the intermediate parent exits, and the raw process table keeps the first workload

- **Lens** E · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** verify-2/attribution_checks.out.txt: 'while the shell lives: {20: agent, 21: agent, 22: agent}', then 'after the shell exits, reparented to pid 1: {20: agent, 22: unattributed}', and the same with the parent pid left stale. e-baseline/raw_store_cases.out.txt: the process table holds '(sh, ..., unattributed, 9.1)' while the workload_raw totals hold 'builds 9.0'. Code: rules.py:130-163 rebuilds every attribution from the current batch alone; raw.py:132-133 registers a process once per file, and raw.py:158-169 uses INSERT OR IGNORE.
- **Impact.** A dev server or background job that an untagged agent started through a shell moves to Unattributed mid-run when the shell exits. On Linux the process is re-parented to a subreaper; on Windows its parent pid goes stale. Attribution fails for polled agent workloads, and per-process history disagrees with the workload totals.
- **Proposed change.** attribution/rules.py: Attributor keeps a ProcessKey→Attribution map across batches, pruned by batch.exited. An inherited attribution lasts for the life of the process. Rules are retried only when exe or cmdline changes, and an explicit rule match wins. Add a test with this probe's shape. store/raw.py: with sticky attribution the process row stays right; record any change of workload as an event.
- **Cost.** About 20 lines plus a test.
- **Decision.** When a process that inherited a workload execs into a program another rule matches, does it (a) keep the inherited workload, or (b) move to the rule's workload, with the change recorded? Recommendation: (b): an explicit rule wins, and inheritance is otherwise sticky.
- **Verification.** Wrote verify-2/attribution_checks.py and reproduced both re-parenting cases on the unmodified Attributor. Searched docs/ for outlive, reparent, orphan and sticky: nothing. docs/17:77,94 define only the order and the parent-start check. R09 covers pid reuse and R15 covers tagged-group totals; neither covers this. Kernel-tagged runs are not affected, because group membership decides them. Only the polled path fails, so high rather than critical.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. The defect is in the built baseline, not only in a design choice. rules.py:130-163 rebuilds every attribution from the current batch alone, with no state carried between batches. Inheritance needs the parent to be in by_pid (rules.py:137,144). Once the shell exits, the server's parent is missing or stale, so _direct returns None and the server falls to Unattributed (rules.py:155-157). verify-2/attribution_checks.out.txt shows both cases: '{20: agent, 22: unattributed}'. a-linux/cgroup_totals.out.txt section 4 shows the real Linux reparenting: 'its parent is now pid 1: False, named systemd'. An orphan therefore inherits whatever workload systemd --user is given, which can be a wrong workload as well as Unattributed. No document rules on this: docs/17:77,94 and docs/14:259 give only the order and the start-time check, and R09 and R15 cover other cases. 'High' fits the definition exactly: the attribution job fails under real conditions. Detached dev servers, `nohup ... &` and Windows `start` launchers whose parent exits at once are all common. It is not critical, because tagged runs are decided by group membership. raw.py:132-133,158-169 confirm the second half: the process row records the workload it had when first seen.
- **Audit.** Correction: The proposed fix needs one more trigger. Under 'rules are retried only when exe or cmdline changes', a rules.toml reload (an epoch event, docs/17:130) would never reach live processes that inherited their workload. Add 'or when the rules file or inherit_from_parent changes'. Also add the case where an orphan is adopted by a subreaper that matches another rule (systemd --user, per cgroup_totals 4), so the test covers wrong-workload as well as unattributed.
- **Leads.** E-baseline#9. Earlier findings touched: R09, R15


### R48. A job's name disappears when its last handle closes, so a collector that is the only holder cannot re-open it by name after a restart; the brief offers that option, contrary to R15

- **Lens** A, G · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** Re-fetched Job Objects: 'Only named job objects can be opened.' and 'The job is destroyed when its last handle has been closed and all associated processes have been terminated.' Re-fetched Life Cycle of an Object: 'A temporary object can be accessed by name only as long as its handle count is nonzero. Once the handle count decrements to zero, the object's name is removed from the object manager's namespace.' docs/17-build-prompt.md:67: 'a small detached holder, or the collector. Choose one and test it.' R15's fix (16-review-log.md:147): 'held open by a detached helper until the group is empty'. P2 gate 17:270 and 15:17: 'a restarted collector re-attaches to live groups'. AssignProcessToJobObject (re-fetched): 'All processes within a job must run within the same session as the job.'
- **Impact.** If the collector holds the only handle, a collector restart after `submeter run` has returned leaves the job alive with no name. Re-attach fails, that run's totals (including exited children) are lost, and the P2 gate fails. The brief leaves the choice open, although R15 already chose a helper.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:67: replace 'a small detached holder, or the collector. Choose one and test it.' with 'a small detached holder that keeps a handle until ActiveProcesses is 0 and then writes the final totals. Not the collector: on Windows a job can be opened by name only while some handle to it is open, so a restarted collector could not find it. If no handle survives, record the run's totals as lost, never as zero. The launcher, the holder and the collector run in the same logon session.'
- **Cost.** Two or three sentences in one document
- **Verification.** Re-fetched both Microsoft pages; the quotes are present and mean what the lead says. Confirmed that the brief (17:67) differs from R15's fix, so R15 does not resolve it. Found no documented way to get a job handle from a member process; IsProcessInJob only tests membership. Mitigating: a builder who chose the collector would fail the P2 gate, so the cost is rework rather than a silently wrong number. The severity stays high because the brief offers an option that fails a gate. I dropped the lead's Global\ prefix suggestion, because the same-session rule above is enough.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. Confirmed. docs/17:67 still offers 'a small detached holder, or the collector. Choose one and test it.' R15's fix (16-review-log.md:147) chose a detached helper, so R15 does not resolve the choice the brief leaves open. With the collector as the only handle holder, two things fail. First, the 17:66 promise ('accounted for even if the collector was not running'): when the command returns while the collector is down and background processes remain, nothing holds the job. Second, a collector restart after the launcher has returned: the name is gone, so the gate's 're-attaches to live groups' fails. Unlike L2, this failure can pass the gate test. A restart test run while the launcher is still alive succeeds, because the launcher holds a handle. The failure then shows up in real use as lost run totals. High is right: the brief offers an option that fails a stated phase-2 requirement.
- **Audit.** Correction: Two fixes to the proposed text. First, 'The launcher, the holder and the collector run in the same logon session' cites the AssignProcessToJobObject rule, which governs processes inside the job. The holder is not inside the job, so that quote does not support the clause. If the clause stays, rest it on the job name being visible to the collector (namespace scope, unverified here), which W7's InteractiveToken task keeps true. Second, AGENTS.md says each run file has one writer, the launcher. Say that the holder is the launcher's own detached part and writes the final totals by atomic rename only after the launcher's last write. Otherwise the run file has two writers (see also B7).
- **Leads.** A-windows#3. Earlier findings touched: R15


### R49. Nested tagged runs double count: an outer job's kernel totals include every inner job's processes, and the brief has no rule to subtract them

- **Lens** A, B · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** Re-fetched Nested Jobs: 'Resource accounting information for a nested job describes the usage of every process associated with that job, including processes in child jobs.' Re-fetched AssignProcessToJobObject: 'Accounting information is added to the immediate job and aggregated in each parent job in the job chain.' docs/17-build-prompt.md:62 treats group totals as authoritative. :94 says 'Every process maps to exactly one workload, in this order: kernel group membership', but does not say which of two groups wins. :127 says run summaries hold 'exact totals'. In docs/, 'nested' appears only at 03-cli-output.md:53 ('nested jobs available'); there is no subtraction rule and the run file has no parent field.
- **Impact.** Example: `submeter run --as ide -- code`, then `submeter run --as agent:x -- claude` from the IDE's terminal. The ide run's kernel totals include the agent's work, so cost and diff charge it twice. Reconciliation (17:98) catches the excess only when the inner share is above 2% of capacity; smaller inner runs pass silently.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md, Collection: 'If submeter run starts inside a tagged group (the outer launcher sets SUBMETER_RUN_ID in the command's environment, and the inner launcher reads its own environment), the run file records the parent run. A process belongs to its innermost group. A parent's reported totals are its kernel totals minus its tagged children's totals, so each CPU-second belongs to one workload.' P2 gate (17:270, 15:17): a nested tagged run reconciles within 2%, and the parent's run total excludes the child.
- **Cost.** Three sentences and one gate clause
- **Verification.** Re-fetched both pages; the quotes hold. Searched docs/ for nested, inner and parent-group handling and found none. Upgraded from medium to high: following the brief as written gives a double-counted, wrong number whenever runs nest, silently below 2%. Not critical, because nesting needs a deliberate usage the brief does not describe. Linux cgroup v2 is also hierarchical if inner groups are created under outer ones (not checked here).
- **Audit.** Kept at high. The mechanism is documented: nested-job accounting is aggregated in every parent (quotes in the record). The brief expects nesting, at docs/17:62 '(nested jobs)' and docs/03:53 'nested jobs available'. It still has no rule for which group a process belongs to, and no subtraction rule; I grepped docs/ for nested, innermost and parent run. Per docs/17:68, the collector reads group totals each tick, so an outer tagged run reports its inner runs' CPU as well, and cost and diff count it twice. A reasonable builder would not fix this unprompted, because docs/17:62 calls the group totals authoritative. It is plausible for this owner: a shell alias or agent workflow that wraps nested `claude`/build invocations in `submeter run` triggers it. It stays high, not critical, because a deliberate nesting of two Submeter runs is required.
- **Audit.** Correction: The SUBMETER_RUN_ID environment variable can be lost (sudo, env -i, sandboxed agent shells), and then the double count returns silently. Say that the inner launcher confirms membership on Windows with IsProcessInJob against the outer job opened by name, and records 'parent unknown' when the variable is missing. Also align with L2's Linux layout: a nested run made as a sibling under the outer payload is included in payload's cpu.stat. So the subtraction rule must apply on Linux too, or the outer run reads payload/main only.
- **Leads.** A-windows#6. Earlier findings touched: R07, R29


### R50. daemon install's Windows scheduled task is unspecified; with Task Scheduler defaults it will not start on battery, stops on unplug, is killed after 72 h and runs at below-normal priority; whether a standard user can register it is unsettled

- **Lens** A, D, G · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** Re-fetched TaskSettings pages. DisallowStartIfOnBatteries: 'If True, the task will not be started if the computer is running on batteries... The default is True.' StopIfGoingOnBatteries: 'will be stopped if the computer is going onto batteries... The default is True.' ExecutionTimeLimit: 'By default, a task will be stopped 72 hours after it starts to run.' and 'A value of PT0S will enable the task to run indefinitely.' Priority: 'The default value is 7.', and 7 maps to BELOW_NORMAL_PRIORITY_CLASS. Re-fetched schtasks create: 'Only Administrators can schedule tasks, regardless of the value of the /ru parameter.' appears under 'To schedule a task that runs with system permissions'; the Remarks say 'Schtasks always prompts for a password unless you provide one, even when you schedule a task on the local computer using the current user account.' docs/17:199 and 13-build-list.md:48 name the task with no settings; 08-configuration.md:71 on_battery_multiplier shows the collector is meant to run on battery.
- **Impact.** A task created with defaults leaves gaps in the trend data on a work laptop (on battery, after unplugging, after 72 h of uptime). It starves the collector under the CPU load that live diagnosis is for. Soaks on mains power under 72 h pass anyway. Fixed rule 2 is at risk if a standard user cannot register a logon task for themselves, or if registration stores a password (suspected).
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:199: 'On Windows, daemon install registers a task for the current user only: a logon trigger with LogonType InteractiveToken (runs only while the user is signed in, stores no password), DisallowStartIfOnBatteries=false, StopIfGoingOnBatteries=false, ExecutionTimeLimit=PT0S, MultipleInstancesPolicy=IgnoreNew, restart on failure, and Priority 5 (normal). If phase 0 shows a standard user cannot register it, use HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run instead. doctor reads these settings back.' windows-priorities.md item 7: add 'register and delete a logon task as a standard user'.
- **Cost.** One paragraph and one checklist line
- **Decision.** The task's priority: keep the Task Scheduler default of 7 (below normal; the collector starves under load), or set 5 (normal, the same as any program the user starts)? Setting Submeter's own priority does not change an observed process, but rule 1 names 'priority'. Recommendation: 5, with a sentence that rule 1 covers observed processes, not Submeter itself.
- **Verification.** Re-fetched five Microsoft pages; every default quoted is present. Searched docs/ for battery, 72 h and execution-limit handling; only 17:199 and 13:48 mention the task, with no settings. The administrators sentence sits in the System-account section, so the rule 2 part stays suspected (would confirm: as a standard user, Register-ScheduledTask with an AtLogOn trigger for $env:USERNAME and LogonType Interactive, then delete it). I did not re-fetch the performance-counter interactive-logon point; InteractiveToken makes it moot. High rests on the verified defaults.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. docs/17:199, 13:48-49 and 17:256 name a scheduled task with no settings. A task created with Register-ScheduledTask, schtasks or the UI without explicit settings takes the quoted defaults: no start on battery, stop when unplugged, a 72 h limit and priority 7. docs/08:71 (on_battery_multiplier) shows the collector is meant to run on battery. The builder works on a Windows machine that may be on mains power, and the CI soak lasts 30 minutes, so neither would show the gaps. The trends job loses data on a work laptop whenever it is unplugged or has been up for 72 h, and the live-diagnosis job loses its collector exactly when the user is on battery. That is a job failing under real conditions, so high. The rule-2 part (whether a standard user can register a logon task) stays suspected, as the record says. The HKCU Run fallback covers it.
- **Leads.** A-windows#8, D-privacy#13, G-buildability#13. Earlier findings touched: R10, R30


### R51. The built-in serious 'memory nearly full' alert and the Memory commit tile read about 265% on a Linux desktop under default overcommit, so the alert fires after two minutes and never clears

- **Lens** A · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** verify-4 probe now: 'vm.overcommit_memory 0; Committed_AS / CommitLimit 2.65'; memory in use 0.62; 'the built-in rule commit_fraction above 0.90 would be: True' (lead: 2.74 and 2.64). https://docs.kernel.org/filesystems/proc.html (re-fetched), CommitLimit: 'This limit is only adhered to if strict overcommit accounting is enabled (mode 2 in vm.overcommit_memory).' Brief: docs/08-configuration.md:138 'commit_fraction | machine | Memory commit as a share of the commit limit'; :148 'memory nearly full | machine | commit_fraction | above 0.90 | 2m | serious', switched on by default; docs/17-build-prompt.md:83 and :182 list commit with no platform split; docs/02-what-it-looks-like.md:31 'Memory commit' tile; docs/13-build-list.md:16 providers.system collects commit. Only docs/07-analysis.md:97 (verdict) and docs/09:20 split commit by platform.
- **Impact.** On Linux with the default heuristic overcommit, the default serious alert fires two minutes after start and stays on, with a desktop notification every cooldown, and the commit tile shows over 100%. The live-diagnosis job is noisy from the first minute.
- **Proposed change.** docs/08-configuration.md:138: 'commit_fraction: Windows; on Linux only when vm.overcommit_memory = 2, otherwise unavailable'. :148 and docs/17:182: on Linux the built-in rule uses memory pressure (PSI memory full) instead. docs/02-what-it-looks-like.md:31: the Linux tile shows memory in use (1 - MemAvailable/MemTotal).
- **Cost.** Three table cells and one clause.
- **Verification.** Re-read every cited line; docs/07 verdict and docs/09:20 split by platform, but the metric list, built-in rule, tile and build list do not, and the metric is deliverable on Linux (so the 'unavailable' rule would not stop a builder computing it). Re-read /proc/meminfo with my own probe (2.65, mode 0) and re-fetched the kernel doc: the quoted sentence is present and says CommitLimit is not enforced in modes 0 and 1. The exact ratio depends on swap size and workload, so 'on a normal desktop' means 'commonly', not always; severity kept high because a default-on serious alert is permanently wrong whenever it happens.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. I re-ran the check with a read-only audit probe (docs/review/probes/audit/l5_commit_ratio.py, .out.txt). It reports 'vm.overcommit_memory 0', 'commit_fraction = Committed_AS / CommitLimit = 2.63', 'memory in use = 0.54', and 'built-in rule commit_fraction above 0.90 would fire: True'. CommitLimit here is swap plus 50% of RAM (11.5 GiB) against 30.3 GiB committed. docs/08:138,148 and 17:182 define the metric and the default-on serious rule with no platform split. docs/07:97 and 09:20 split only the verdict. The metric can be computed on Linux, so the unavailable rule does not stop a builder from computing it. A serious alert that is always on and says 'memory nearly full' at 54% use fails the live-diagnosis job under ordinary conditions, so high.
- **Audit.** Correction: The proposal changes the built-in Linux rule to PSI memory full, but docs/08:130 and 17:182 refuse any alert metric outside a fixed list, and that list has no pressure metric. As written, the new built-in rule would be refused when the settings are loaded. The fix must also add a machine metric id (for example memory_pressure_full, Linux only) to the allowed list in docs/08 and 17:182 (see B4 on validation).
- **Leads.** A-linux#8. Earlier findings touched: none


### R52. The plateau test clears or flags by noise: the brief's own browser warm-up is falsely flagged at its stated noise, and a leak that grows in 2-hour steps is never flagged with a 2-hour window

- **Lens** B · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `plan` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** analysis/growth.py:45-53 'cut = int(len(xs) * (1 - tail))' ... 'theil_sen(xs[cut:], ys[cut:]) < ratio * overall', with tail 1/3 and ratio 0.25 (the lead cites :157-165, which do not exist in this 110-line file). docs/07:24 'Browser: 3.6 GB plus 0.9 x (1 - e^(-t / 0.35 h)) GB, with noise of up to 0.04 GB either way'; docs/01:18 'and a cache warming up is not'. verify-5/leak_checks.out.txt (sliding 2 h window, a check every 5 min over hours 2-4, 60 seeds): 'noise-free warm-up (a fixture a builder might write): flagged=False'; 'minute buckets noise +/- 5 MB: 0 of 60', '+/-20 MB: 11 of 60', '+/-40 MB: 34 of 60 runs falsely flagged'; '5-minute points as in docs/07 noise +/-40 MB: 51 of 60'; 'staircase +256 MiB every 120 min ... sliding 2 h window: flagged in 0 of 121 checks', 'growing window: flagged in 53 of 121 checks'. growth_acceptance.py rerun identical (section 9: 'noise 40 MB: sliding 34 of 60 runs').
- **Impact.** Job 3's rule that a warm-up is not flagged fails in about half of runs when the noise on each bucket is the brief's own +/-40 MB. The P3 quiet fixture passes if the builder writes the warm-up without noise, so the gate hides the problem. A leak that grows in steps reads as 'levelled off' under a 2-hour window.
- **Proposed change.** Plan a P3 task: replace the ratio test in growth.py plateaued(). The new test should not compare a noisy 40-point tail with a fraction of the whole-window slope; for example, require the tail slope's lower confidence bound to clear min_slope, or require a minimum tail span. Make the detector window span at least two steps of a staircase. docs/17:161 and the docs/15 P3 gate: list as fixtures, all on the minute tier, the docs/07 warm-up with +/-40 MB noise per bucket (quiet) and a staircase leak (trigger).
- **Cost.** A few lines of fixture wording in docs now; the detector change and tuning as one planned task.
- **Verification.** Re-read growth.py:45-84. Wrote leak_checks.py to test the noise assumption. At +/-5 MB per bucket the warm-up is never falsely flagged (0 of 60 runs), so the result depends on how much noise survives into a bucket; per-second noise averaged into minute means would be smaller. docs/07:24 states +/-40 MB on 5-minute points, and at that resolution 51 of 60 runs are falsely flagged, so the brief's own example fails as the brief draws it. The staircase miss depends on the window: a growing window flags it in 53 of 121 checks, and docs/06:64 ('2 to 48 h back') leaves the window open. The garbage-collection sawtooth case is left out because the lead itself calls it arguable. Severity kept at high.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. Re-ran leak_checks.py: identical. My own check makes the warm-up half stronger, because it does not depend on the reviewer's sliding 2-hour window (audit_growth_windows.out.txt). With a window that grows from the start of the series, as docs/06:64 '2 to 48 h back' reads, the docs/07 warm-up is falsely flagged in 18 of 60 runs at +/-40 MB.
