When the warm-up is already 30 minutes old as the series begins (collector or window starting after the browser), it is falsely flagged in 22 of 60 runs at +/-20 MB with a growing window and 15 of 60 with a sliding one. Cause: growth.py:53 compares a noisy 40-point tail with 25% of the whole-window slope, so a flat tail can pass by noise. The R11 fix (docs/16:117-121, 'a leak needs sustained growth with no plateau') therefore fails under realistic per-bucket noise. A noise-free quiet fixture still passes, so the P3 gate hides it, and production raises false leak findings. That is a silent failure of the trends job: high.
- **Audit.** Correction: The staircase sub-claim depends on the 2-hour sliding window: with a growing window it is flagged in 53 of 121 checks. It is the weaker half, and 'make the window span at least two steps' is not a usable rule because step periods have no bound. State instead that the window grows to 48 h. The suggested replacement, a tail lower confidence bound that must clear min_slope, would make true leaks hard to flag: a 75 MiB/h leak passes only 38% of the time at +/-40 MB (audit Part 4), which breaks M3's proposed acceptance. The planned task must tune M4's test and M3's acceptance rate together, for example with a longer minimum tail span or a point estimate against min_slope. Also, the lead's growth.py:157-165 citation is :45-53.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#7. Earlier findings touched: R11


### R53. workload_samples selects samples by their end and roll_up assigns them by their middle, so per-minute roll-ups lose CPU

- **Lens** B, C · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** raw.py:199 'WHERE ts > ? AND ts <= ?'; rollup.py:110 'bucket_start(sample.taken_at - sample.interval_s / 2, width_s)'. docs/17-build-prompt.md:269 tells phase 1 to build minute and history files 'on the existing rollup functions'. verify-6/v6_membership.out.txt: 'interval 1 s, phase 0.2 s: ... 1 per-minute window 3540 (-1.67%); 2 widened window 3600 (+0.00%); 3 whole hour 3600 (+0.00%)'; '5 s, phase 2 s: ... 3300 (-8.33%)'; '2 s, phase 0.5 s: ... 3480 (-3.33%)'; 'sample ending +0.2 s from midnight is in file 2026-11-21; ... roll_up's middle rule in 23:59 (of 2026-11-20)'. The lead's bucket_membership.py re-ran with identical output (upsert variant: '118.0 of 3600.0 (-96.72%)'). The baseline test uses whole seconds (tests/test_baseline.py:426 'DAY0 + second + 1').
- **Impact.** The P1 gate 'CPU-hours from the hourly tier match the raw sum within 0.1%' (docs/15:13) fails with real fractional clocks. If the gate is tested with whole-second fixtures it passes, and production minute and hourly CPU then runs 1.7% to 8% low whenever the tick phase is under half an interval. Under the middle rule a 23:59 bucket also needs the next day's file.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/store/rollup.py:110 and raw.py:199: use one rule. A sample belongs to the bucket [start, start+width) that holds its end: bucket_start(taken_at), and the query becomes 'ts >= ? AND ts < ?'. This is the same half-open split utc_day uses, so no bucket straddles two day files. docs/17-build-prompt.md Store: state the rule. Add a test that sends fractional timestamps through workload_samples and then roll_up.
- **Cost.** About 4 lines in two files, one doc sentence, one test.
- **Verification.** I re-ran the lead's probe and got identical output. I also wrote v6_membership.py independently. The loss appears only when the minute window is queried and rolled up on its own. A window widened by one interval, or one roll_up over the whole span, is exact, so the defect is a trap in composing the two functions rather than an impossibility. The brief directs builders into it (docs/17:269). R24 covers sums and covered seconds, and R13 covers the hourly source. Neither sets a membership rule. I changed the fix: the lead's '(start, start+width]' conflicts with utc_day's [00:00, 24:00) file split, because a sample ending exactly at 00:00:00.0 lands in the next file. I checked and dropped a possible edge effect: under steady load the end and middle sums agree (3600 each). Severity stays high because the gate fails, or the number is wrong, under real clocks.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. Re-ran verify-6/v6_membership.py: identical, -1.67% at 1 s, -8.33% at 5 s and -3.33% at 2 s per minute window when the tick phase is under half an interval. The brief leads straight into the composition that loses the data. docs/17:123 says 'Build hourly buckets from raw data ... A bucket is final 30 seconds after it ends', which invites a read of each finished window, and docs/17:269 says 'built on the existing rollup functions'. The baseline test (tests/test_baseline.py:426, 'DAY0 + second + 1') uses aligned whole-second ticks, where the end and middle rules agree, so the tests cannot see the bug. The inconsistency is in shipped code, so it cannot be rejected as a builder's choice. It is high rather than critical because the composing code is not written yet, and the P1 gate (CPU-hours within 0.1%), run on a real-clock soak, would catch it.
- **Audit.** Correction: Absorb M7 (same defect) and its numbers: -0.139% per hour at 5 s, and the fragment-bucket upsert hazard. S1's fix, the end rule [start, end) with bucket_start(taken_at), is the one to keep because it matches utc_day. H6 (cut the minute tier) would not remove the problem: building hourly buckets from raw still loses one sample per hour.
- **Audit.** Absorbed 'The baseline's raw reader and its rollup put boundary samples in different buckets, so building a bucket from that bucket's read loses one sample and fails the 0.1% hourly gate at the default 5 s idle cadence'. Its evidence: store/raw.py:199 'WHERE ts > ? AND ts <= ?' (by end time); store/rollup.py:110 'bucket_start(sample.taken_at - sample.interval_s / 2, width_s)' (by the middle of the interval). Neither docs/06 nor docs/17 states a convention. docs/17:269 'CPU-hours from the hourly tier match the raw sum within 0.1%'. uv run python docs/review/probes/verify-5/rollup_and_histogram.py (steady 1 core, first tick 0.3 of an interval past the boundary): '3600 5 s 0.3x 2 3595 3600 3600 -0.139%'; '3600 10 s 0.3x ... -0.278%'; '60 1 s 0.3x 2 59 60 60 -1.667%'; '60 5 s 0.3x ... -8.333%'; at 0.7x '+0.000%' with 1 bucket back. b-measurement/rollup_conventions.py rerun identical ('idle 5 s ... worst 0.788%' for a bursty load). Its proposed change: Use one convention in both functions. For example, store/raw.py workload_samples selects 'ts - interval_s/2 >= start AND ts - interval_s/2 < end', which is the rollup's middle-of-interval rule. Add a test that reads one hour, rolls it up, and gets exactly one bucket equal to the whole-day rollup's bucket. docs/06 'What every bucket records': 'a sample belongs to the bucket that holds the middle of its interval'. docs/17:269: the raw sum is the sum of the samples assigned to that hour by the same rule. The lead's proposal to split samples across bucket edges is not needed and would introduce an estimate. Audit reason for merging: M7 states the same defect as S1, from the same two lines: raw.py:199 'ts > ? AND ts <= ?' and rollup.py:110 'bucket_start(taken_at - interval_s/2)'. The mechanism is the same: a sample ending in the first half-interval after a boundary is read with one bucket and rolled into the previous one. Re-ran verify-5/rollup_and_histogram.py: identical. The two fixes conflict, and S1's is the right one. M7 moves raw.py to the middle rule, which puts a sample ending just after 00:00 UTC into the previous day's 23:59 bucket while utc_day (files.py:29-30) stores it in the next day's file. v6_membership.out.txt shows this: 'sample ending +0.2 s from midnight is in file 2026-11-21; ... roll_up's middle rule in 23:59 (of 2026-11-20)'. Building that bucket would then need two files. S1's half-open end rule matches the file split. Correction: Carry M7's evidence into S1. At 5 s idle cadence, reading one hour and rolling it up loses -0.139%, which is over the 0.1% P1 gate even for a steady load. The rollup also returns a one-sample fragment of the previous bucket, and if that is upserted ('recomputing a bucket gives the same row', docs/17:123) it replaces the real bucket.
- **Leads.** C-storage#3; B-measurement#12. Earlier findings touched: R24, R13


### R54. One process with a non-UTF-8 argument or name stops every raw write while it lives

- **Lens** D, C · **Severity** high · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** privacy_recheck.out.txt P3 (a child started with argument b'caf\xe9-FAKE'): 'psutil returns the last argument as 'caf\udce9-FAKE'' / 'scrub bad item is last arg: ticks ['UnicodeEncodeError' x3]; system_raw rows 0' / 'exe_only bad item is last arg: ticks ['ok' x3]; system_raw rows 3' / 'exe_only, bad character in exe: UnicodeEncodeError'. Mechanism: whole tick in one transaction (raw.py:96); command text bound at raw.py:154-155 and sample.exe at raw.py:164; sqlite3 cannot encode a lone surrogate, so system_raw, workload_raw and process rows roll back; the key never enters self._memory (raw.py:132-133), so every later tick retries and fails. No gap row is written. psutil decodes with the filesystem error handler (surrogateescape; psutil/_common.py:141-142).
- **Impact.** Any local process with a Latin-1 argument (scrub mode) or a non-UTF-8 executable name (both modes) blinds the collector with no gap recorded, so attribution, live diagnosis and trends lose the period silently. On Linux other users' processes are read too.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/store/raw.py _register (or once in the provider): pass every text field (command text, exe, workload, event detail) through text.encode('utf-8', 'backslashreplace').decode('utf-8'). Test with a b'caf\xe9' argument and a non-UTF-8 exe. docs/17 Operations: 'A record that cannot be stored is replaced by a marker; it never fails the tick.'
- **Cost.** About 5 lines, a test, one sentence
- **Verification.** Ran my own probe with a real child process (not a hand-made string) to confirm psutil produces the surrogate, then wrote three ticks per mode: scrub fails every tick with zero system rows; exe_only survives a bad argument but not a bad exe name, which widens the lead's claim to both modes. Searched R01-R37: none covers encoding. Windows behaviour with unpaired UTF-16 surrogates not checked. Note: docs/review/probes/verify-7/p3_surrogate.py, an earlier file in this folder that I did not create, fails ruff (SIM105); I left it unchanged because existing files may not be edited.
- **Audit.** Kept at high. Re-ran privacy_recheck.py and got identical P3 lines: 'scrub bad item is last arg: ticks UnicodeEncodeError x3; system_raw rows 0', and with exe_only a bad exe still raises UnicodeEncodeError. The code matches: one transaction per tick (raw.py:96), _register runs before self._memory is set (raw.py:132-133 against :142), so every tick retries and fails, and the gap insert (:97-101) rolls back too. One local process, on Linux any user's (L9), blinds collection for as long as it lives, with no gap recorded. All three jobs lose that period silently, which is high. It is not critical because the lost period has no rows, so coverage drops instead of showing a wrong value.
- **Audit.** Correction: The impact is wider than the record says. My probe docs/review/probes/audit/audit_p3_state_desync.py shows that after the bad process exits, a process first seen during the failed tick has 'process rows 0, process_raw rows 1', and its CPU from that tick is lost ('0.5 s of 1.0 s read'). The reason is that _memory, _pending and _waited (raw.py:134-146) advance before the commit and are not restored on rollback. Any exception inside write(), such as a full disk or a busy database, leaves the in-memory state and the file out of step. Add to the fix: update the in-memory maps only after the transaction commits, or restore them on exception, besides sanitising text with encode('utf-8', 'backslashreplace').
- **Leads.** D-privacy#3


## Medium (68)


### R55. A wall-clock step back reopens a closed day file, packs 90 covered seconds into a minute and delays keyframes; a step forward discards an observed interval

- **Lens** C, E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** sampler.py:55 marks a gap only when wall runs ahead of monotonic. :58 taken_at is the raw wall time. raw.py:265-274 opens any day that differs from the current one. :275-278 makes a keyframe due on the difference of taken_at values. docs/17:112 'A closed day's file is never written again'. verify-1/clock_step.out.txt (sub-millisecond jitter on every tick): 'back-30s: true CPU 120 s; ... stored CPU 120; gap batches 0; batches stamped earlier than the one before 1; fullest minute covered_s 90 of 60'; 'back-50s-midnight: ... files written in order [2026-11-20.db, 2026-11-21.db, 2026-11-20.db, 2026-11-21.db]'; 'forward-5s: true CPU 120 s; sampler credited 119; ... gap batches 1'.
- **Impact.** After a backward step (an NTP step, a manual change, dual boot), timestamps go backwards. One minute bucket claims more coverage than its width, a closed day file is written again, and the next keyframe waits an extra S seconds beyond R31's 10 minutes. Nothing records the step, so no epoch explains the dip. A forward step of more than 2 s turns an observed 1 s interval into a 6 s gap with zero CPU. CPU totals survive a backward step when timestamps carry real sub-second values.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/collector/sampler.py: set taken_at = anchor wall time + monotonic elapsed. When the wall clock departs from that by more than the slack and the suspend test (G9) does not fire, re-anchor and emit a 'clock stepped' event that starts an epoch (R32), not a gap. taken_at never decreases. src/submeter/store/raw.py RawStore.write: never reopen a day earlier than the open file. docs/14:244 and docs/17 Collection: one sentence on clock steps. Tests: a step back and a step forward.
- **Cost.** About 20 lines in sampler.py and raw.py, plus two tests
- **Verification.** I re-read sampler.py:53-59 and raw.py:97-129 and 259-282. The lead's headline, 'silently overwrites stored samples', comes from whole-second synthetic ticks, which collide under INSERT OR REPLACE. C-storage#4 itself found no loss with a 30.25 s step. My probe verify-1/clock_step.py adds sub-millisecond jitter, as time.time() has, and found no rows lost and stored CPU 120 of 120 after a 30 s step back. What remains, all reproduced: non-monotonic timestamps, a 90-of-60 minute, a reopened closed day, and a forward step that drops 1 CPU-s and records a gap. The keyframe delay I verified by reading raw.py:275-278, not by running it. I downgraded severity from high (C) to medium: the totals survive, and the trigger, a clock step, is uncommon. How often Windows steps rather than slews is not verified here.
- **Leads.** C-storage#4, E-baseline#20. Earlier findings touched: R08, R31, R32


### R56. ProcessKey.started is a wall-clock float derived from btime, so a clock step cuts children from their parent's workload and an uncached reader re-keys every process

- **Lens** A, E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** psutil/_pslinux.py:1849-1863 create_time = cached ticks/CLOCK_TICKS + boot_time() ('This is subject to system clock updates'). :1507-1515 boot_time() reads /proc/stat btime on every call. psutil/__init__.py:390-394 makes psutil's own Linux identity create_time(monotonic=True) 'since it is stable over changes to system time'. :776-781 create_time is cached per object and 'may be affected by ... NTP'. psutil_provider.py:57 uses the wall form. rules.py:145-148 rejects a parent that started after the child. sampler.py:84 runs the born-this-interval test on wall time. verify-1/start_time.out.txt: 'same process, two fresh objects: start times differ by 1.000 s'; 'child start minus parent start -3600 s; parent -> 'probe', child -> 'unattributed' (no rule matched)'; 'no cache: ... 456 processes; first_seen 456; reported exited 456; CPU credited 0.000 s, machine busy 0.460 s'.
- **Impact.** After a backward step of S seconds, every child born within S seconds of its cached parent's start loses the include_children rule and stays unattributed for the parent's lifetime, so attribution fails for a freshly started agent. A provider that does not cache start times re-keys every process whenever btime moves by one second: all processes exit and restart, one interval's CPU is lost, and the process table duplicates (R25). The /proc reader of docs/17:71 is such a provider, and so is a switch to the psutil fallback (R17) if the two compute the float differently.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17:77: 'The start time in a process identity is the platform's integer stamp: on Linux, field 22 of /proc/PID/stat (ticks since boot) with /proc/sys/kernel/random/boot_id; on Windows, CreateTime as a FILETIME integer. Convert to wall time only for display. Compare start times, and test whether a process was born in an interval, on the boot clock.' Baseline: models.py ProcessKey.started becomes an int; psutil_provider.py:57 uses the boot-relative value on Linux; Snapshot carries a boot-clock reading for sampler.py:84; raw.py process and process_raw get INTEGER columns. Windows CreateTime is system time at creation, so a backward step there still inverts parent order; record step events (G4) and state this in known limits.
- **Cost.** One sentence in docs/17, and about 30 lines across models.py, psutil_provider.py, sampler.py and raw.py, plus a test
- **Verification.** I re-read psutil 7.2.2 source in .venv and confirmed that btime is re-read on every create_time() of a new object, and that psutil itself avoids the wall form for identity. I wrote verify-1/start_time.py with a simulated step (psutil's boot_time replaced in-process; the real clock was untouched). It reproduced the parent rejection through the real provider, Sampler and Attributor, and the re-keying of all processes with an uncached reader. Refutation: the psutil provider's per-object cache protects processes already seen, so re-keying needs an uncached provider (the planned /proc reader) or a provider switch. Parent rejection affects only children born within S seconds of the parent's start. A float mismatch between a Windows bulk provider and psutil is plausible but not verified here. Severity: E said high, A-linux said medium. I chose medium because steps are uncommon and the effect is bounded.
- **Leads.** A-linux#9, E-baseline#11. Earlier findings touched: R09, R17, R08


### R57. A stand-in zero CPU counter becomes the next delta's baseline, crediting a process's whole lifetime of CPU to one interval

- **Lens** E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** psutil_provider.py:65-66 sets cpu_s 0.0 and restricted True on AccessDenied. :104-110 sets started=0.0 on outer AccessDenied. sampler.py:80-81 subtracts before.cpu_s with no check and no upper bound. verify-1/zero_baseline.out.txt: 'cpu stand-in: interval 1 s, first_seen False, credited 5000 CPU-s on a 4-core machine'; 'start-time stand-in: exited 1, first_seen True, credited 0 CPU-s'. How often it fires, on this machine as an ordinary user: '453 processes: create_time 0, cpu_times 0, memory_info 0, num_threads 0, io_counters 298, cmdline 0'. psutil _pswindows.py:937-946 (cpu_times), :789-803 (memory_info) and :913-921 (create_time) fall back to NtQuerySystemInformation on a permission error.
- **Impact.** When the flip happens, one workload is credited thousands of CPU-seconds in a single 1 s interval, which is impossible on the machine and wrong in every total and percentile. With psutil the stand-in path is close to unreachable: no refusals here, and fallbacks on Windows. It needs hidepid mounts with a process that changes credentials, or a future provider that writes stand-in zeros. The module whose stated job is to stop invented CPU has no guard against it.
- **Proposed change.** core/models.py: ProcessCounters.cpu_s becomes float | None, None meaning unreadable. providers/psutil_provider.py: write None, not 0.0, and never write started=0.0. collector/sampler.py: a None on either side gives no CPU baseline (credit 0, as for an old first sighting). Cap every per-process delta at interval_s x logical_cores and count the capped samples. Do not key this on before.restricted: io_counters refusal alone marks 298 of 453 processes restricted here, so that test would stop crediting CPU to most system processes.
- **Cost.** About 12 lines and two tests
- **Verification.** I reproduced the 5000 CPU-s credit with the real Sampler. To test how often it happens, I counted AccessDenied per psutil call over every process on this machine: zero for every call that feeds the stand-in. I read psutil's Windows fallbacks in .venv. I downgraded severity from high to medium because the code path is certain but no real trigger was shown with the shipped provider. I changed the fix after finding that `restricted` is set by io_counters alone on two-thirds of the processes, which makes the lead's restricted-based test unsafe.
- **Leads.** E-baseline#8. Earlier findings touched: R07, R08


### R58. One process's unexpected read error aborts the whole snapshot, and zombies are dropped although their CPU is readable

- **Lens** E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** psutil_provider.py:76-82 catches only AccessDenied and NotImplementedError on io_counters. :44 has no per-process guard. :102-103 maps ZombieProcess to None. psutil/_pslinux.py:1808-1825 raises RuntimeError for an empty /proc/PID/io and ValueError for a missing field. :1584-1607 wrap_exceptions re-raises FileNotFoundError when stat still exists. :1790 defines io_counters only if /proc/self/io exists. verify-1/read_errors.out.txt: 'RuntimeError: snapshot() raised RuntimeError', the same for ValueError and OSError; real zombie: 'cpu_times: ok', 'cmdline: ZombieProcess', 'in PsutilProvider().snapshot(): False'.
- **Impact.** A single malformed /proc entry costs the whole tick, or crashes the collector if the loop does not catch it, against 'never dropped' (docs/17:78). How often it fires is unknown. A zombie's final CPU, which is readable, is discarded; that hits the short-lived children polling most needs (R01).
- **Proposed change.** providers/psutil_provider.py: each optional read (io_counters, cmdline, cwd) catches psutil.Error, OSError, RuntimeError and ValueError and leaves that field None, marking the process partial. ZombieProcess raised by cmdline or cwd keeps the record with its CPU. Wrap _read so that one process failing yields a restricted record, not an aborted snapshot. Note for the child-time recovery in docs/17:71: a zombie counted here must not be counted again from the parent's cutime after reaping.
- **Cost.** About 10 lines and one test
- **Verification.** I re-read the provider and psutil's Linux paths. My probe verify-1/read_errors.py patched io_counters for one child, and each of the three exceptions escaped snapshot(). It then created a real zombie: cmdline is the call that raises ZombieProcess, while create_time, cpu_times, memory_info, num_threads, ppid and name succeed. io_counters exists only where /proc/PID/io exists, so a kernel without I/O accounting is not a trigger; the trigger frequency stays unknown, hence medium rather than high.
- **Leads.** E-baseline#15. Earlier findings touched: R01


### R59. On Windows the monotonic clock counts sleep, so suspend detection rests only on the 30 s rule, and the documented check for it cannot fail

- **Lens** B · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/sysinfo/acquiring-high-resolution-time-stamps: 'QueryPerformanceCounter reads the performance counter and returns the total number of ticks that have occurred since the Windows operating system was started, including the time when the machine was in a sleep state such as standby, hibernate, or connected standby.' https://docs.python.org/3/whatsnew/3.13.html: 'On Windows, monotonic() now uses the QueryPerformanceCounter() clock ...'. https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/realtimeapiset/nf-realtimeapiset-queryunbiasedinterrupttime: 'The unbiased interrupt-time count does not include time the system spends in sleep or hibernation.' sampler.py:55. docs/reference/windows-priorities.md:69 lists this as unknown and says 'Either answer produces a gap, by a different rule'. :70 check: 'start uv run submeter top --seconds 60, put the computer to sleep for a minute inside that window, and confirm the result reports no burst of CPU time'. verify-1/gap_rule.out.txt part 3: '--seconds 60, no sleep ... CPU-s summed 0.000', and 0.000 again with 40 s asleep under either clock behaviour.
- **Impact.** On Windows with Python 3.13 or later, wall minus monotonic stays near 0 across a suspend. Only the 30 s rule marks it, so a suspend under 30 s is counted as observed time. G1's fix also cannot tell a stall from a suspend using these two clocks. The phase-0 check gives false assurance: `top --seconds 60` is always a gap and always prints zero CPU, whatever the clock does.
- **Proposed change.** docs/reference/windows-priorities.md section 5: replace the 'unknown' with the Microsoft sentence, and replace the check with one that can fail (run a 10 s expected interval across a 60 s sleep and print gap and interval_s). docs/17 'Detect suspend and resume': 'Suspend is the growth of a clock that counts sleep over one that does not: CLOCK_BOOTTIME minus CLOCK_MONOTONIC on Linux, QueryPerformanceCounter minus QueryUnbiasedInterruptTime on Windows. Neither needs administrator rights.' Snapshot carries both readings; the sampler code is part of G1's change.
- **Cost.** Three sentences now. About 10 lines in the provider and sampler with G1, plus a Windows check in P0.
- **Verification.** The lead was suspected because it could not find vendor text. I fetched Microsoft's high-resolution time stamps page and found the sentence that settles it (QPC includes standby, hibernate and connected standby). I also re-fetched the Python 3.13 note that monotonic() uses QPC, and the QueryUnbiasedInterruptTime page as the sleep-excluding counterpart, which runs in user mode and fits fixed rule 2. Running the real `top` through CliRunner showed that the windows-priorities.md check reports zero CPU in every case, so it cannot detect anything. I raised status to verified and severity from low to medium, because the documented check is misleading and the defect constrains G1's fix. The CPU deltas themselves stay exact, so it is not high.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#16. Earlier findings touched: R08


### R60. On Linux, cgroup cpu.stat, readable in every cgroup without root, can name the unit where polling missed CPU; the brief uses cgroups only for tagged runs

- **Lens** A · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `plan` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** verify-2/linux_reads.out.txt: 'running as root: False' / 'cgroups: 235; cpu.stat readable with usage_usec: 235'. a-linux/cgroup_whole_machine.out.txt: 'machine busy, /proc/stat: 12.86 CPU-s' / 'sum of cpu.stat over the top-level cgroups: 12.81' / 'every process polled from /proc at 1 s: 5.18' / 'cpu.stat of the cgroup that held the spawner, whole run: 6.54; its children, from wait4 as they ended: 6.48'. docs/09:9 uses cpu.stat for tagged groups only, and docs/17:71 polls everything else.
- **Impact.** Without it, CPU of orphans and other unseen processes on Linux has nowhere to go (R3) beyond what R1's child-time recovery restores. With it, the residual per cgroup (the cpu.stat increase less the polled deltas inside it) is exact and names the unit: a terminal-tab scope, a browser scope or a service. Orphans stay in their cgroup when re-parented, so this covers what R1 cannot.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17 Reconciliation, alongside R3's term: 'On Linux, read cpu.stat of each leaf cgroup each tick. The increase less the polled deltas of the processes in it is unseen CPU. Show it as "not seen by polling in <unit>" and charge it to the workload that owns all of that cgroup's processes, else leave it in the not-seen term, never in System.' Add this as a P1 task for providers.linux.procfs (build list 05), and measure its per-tick cost in phase 0.
- **Cost.** One paragraph of brief and one provider task: about 235 small reads a tick on this machine, not timed.
- **Decision.** Should version 1 on Linux place not-seen CPU by cgroup, which is more exact and costs one read per cgroup per tick, or only total it as R3's term? Recommendation: by cgroup, because it needs no privilege and covers orphans. Drop it if phase 0 shows the read cost breaks the budget.
- **Verification.** Ran verify-2/linux_reads.py as a non-root user: cpu.stat is readable in all 235 cgroups. Did not re-time the sum comparison and relied on the lens's run. Downgraded from high to medium: this is a remedy for the gaps in R3 and R1, not a separate failure, and docs/10:10 already accepts the polled undercount. One correction: a scope can hold several workloads (a terminal tab holds both the agent and its shell), so the residual is per unit, not per workload. The proposal says so.
- **Leads.** A-linux#6. Earlier findings touched: R07, R01


### R61. aggregate() labels a polled sum 'kernel' for any workload in kernel_workloads, and the test asserts that label

- **Lens** E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** verify-2/attribution_checks.out.txt: 'source=kernel cpu_s=0.40; aggregate() has these parameters: (batch, assignment, kernel_workloads)'. rules.py:196-216: kernel_workloads changes only source(). tests/test_baseline.py:268-272 asserts source 'kernel' on a polled sum. docs/14's attribution entry: 'aggregate marks each workload as measured by the kernel, by polling, or partially'. cli.py:94 calls aggregate(batch, assignment) without kernel_workloads.
- **Impact.** No current caller passes kernel_workloads, so nothing is mislabelled today. When phase 2 wires the launcher, the obvious call shows the polled undercount (R01's problem) under the 'kernel' label, which the brief uses to mark an exact figure.
- **Proposed change.** rules.py aggregate: replace kernel_workloads with a mapping from workload to the group's own totals (CPU, I/O, processes). Use those totals for KERNEL workloads and keep the polled sum only as a cross-check. tests/test_baseline.py:268-272: assert the group's figure. A smaller alternative: docs/14 says the parameter is a label only and that phase 2 must replace it.
- **Cost.** About 15 lines and one test change.
- **Verification.** Ran the probe and grepped for callers: only the test passes kernel_workloads. Downgraded from high to medium: this is a latent trap in an API that will mislead once it is wired, not a wrong number today.
- **Leads.** E-baseline#10. Earlier findings touched: R01, R15


### R62. include_children is used by the sample rules but defined nowhere, and the code ORs it with inherit_from_parent, so a rule cannot turn inheritance off

- **Lens** F · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/08-configuration.md:232,238,244 set include_children = true, and grep finds no document that defines it. rules.py:62 defaults it to False; rules.py:181 has 'inheritable=self._inherit or rule.include_children'. verify-2/attribution_checks.out.txt: 'include_children=False, inherit_from_parent=True: child cc -> builds'; 'include_children=True, inherit_from_parent=False: child cc -> builds'. docs/08:101: 'inherit_from_parent | true | Turn off to see helper processes on their own'.
- **Impact.** A rule cannot decline children while the global default is on. Turning the global setting off, as docs/08:101 suggests, still leaves browser, IDE and agent children inside those workloads. CPU and memory move between workloads in ways the documented settings do not predict.
- **Proposed change.** docs/08, under 'Sample rules.toml', and the attribution entry in docs/14: 'include_children decides whether processes started by a matched process inherit its workload; a rule that omits it follows attribution.inherit_from_parent.' rules.py: include_children: bool | None = None, and inheritable = rule.include_children when it is set, otherwise self._inherit. Add one test.
- **Cost.** Two sentences, about five lines of code and one test.
- **Decision.** Should a rule's include_children override the global setting in both directions (recommended), or should include_children be removed, leaving only inherit_from_parent?
- **Verification.** Ran the probe on the unmodified Attributor. Grepped docs/ for include_children and 'with children': they appear only in the sample and in the docs/02:106,116 footers. R01-R37 do not cover it.
- **Leads.** F-consistency#7. Earlier findings touched: none


### R63. On Linux the baseline stores psutil's name() as exe, which differs from the executable's file name for about one in six readable processes

- **Lens** A · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** verify-2/linux_reads.out.txt: 'exe readable: 154; name() equals the executable's file name: 130; differs: 24'. Examples: 'apport-gtk' vs 'python3.12', 'WebExtensions' vs 'thunderbird-bin', 'pipewire-pulse' vs 'pipewire'. a-linux/process_names.out.txt: 109 of 452 processes have a 15-character comm, and psutil repairs 61 of them from the command line. Code: psutil_provider.py:92 sets exe=process.name(); rules.py:78 matches the lowercased base name exactly. docs/08-configuration.md:217 calls the field the executable, and docs/14 says 'Program names match on file name'. On Windows, name() is the base name of exe() (.venv _pswindows.py:730-738), so Windows is unaffected.
- **Impact.** On Linux, rules written by executable name miss processes a program has retitled, such as browser content processes, and scripts run by an interpreter. The hourly top executables (docs/17:128) are keyed by names the program can change. Attribution falls to Unattributed or to inheritance. The shipped examples/rules.toml matches only 'claude' on Linux mainly because its names are Windows .exe names, not because of name().
- **Proposed change.** docs/08, rules section: 'exe matches the executable's file name (on Linux from /proc/PID/exe where readable, else the first word of the command line); name matches the process name. Both accept glob patterns such as python3*.' Provider: store exe from the base name of psutil exe(), with name() as the fallback, and store name separately. examples/rules.toml: a comment that the names are Windows names, with their Linux equivalents.
- **Cost.** A paragraph in docs/08, one model field, and two lines each in rules.py and the provider.
- **Verification.** Wrote verify-2/linux_reads.py, which compares name() with readlink(/proc/PID/exe) for every process whose exe is readable: 24 of 154 differ. Read psutil's Windows name() to confirm Windows is unaffected. Changed the headline: the sample rules miss on Linux chiefly because they use Windows names; the name()/exe mismatch is the real defect. R09 concerns environment and parent rules, not this.
- **Leads.** A-linux#12. Earlier findings touched: R09


### R64. On Linux the Restricted bucket holds two thirds of processes, every kernel thread among them, because restricted is set when only I/O is unreadable

- **Lens** A · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** verify-2/linux_reads.out.txt: '451 processes, 297 restricted; all kernel threads: 160' / 'of the restricted: user process 137, kernel thread 160, I/O missing 297, command line missing 0, CPU above zero 185'. a-linux/restricted_split.out.txt: other users' processes hold 42.2% and kernel threads 3.5% of CPU time since start. Code: psutil_provider.py:79-80 sets restricted on an I/O AccessDenied; rules.py:156. Brief: docs/17:97 'System (kernel, interrupts)'. docs/10:16 says restricted processes' 'Command lines and USS are not readable', but on Linux command lines are readable (proc_readability: 134 of 134). docs/03:63 shows 'Restricted 14 processes'.
- **Impact.** On Linux, Restricted reads as 'unreadable' although CPU and command line are known. It holds about 46% of CPU time since boot on this machine, and kernel threads that docs/17:97 places in System are not there. The doctor line and the 'how much is restricted' check in windows-priorities.md:51 give a misleading picture of attribution. CPU totals are correct.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17:97: 'On Linux, kernel threads (PF_KTHREAD in the flags field of /proc/PID/stat) count as System.' docs/10:16: on Linux, restricted processes lack I/O and USS, and their command lines are readable. Provider: set restricted only when CPU or memory is unreadable, and report missing fields per field (R8).
- **Cost.** Two sentences now. The PF_KTHREAD check, about 5 lines, goes in the P1 procfs reader.
- **Verification.** Recounted with my own probe: 297 restricted, including all 160 kernel threads, all for I/O alone. Raised from low to medium: the label misleads the doctor output and the dashboard on Linux, which is what medium means here. The CPU numbers are unaffected, so not higher. This overlaps R8, where one restricted flag stands for 'I/O unreadable'. This finding keeps the kernel-thread/System part and the Linux meaning of Restricted.
- **Leads.** A-linux#11. Earlier findings touched: R07


### R65. docs/03's cost sample labels System 'kernel' and Restricted 'polled', against docs/02 and the code; Restricted memory is 0.3 GB in one and n/a in the other

- **Lens** F · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/03-cli-output.md:18 'system kernel 0.24 n/a n/a n/a'; :19 'restricted polled 0.05 n/a n/a 14'. docs/02-what-it-looks-like.md:44 '| System | counters |'; :45 '| Restricted | partial | 0.16 | 0.3 GB | 14 |'. models.py:28-34 define KERNEL, POLLED, COUNTERS and PARTIAL, and rules.py:216 labels Restricted PARTIAL. docs/03:61 prints status 'opaque', which Availability (models.py:22-25) does not include (interface_cases: refused). docs/README.md:70-76 ranks the topic files together, so 02 does not override 03.
- **Impact.** The accounting source is the brief's mark of how far a number can be trusted, and 'kernel' on System claims exact group accounting that no kernel group provides. A builder copying docs/03 prints the wrong labels. The memory rule for Restricted is left open: 02 sums a working set into the USS total, and 03 hides it.
- **Proposed change.** docs/03-cli-output.md:18-19: 'system counters' and 'restricted partial'. docs/06 metric catalogue: one rule for Restricted memory, for example 'workload totals are USS or PSS; where neither can be read the total is unavailable and working set is shown separately'. Align 02:45 and 03:19 to it. docs/03:61: show 'partial' with the note, or add 'opaque' to Availability in docs/14 and models.py.
- **Cost.** Three lines in docs/03 and one sentence in docs/06.
- **Verification.** Re-read every cited line and checked the precedence in docs/README: no rule settles 02 against 03. Searched for any statement that cost uses different labels: none. R01-R37 do not cover it.
- **Leads.** F-consistency#3. Earlier findings touched: R07


### R66. docs/10 and docs/17 state the polled undercount of short-lived children as Windows-only, while Linux recovery is partial

- **Lens** F · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/10-known-limits.md:10: 'Their CPU time is undercounted on Windows, by about 70%'. docs/17-build-prompt.md:221: 'Polled workloads on Windows undercount short-lived children.' docs/05-data-flow.md:65: 'On Linux part of the gap can be recovered'. docs/03:22 names no platform. verify-2/reap_counters.out.txt: 'grandchild last seen with 0.15 s own CPU; this process's child-time rose by 0.07 s (the launcher only)'. a-linux/child_times.out.txt, with SIGCHLD ignored: 'parent's counter shows 0.00 s'.
- **Impact.** On Linux the dashboard and doctor would show no undercount note for polled workloads. Yet orphans, children of parents that ignore SIGCHLD, and children not yet reaped are still missed, and the baseline recovers nothing yet.
- **Proposed change.** docs/10-known-limits.md:10 and docs/17-build-prompt.md:221: 'undercounted on Windows, and on Linux for children that are orphaned, not yet reaped, or whose parent ignores SIGCHLD'. The rule in docs/17:71 itself is fixed under R1.
- **Cost.** Two sentences.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/05:65, 10:10, 17:71, 17:221 and 03:22. Dropped the 'optional in 05, required in 17' part: 05:65 says what is possible and 17:71 instructs doing it, which is not a contradiction under docs/README's precedence. The missing subtraction rule is R1's finding and is merged there. My orphan case confirms the remaining Linux undercount.
- **Leads.** F-consistency#8. Earlier findings touched: R01


### R67. On Windows a tagged group has no current memory figure from the kernel, so the brief's claim that the kernel provides 'CPU time, memory, I/O' totals is false for memory

- **Lens** A, B · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:62 says 'The kernel's totals for the group are authoritative: CPU time, memory, I/O and process counts'. 05-data-flow.md:52 says 'the kernel totals CPU, memory and I/O'. 09-telemetry.md:9 marks tagged totals as 'Full'. Re-fetched Microsoft Learn: JOBOBJECT_BASIC_ACCOUNTING_INFORMATION has TotalUserTime, TotalKernelTime, ThisPeriod*, TotalPageFaultCount, TotalProcesses, ActiveProcesses and TotalTerminatedProcesses, and no memory member. JOBOBJECT_EXTENDED_LIMIT_INFORMATION has only peaks: 'PeakProcessMemoryUsed: The peak memory used by any process ever associated with the job.' and 'PeakJobMemoryUsed: The peak memory usage of all processes currently associated with the job.' Its limits are commit ('virtual memory that can be committed'). JOBOBJECT_LIMIT_VIOLATION_INFORMATION.JobMemory is filled only 'If the ViolationLimitFlags member specifies JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_JOB_MEMORY ... at the time the notification was sent', which needs a limit, and 17:69 forbids limits. None of the other 15 classes in the QueryInformationJobObject table returns memory. The brief contradicts itself: 17:81 says 'Workload totals use USS', and 10-known-limits.md:18 says workload memory 'can lag by up to the USS interval'. 17:66 ('accounted for even if the collector was not running') and 17:127 ('peak and mean memory') cannot both hold for memory.
- **Impact.** The brief tells the builder to read tagged-workload memory from a Windows source that does not exist. Taking PeakJobMemoryUsed would show peak commit as current memory, in a different unit from the USS used for polled workloads. Otherwise the brief gives tagged memory no stated source. A run file the launcher writes while the collector is down cannot contain mean memory.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:62: 'On Windows the job provides CPU time, I/O and process counts, including exited processes, plus peak commit (PeakJobMemoryUsed), and no current memory. Current memory for a tagged group is the sum of USS over its members (JobObjectBasicProcessIdList), shown with the age of the reading like any other workload.' 05-data-flow.md:52: 'the kernel totals CPU and I/O'. 09-telemetry.md row 'Tagged workload totals': 'CPU, I/O, process counts: full. Memory: peak commit only; current memory from per-process reads.' 17:127: mean memory comes from the collector's samples and is unavailable for a run the collector never saw; a peak taken from the job is labelled peak commit.
- **Cost.** About five sentences across three documents
- **Decision.** Where should tagged-run I/O come from on Linux? (a) After waiting for the command, the launcher reads its own /proc/self/io: reaped children's I/O folds into the reaper (a-linux/io_children.out.txt 2: '+32.0 MB' through an intermediate shell). Gaps: processes still running when the command returns, and double-forked orphans, which the user's systemd adopts (cgroup_totals 4: parent 'named systemd'), so the value must be labelled partial. (b) As (a), with the holder made a child subreaper (PR_SET_CHILD_SUBREAPER) so orphans come back to it: complete, but it changes which process adopts the tree's orphans, which risks fixed rule 1; not probed. (c) Show tagged I/O on Linux as unavailable. Recommendation: (a) labelled partial, with doctor stating the gap; (c) if a partial label is judged too close to an estimate.
- **Verification.** Re-fetched four Microsoft pages: QueryInformationJobObject, BASIC_ACCOUNTING, EXTENDED_LIMIT and LIMIT_VIOLATION. Their quotes hold, and no documented class returns current job memory. Searched docs/ and found 17:81 and 10:18, which already point to USS, so the brief contradicts itself rather than having no answer. R01, R06 and R15 do not cover this. Downgraded from critical to high. A wrong number appears only if the builder labels peak commit as current memory, and priority 1, 17:81 and AGENTS.md ('say so and ask') steer against that. What is certain is a false statement in the spec and a run-file promise that cannot be met. The Linux cgroup memory.current is a different unit from PSS; that belongs to the Linux batch.
- **Audit.** Severity changed from high to medium. The false statement is real. docs/17:62 says the kernel totals are authoritative for memory, and docs/05:52 and 09:9 ('Full') say the same. The Microsoft quotes in the record list no current-memory member in any documented class, and my search found no documented class either. However, the brief already gives the answer that works: docs/17:81 says 'Workload totals use USS', and docs/10:18 says workload memory 'can lag by up to the USS interval'. A builder who looks for job memory finds none, and docs/17:277 plus AGENTS.md ('Say so and ask') send them to USS. No gate depends on memory: the P2 gate at 17:270 and 15:17 is about CPU charge, survival, re-attach and return. No job fails either, because tagged memory is still delivered from USS. The wrong number appears only if a builder labels PeakJobMemoryUsed (peak commit) as current memory, which the verifier itself calls steered against. The 17:127 'mean memory' promise for a run the collector never saw is a document promise that cannot be met. Under the strict definitions that is misleading or costly later, so medium.
- **Audit.** Correction: W1's text for docs/05:52 ('the kernel totals CPU and I/O') directly conflicts with L3's ('CPU and memory, and on Windows I/O and processes started'). L4 (memory.current includes file cache) rewrites the same sentence again. Applied one commit per finding, the second edit undoes the first. Write one rewrite per platform for 17:62, 05:52 and the 09:9 row. CPU: kernel, both systems, including exited processes. I/O and processes started: kernel on Windows (BASIC_AND_IO_ACCOUNTING, TotalProcesses); unavailable from the kernel on Linux, with L3's decision. Memory: current memory is the sum of USS/PSS over live members on both systems. The kernel peak is kept only under its own label: peak commit on Windows, charged memory including file cache on Linux (L4).
- **Audit.** Absorbed 'On Linux a tagged group has no I/O totals and no count of processes started, though docs/17, docs/05 and docs/09 present both as authoritative and Full'. Its evidence: verify-4 probe: root passes down 'cpuset cpu io memory ...', user.slice passes down 'cpu memory pids' (no io from that level on); app.slice io.* files: ['io.pressure'] only; 'files that could count processes started: none'; pids files: pids.current, pids.events, pids.events.local, pids.max, pids.peak. /usr/lib/systemd/system/user@.service:24 'Delegate=pids memory cpu'. a-linux/cgroup_whole_machine.out.txt: delegated to this user 74 cgroups, 'io.stat present in 0'. cgroup_totals 3: '8 extra threads: pids.current 9, lines in cgroup.procs 1'. Kernel doc (re-fetched): 'Note that PIDs used in this controller refer to TIDs, process IDs as used by the kernel.' Windows has the counter: learn.microsoft.com JOBOBJECT_BASIC_ACCOUNTING_INFORMATION (re-fetched) TotalProcesses 'The total number of processes associated with the job during its lifetime, including those that have terminated.' Claims: docs/17-build-prompt.md:62 'authoritative: CPU time, memory, I/O and process counts'; docs/05-data-flow.md:52 'the kernel totals CPU, memory and I/O'; docs/09-telemetry.md:9 'Without admin: Full'; docs/17:127 run totals include 'I/O, processes started'. Its proposed change: docs/09-telemetry.md row 1, Linux: 'cpu.stat, memory.* and pids.* (pids count threads); no io.stat and no started-process count without root'; Without admin: 'Partial on Linux'. docs/17-build-prompt.md:62 add: 'On Linux the kernel gives CPU time and memory only. Processes started is unavailable for a tagged run (never pids.peak); I/O comes from the source chosen below, labelled with its gap, or is shown as unavailable.' docs/05-data-flow.md:52: 'CPU and memory, and on Windows I/O and processes started'. Audit reason for merging: The substance holds. user@.service delegates only 'pids memory cpu' (a-linux/cgroup_scope.out.txt section 1), and app.slice has only io.pressure (verify-4 readonly output), so there is no io.stat without root. pids.current counted 9 for one process with 8 threads (cgroup_totals 3), and no file counts processes started. docs/09:9's 'Full' and docs/17:62's unhedged claim are therefore false on Linux. The severity is medium for the same reason as W1. Phase 0's capability matrix (17:265) is where this is found. AGENTS.md makes the missing figures 'unavailable', not zero. No gate depends on tagged I/O or process counts. Linux is the second target. The polled-sum-labelled-kernel risk is R10's, already filed. The merge is needed because L3 and W1 (and L4) rewrite the same three sentences (17:62, 05:52, 09:9 row 1) with conflicting text. W1 would make 05:52 'CPU and I/O' and L3 would make it 'CPU and memory, and on Windows I/O and processes started'. One per-platform rewrite, which carries L3's decision on where Linux I/O comes from, avoids one commit undoing another. Correction: In the merged text, Linux tagged memory must not be simply 'the kernel gives memory'. memory.current includes file cache and kernel memory (L4). The current figure should be PSS/USS as for every workload, with the kernel peak labelled as charged memory.
- **Leads.** A-windows#1; A-linux#3. Earlier findings touched: R01, R06, R15


### R68. With breakaway forbidden, a program that runs directly in a tagged job and asks for CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB, or that puts a child in a job with UI limits, fails under submeter run; allowing breakaway may lose Node agents' grandchildren

- **Lens** A, D · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:69: 'Do not allow breakaway'. 17:23: 'Placing a process tree ... into a Job Object ... for accounting is allowed. Setting any limit on that group is not.' Re-fetched Process Creation Flags, CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB: 'If the calling process is associated with a job, the job must set the JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_BREAKAWAY_OK limit.' Re-fetched libuv src/win/process.c: 'CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB makes the CreateProcess call fail if we're under job control that doesn't allow breakaway.' libuv's own job sets LimitFlags = BREAKAWAY_OK | SILENT_BREAKAWAY_OK | DIE_ON_UNHANDLED_EXCEPTION | KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE. Re-fetched AssignProcessToJobObject: the target job 'cannot have UI limits set', and 'The child process breaks away from every job in the job chain unless a job in the chain does not allow breakaway. In this case, the child process does not break away from that job or any job above it'. Re-fetched Nested Jobs: a breakaway moves 'up the hierarchy until it reaches a job that does not permit breakaway.'
- **Impact.** A program that requests breakaway from inside the tagged job, or that nests its own UI-limited job, behaves differently only when launched through Submeter. How many programs do this is undocumented. The text of fixed rule 1 is not broken: it lists priority, affinity, suspend, kill and memory cap; 17:23 allows job placement; and forbidding breakaway is the job's default, not a limit. Counter-risk to the leads' fix: Node (libuv) puts each child into its own job with SILENT_BREAKAWAY_OK, so grandchildren such as bash, git and rg break away and move up the chain. Today Submeter's job stops them. If that job set BREAKAWAY_OK, it is undocumented whether those silent breakaways also leave it. If they do, Node-based agents, the main use case, lose their children from exact accounting. Setting BREAKAWAY_OK is also a LimitFlags value, which 17:23 forbids as written.
- **Proposed change.** Owner ruling first. Recommended: keep 'Do not allow breakaway'. Add a row to docs/10-known-limits.md: 'A program that asks to break away from a tagged group, or that places children in its own job with UI limits, cannot start those children under submeter run.' Add a Windows test to P0 (windows-priorities.md item 7) and to the P2 gate: (a) a direct child calls CreateProcess with CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB, record the error; (b) node, then bash, then a grandchild, and the grandchild stays in the group; (c) a Chromium-based browser started in a tagged run opens pages. Consider BREAKAWAY_OK only if (b) still holds with it set.
- **Cost.** One table row and one gate clause, after the ruling
- **Decision.** Tagged Windows groups: (1) keep breakaway forbidden, so exact totals include all of Node's grandchildren, but a program that requests breakaway fails under submeter run; or (2) set JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_BREAKAWAY_OK (never SILENT), so those programs behave as they would without Submeter, but silent breakaways from libuv's job might then escape the tagged group (undocumented), and 17:23 'no limits' would need an exception. Recommendation: (1), with the known-limits row, plus the Windows test in P0 that decides whether (2) is safe.
- **Verification.** Re-fetched four Microsoft pages and the libuv source. The quotes are present. The nested-chain rule in AssignProcessToJobObject narrows the failure to processes whose immediate job is Submeter's. It also brought to light libuv's SILENT_BREAKAWAY_OK job, which neither lead mentions and which makes their recommended fix risky. Downgraded from high to medium: no fixed rule is broken by its text, node/libuv itself never requests breakaway, and which programs are affected is suspected (would confirm: test (c) above). Chromium is the likeliest case, because of its UI-limited sandbox jobs.
- **Leads.** A-windows#4, D-privacy#14. Earlier findings touched: R01, R15


### R69. The brief does not say how the command enters the job; with Popen followed by AssignProcessToJobObject, early children escape the group, and the CREATE_SUSPENDED workaround invites a dispute under rule 1

- **Lens** A, G · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** Re-fetched Job Objects: 'After a process is associated with a job, by default any child processes it creates using CreateProcess are also associated with the job.' CPython 3.14.5 subprocess.py:1553 has `hp, ht, pid, tid = _winapi.CreateProcess(...)`, and :1574-1576 keeps _handle and pid, calls `_winapi.CloseHandle(ht)`, and discards tid. In subprocess.py:206 and :209-211, STARTUPINFO.lpAttributeList carries only handle_list. Re-fetched UpdateProcThreadAttribute: 'PROC_THREAD_ATTRIBUTE_JOB_LIST: The lpValue parameter is a pointer to a list of job handles to be assigned to the child process ... Supported in Windows 10 and newer'. AssignProcessToJobObject remarks point to CREATE_SUSPENDED. docs/17:62 and 13-build-list.md:23 say what the launcher does, not how. AGENTS.md:9 lists 'suspend' under rule 1.
- **Impact.** A command that spawns at once (a shell running git or rg) can start children before the assignment. Their CPU is missing from the 'exact' total in the spawn-heavy case R01 exists for. The P2 gate compares against the same kernel total, so it cannot notice. Creating the command suspended and resuming it needs ctypes anyway, and reviewers may read it as a breach of rule 1.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md after :62: 'On Windows the launcher creates the command inside the job with PROC_THREAD_ATTRIBUTE_JOB_LIST through CreateProcessW (ctypes), so no descendant is born outside the group and nothing is created suspended. If the launcher instead joins the job itself before starting the command, it must start the detached holder first, because a process in a job that forbids breakaway can never leave it.' Add the same rule for the cgroup path in docs/13 component 13 (Linux detail belongs to the Linux batch).
- **Cost.** Two sentences
- **Verification.** Read the subprocess.py lines on this machine's 3.14.5 standard library and re-fetched both Microsoft pages; the claims hold. Searched docs 05, 09, 13, 14 and 17 for a launch sequence and found none. The width of the race is unmeasured (no Windows), so it stays medium. New detail: G's 'launcher joins the job itself' alternative conflicts with the holder. A holder started after the join sits inside the job, which forbids breakaway, so ActiveProcesses never reaches 0 unless the holder counts itself. The proposed text says how to avoid that.
- **Leads.** A-windows#5, G-buildability#6. Earlier findings touched: R01, R15


### R70. The psutil fallback is not independent of the bulk snapshot: CPU times, start time and I/O sit in Reserved members, psutil reaches the same call for thread counts and denied processes, and it makes one whole-system query per process per snapshot

- **Lens** A, B · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** Re-fetched NtQuerySystemInformation: '[NtQuerySystemInformation may be altered or unavailable in future versions of Windows...]'. The SYSTEM_PROCESS_INFORMATION layout shows 'BYTE Reserved1[48];' after NumberOfThreads and 'LARGE_INTEGER Reserved7[6];' at the end, and 'The other members of the structure are reserved for internal use by the operating system.' Probe docs/review/probes/verify-3/w6_thread_queries.out.txt (psutil 7.2.2): '_proc_info calls: [cext.proc_info]'; 'num_threads reaches _proc_info: True (on the only path)'; create_time, cpu_times and io_counters reach it in except fallbacks; oneshot_enter caches per Process; 'snapshot 1: 466 processes returned ... num_threads called 467 times'. psutil_provider.py:64 calls num_threads for every process. docs/17:71 and R17 (16-review-log.md:155-159) present psutil as the fallback.
- **Impact.** If the structure changes, the fallback is hit as well: thread counts and every process the user cannot open go through the same call. On Windows the fallback makes about N whole-system queries of N entries each per snapshot, so it is the costliest path exactly when it is in use. Today PsutilProvider is the baseline's only provider, so `submeter top` on Windows already runs this path.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:71, plus a note under R17 in docs/16-review-log.md: 'CPU times, start time and I/O are members Microsoft documents only as Reserved, and psutil's Windows path uses the same call for thread counts and for processes it cannot open, so the fallback reduces the dependency but does not remove it. The CI parse test is the guard.' src/submeter/providers/psutil_provider.py:64: on Windows do not call num_threads, and record threads as unavailable (ProcessCounters.threads becomes int | None).
- **Cost.** Two doc sentences; about 5-8 lines in the provider and core/models.py
- **Verification.** Re-fetched the Microsoft page (the Reserved layout holds). Wrote and ran my own AST and runtime probe; it confirms num_threads goes to _proc_info and cext.proc_info, with the cache per Process only, one call per process per snapshot. Downgraded from high to medium. The parse risk itself is R17's and already has a CI guard. windows-priorities.md item 3 already warns that psutil will miss the 10 ms target, so P0 will not misread the cost. The quadratic cost applies only while the fallback runs and its size is unmeasured (no Windows). No gate fails as such.
- **Leads.** A-windows#7. Earlier findings touched: R02, R17


### R71. One-instance lock on Windows: the OS may release a dead holder's lock late and acquire() tries once; platform_check waits a fixed 0.5 s; a test reads the locked byte through a second handle; holder() reports a dead pid

- **Lens** A, E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** Re-fetched LockFile: 'If a process terminates with a portion of a file locked ... the locks are unlocked by the operating system. However, the time it takes for the operating system to unlock these locks depends upon available system resources.' and 'If the locking process opens the file a second time, it cannot access the specified region through this second handle until it unlocks the region.' files.py:219-231: one _try_lock call, then return False. Probe verify-3/w8_lock_attempts.out.txt: 'acquire() returned False after 1 attempt(s)'; 'holder() returns that pid: True; a process with that pid exists now: False'. Re-ran e-baseline/lock_cases.py with the same result. platform_check.py:53-55: kill, wait, sleep(0.5), then one retry. tests/test_baseline.py:329 and platform_check.py:52 call read_bytes() on the lock file from the locking process while byte 0 is locked.
- **Impact.** A collector restarted right after a crash or a Task Manager kill can exit with 'another copy is already running', leaving no collector. platform_check can print a false FAIL. If Windows refuses Python's read at offset 0 (readall asks for size+1 bytes, which overlaps the locked byte), test_file_map_names_expiry_and_lock fails on Windows and check_lock crashes, blocking the P0 gate (suspected). holder() names a dead pid after a crash; nothing outside the tests calls it yet.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/store/files.py InstanceLock.acquire(): when refused, retry every 100 ms for up to 5 s. holder(): return None when a non-blocking try-lock succeeds. tests/test_baseline.py:329 and scripts/platform_check.py:52: check emptiness with stat().st_size == 0 instead of read_bytes(). platform_check.py:55: poll for up to 5 s instead of one 0.5 s sleep.
- **Cost.** About 15 lines across three files
- **Verification.** Re-fetched LockFile; both quotes hold. Wrote a probe counting _try_lock calls and a stale-pid case, and re-ran the E probe. The no-retry behaviour and the stale pid are verified on Linux from the code. The locked-read part stays suspected: the documentation does not say whether a read at EOF counts as accessing the locked region. windows-priorities.md:37 already knows another program cannot read a locked byte, but the test reads through a second handle in the same process. Kept medium (misleading check results, a refused restart), not high, because the gate failure is unconfirmed.
- **Audit.** Overlap: holder() returning a dead holder's pid is fixed under P13; this finding keeps the Windows retry and test items.
- **Leads.** A-windows#11, E-baseline#24. Earlier findings touched: R30


### R72. The brief never says where a Linux tagged cgroup lives; the only unprivileged, systemd-sanctioned place is a delegated transient user scope with holder and payload leaf groups, and R15 misstates cgroup removal

- **Lens** A · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** verify-4/linux_platform_readonly.out.txt: user.slice and user-N.slice owner uid 0, cgroup.procs uid 0; user@N.service owned by this user; this process's own group passes down 'nothing'. a-linux/cgroup_totals.out.txt A: group made below the shell's own cgroup 'cpu.stat yes, memory.current NO, memory.peak NO, pids.current NO'; 2: mkdir group 'directory still there: True; populated 0'. a-linux/cgroup_scope.out.txt 2: Delegate scope 'passing memory down while this process is in it: refused (Device or resource busy)', 'moving this process into a sub-group: allowed', payload memory.peak readable after last exit; 3: once empty 'scope directory, with the sub-groups made inside it, still there: False', 'LoadState=not-found'; 4: no session bus 'Failed to connect to bus: No medium found'. https://systemd.io/CGROUP_DELEGATION/ (re-fetched): 'Never create your own cgroups below arbitrary cgroups systemd manages, i.e cgroups you haven't set Delegate= in.'; 'it's not permitted to have processes directly attached to a cgroup that also has child cgroups'; suggested layout a 'supervisor/' leaf and 'payload-xyz/' leaves. Kernel cgroup-v2 doc (re-fetched): 'A cgroup which doesn't have any children or live processes can be destroyed by removing the directory.' docs/16-review-log.md:145 (R15): 'A cgroup is removed once it is empty.' docs/09:9 and docs/17:42 are the only Linux guidance.
- **Impact.** Phase 2's gate ('final totals survive its exit', background processes still accounted) depends on a layout the brief leaves to guesswork. A group under the shell's own scope gives CPU only and breaks systemd's single-writer rule; a plain scope is deleted with its counters the moment it empties; mkdir groups leak a directory per crashed run. Under su, sudo -u, cron or a system service there is no user bus, so tagged runs cannot be created at all. R15's premise is true only for systemd scopes.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md, a Linux paragraph after line 62: 'On Linux, ask the signed-in user's systemd over the user bus for a transient scope with Delegate set to the controllers its slice already passes down (see the controller rule). Inside it make leaf groups: holder for the waiting process and payload for the command; payload passes its controllers down and runs the command in payload/main, so a nested run can make a sibling under payload with the same files. The holder watches payload/cgroup.events; when populated is 0 it reads cpu.stat, memory.peak, memory.stat and pids.peak, writes the run file and exits, and systemd removes the scope. Never make cgroups outside such a scope. With no user bus (su, sudo -u, cron, a system service, WSL without systemd), say that tagged runs are unavailable and run the command polled.' docs/16-review-log.md R15: 'systemd removes a scope once it is empty; a cgroup made with mkdir stays until it is removed.'
- **Cost.** One paragraph in docs/17 and one corrected clause in R15.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/09:9, docs/17:42,62-69 and R15; grepped docs/ for cgroup/systemd/delegat: no placement, layout or no-bus rule anywhere, R15 covers only who reads final totals. Re-checked ownership and pass-down state read-only with my own probe (matches the lead's table). Did not re-run cgroup_scope.py or cgroup_totals.py: the first creates a Delegate=yes scope (which, per L1, changes sibling CPU sharing while other verifiers time things) and the second burns CPU. Re-fetched systemd.io and the kernel doc: both quotes are present and mean what the lead says; systemd.io's supervisor/payload layout matches the proposal. Amended the proposal to (a) take the Delegate list from L1 instead of hard-coding it and (b) run the command in payload/main so nested runs keep memory and pids (from cgroup_totals 6: a group nested under a pass-down group has memory.current and memory.peak; a pass-down group cannot hold processes). That layout is reasoned from the probe's rules, not run end to end. Unverified extra: Linux CI runners may lack a user bus too, which would block the phase 2 gate on CI.
- **Audit.** Severity changed from high to medium. The gap is real: there is no placement rule, and R15 (16-review-log.md:145) wrongly says 'A cgroup is removed once it is empty'. a-linux/cgroup_totals.out.txt section 2 shows a mkdir group still present with populated 0. The high rating, though, rests on the P2 gate failing, and the probes do not show that. The brief already points at the right documentation (17:42 names cgroup v2 delegation, and systemd.io gives the supervisor/payload layout). The wrong designs a builder might choose either fail the gate visibly and get reworked, or pass it. A plain scope is removed when empty, so 'final totals survive its exit' fails in the builder's own test. A mkdir group under the shell's own cgroup passes the CPU gate: cgroup_totals A and 1 show cpu.stat 10.677 s against wait4 10.678 s, and section 2 shows the directory and counters surviving. Its costs are memory unavailable for the run, a breach of systemd's ownership rule, and leaked directories: costly later, not a gate failure. With no user bus, tagged runs are unavailable, which AGENTS.md's general 'reported as unavailable' rule already governs. The CI-bus concern is unverified, and CI can enable linger in setup without Submeter elevating. Medium, as costly later.
- **Audit.** Correction: Keep the R15 correction. The layout text should say what the outer run reads when a nested run sits under payload (see W5): either payload minus its tagged children, or payload/main only.
- **Leads.** A-linux#2. Earlier findings touched: R15


### R73. A tagged group's kernel memory figure (memory.current/memory.peak) includes page cache and kernel memory, a different unit from the PSS/USS used for every other workload

- **Lens** A · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** a-linux/cgroup_totals.out.txt 5: child wrote 64 MB and exited: 'the child's own peak resident memory: 16.6 MB', 'memory.current ... after 66.3 MB, memory.peak 70.2 MB, populated 0', 'memory.stat: anon 0.0 MB, file 64.0 MB'. verify-4 probe on this user's 68 live leaf groups: memory.current 9.31 GB, shares anon 0.60, file 0.31, kernel 0.08. a-linux/smaps_cost.out.txt 3: PSS vs memory.current 1.88 vs 2.34, 1.41 vs 1.75, 0.47 vs 0.88 GB (+24% to +87%); anon is close to USS (1.69 vs 1.67, 1.20 vs 1.23, 0.26 vs 0.33). Kernel doc (re-fetched) lists tracked memory as 'Userland memory - page cache and anonymous memory'. Brief: docs/17-build-prompt.md:81 and docs/06-storage.md:83 'USS or PSS'; nothing names the group field.
- **Impact.** A tagged build that writes artifacts reports peak memory near its write volume (70 MB for a 17 MB process), and the cache stays charged to an empty group. Tagged and polled workloads are compared in different units (24-87% apart here), and run-over-run peak memory diffs (docs/17:127) track file writes rather than memory.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:81 add: 'For a tagged group on Linux, the figure comparable with USS is memory.stat anon plus memory.swap.current, sampled by the collector. memory.current and memory.peak include page cache and kernel memory: store the peak labelled "charged memory, includes file cache" and never add it to PSS or USS.' docs/06-storage.md:83: note the Linux tagged unit.
- **Cost.** Two sentences.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/17:81, docs/06:83, docs/09:9: no group memory field is named, so not handled; R06 is about leak detection under trimming, not this. Re-checked read-only on live groups with my own probe: 31% of this user's charged group memory is file cache and 8% kernel, so the unit difference holds without the lead's write test. Did not repeat the 64 MB write. Kept medium: the figure is misleading, not invented. Not checked: what a Windows Job's peak memory measures (committed memory per Microsoft's naming), which would make the two platforms differ again.
- **Audit.** Overlap: Write this together with R-W1 (the same sentence, docs/17:62, and its echoes at docs/05:52 and docs/09:9 are corrected there); the replacement texts must agree.
- **Leads.** A-linux#7. Earlier findings touched: none


### R74. Linux cannot stop a tagged tree leaving its group: descendants started through systemd-run or snap launchers, and a nested 'submeter run' that asks systemd for a new scope, drop out of the outer run's kernel total

- **Lens** A · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** a-linux/cgroup_leaving.out.txt 2: 'a plain child: still inside the group'; 'through systemd-run --user --scope: OUTSIDE the group'; 'through the launcher of a snap (firefox): OUTSIDE the group' (the probe uses 'snap run --shell', cgroup_leaving.py:115, it did not start the app). cgroup_totals 7: 'a tagged process moving itself to a sibling group: ended up in away; CPU time charged to the group it left: 0.002 s'. cgroup_totals 6: a nested group's usage counts in its parent and stays after removal. Kernel doc (re-fetched): 'The writer must have write access to the cgroup.procs file of the common ancestor of the source and destination cgroups.' (the whole user@N.service subtree is user-owned, verify-4 probe). Brief: docs/17-build-prompt.md:62 'nested jobs' (Windows), :69 'Do not allow breakaway'.
- **Impact.** 'Do not allow breakaway' has no Linux equivalent. Descendants started through systemd-run, snap or flatpak launchers run elsewhere and the run file's 'authoritative' kernel total omits them; polling still attributes them through ppid, but only at polled precision, and cost/diff read the run total. A tagged agent that runs 'submeter run --as builds -- pytest' loses the build from its own total under a new-scope design, while on Windows nested jobs keep it.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:69, Linux sentence: 'Linux cannot forbid leaving the group; polling still attributes such a process through its parent, and the run file says the kernel total excludes it. A launcher that finds itself in a tagged group nests by making a sibling of payload/main inside the outer payload and moving itself there, so the inner run counts in the outer one.' docs/10-known-limits.md: a row for descendants started through systemd-run, snap or flatpak launchers.
- **Cost.** Two sentences and one table row.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/17:62,69 and docs/10. Narrowed the finding: D-Bus activation, which the lead lists, is already covered by docs/10-known-limits.md:9 ('anything started through a service is charged to itself', R21), so only the run's own descendants that move themselves and nested runs remain. Read cgroup_leaving.py to confirm what it launched. Did not re-run it (it creates scopes during others' timing). The containment rule is in the re-fetched kernel doc. Kept medium, not critical: the escaped processes stay attributed through ppid, so the workload series is preserved and only the run total understates. Replaced the lead's 'sub-group of its own payload group, CPU time only' with the payload/main layout from L2, which keeps memory and pids for nested runs; that layout follows the probe's rules (cgroup_totals 6) but was not run end to end.
- **Leads.** A-linux#13. Earlier findings touched: R21


### R75. Per-process GPU load on Linux is not 'NVML or AMD sysfs, Full': amdgpu sysfs is device-wide, Intel has no source in the brief, and the per-process DRM fdinfo source is own-user only

- **Lens** A · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** verify-4 probe: cards {card1 i915, card2-5 evdi}, no gpu_busy_percent file; 12 processes with a DRM client in fdinfo; other users' processes 'fdinfo denied 294, readable 0'. https://docs.kernel.org/gpu/amdgpu/thermal.html (re-fetched): 'The SMU firmware computes a percentage of load based on the aggregate activity level in the IP cores.'; no per-process file on the page. https://docs.kernel.org/gpu/drm-usage-stats.html (re-fetched): 'One purpose of this output is to enable writing as generic as practically feasible top(1) like userspace monitoring tools.'; 'drm-engine-<keystr>: <uint> ns'; driver sections list i915, Xe, Panfrost, Panthor, Amdxdna. Brief: docs/09-telemetry.md:13 'NVML for NVIDIA, sysfs for AMD | Full'; docs/07-analysis.md:100 (not :99) Linux GPU-bound 'NVML utilisation above 90%'; docs/17-build-prompt.md:87 'On Linux use NVML and sysfs.'
- **Impact.** On Intel and AMD Linux machines a builder following the brief delivers no per-process GPU load and no Linux GPU-bound verdict, while doctor reports 'Full'. Other users' GPU clients are invisible in any case.
- **Proposed change.** docs/09-telemetry.md:13, Linux: 'NVML for NVIDIA; DRM fdinfo drm-engine-* per process where the driver provides it (i915 and xe documented); amdgpu gpu_busy_percent for device load only'; Without admin: 'Own user only'. docs/07-analysis.md:100 and docs/17:87 to match: 'busiest DRM engine or NVML utilisation above 90%'.
- **Cost.** Three table cells or clauses.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/09:13, docs/07:100 (lead cited :99), docs/17:87; R14 covers engine percentages not adding, not the Linux source. Re-ran the read-only part with my own probe (same cards, same 12 clients, other users' fdinfo all denied). Re-fetched both kernel pages: quotes present. One correction: the drm-usage-stats page does not list amdgpu among its driver sections (it says the deprecated drm-memory key 'is only printed by amdgpu'), so the proposal names only i915 and xe as documented instead of 'i915, xe, amdgpu'. NVML's per-process figure was not tested (no NVIDIA card), so that half of the row is unchanged.
- **Leads.** A-linux#14. Earlier findings touched: R14


### R76. With the defaults, the PSS/USS reading costs about 0.7-0.8% of a core on Linux, and with the measured /proc read it puts the collector over the 1% idle budget on a desktop with about 8 GB in its largest processes

- **Lens** A · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** a-linux/smaps_cost.out.txt 1 (reader CPU time, not wall): 'smaps_rollup 24,570 us (29.3 ms per GB)', '25.1 ms per GB'; 'smaps_rollup for the 25 largest: 241.6 ms a round, which at one round every 30 s is 0.805% of a core'. psutil 7.2.2 uses the same file: .venv .../psutil/_pslinux.py:1940-1942 'if HAS_PROC_SMAPS_ROLLUP: ... self._parse_smaps_rollup()'. verify-4 probe, arithmetic only: own top 25 hold 8.23 GB now; at 25.1-29.3 ms per GB every 30 s: 0.69-0.80% of a core; 241.6/30000 = 0.805%. probes/measure/snapshot_cost.out.txt:14: direct /proc stat+statm+io '0.5% at 5 s' (449 processes). Defaults docs/08-configuration.md:66-67 (uss_interval 30s, uss_top_n 25; ranges allow 10s and 200); budget docs/17-build-prompt.md:230 and R02.
- **Impact.** The slow memory reading alone takes 70-80% of the 1% idle budget here; with the measured per-tick /proc read (0.3-0.5% at 5 s) the total is about 1.1-1.3%, over budget. The cost scales with resident size, so larger machines exceed it further. The guard (docs/17:191) then slows cadence, so the budget holds by coarser data; the phase 1 CI soak, on runners with little resident memory, would not show it.
