- **Proposed change.** docs/08-configuration.md:66-67: 'On Linux the reading costs about 25-30 ms of CPU per GB resident in the processes read; with the defaults that is about 0.8% of a core on a desktop with 8 GB in its 25 largest processes. The overhead guard stretches uss_interval or shrinks uss_top_n before it slows the hot set.' docs/17-build-prompt.md:230: phase 0 measures one USS round as well as one sample. For tagged groups, take memory from memory.stat (see L4) rather than smaps.
- **Cost.** Three sentences.
- **Verification.** Did not re-time (instruction). Checked the arithmetic (241.6 ms / 30 s = 0.805%), recomputed the top-25 resident size read-only (8.23 GB, so 0.69-0.80% at the lead's own rates), and confirmed psutil's fallback reads smaps_rollup, so the measured file is the one any build would read. Added the shared measurement from probes/measure/snapshot_cost.out.txt to show the combined total crosses the idle budget. The baseline provider does not read USS yet (psutil_provider.py has no memory_full_info call), so this is a design default, not a code defect. Kept medium: the guard keeps the budget by degrading cadence, so no job fails outright. R02 mentions USS cost growing with memory but gives no figure and no guard order.
- **Leads.** A-linux#15. Earlier findings touched: R02


### R77. On Linux other users' command lines are readable, and the baseline reads, matches rules on and stores them, though the brief says they are not readable

- **Lens** A, D · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** verify-4 probe: /proc options 'rw,nosuid,nodev,noexec,relatime', hidepid set False; other users' processes with a non-empty command line 134, with arguments 67; 'baseline provider: 452 processes; other users' (root included) handed on with a command line: 134'. Code: src/submeter/providers/psutil_provider.py:67-71 reads cmdline for every process; src/submeter/attribution/rules.py:149-156 tries rules (including command-line patterns) before the Restricted bucket; src/submeter/store/raw.py:153-157 stores the redacted text. Brief: docs/10-known-limits.md:16 'Command lines and USS are not readable'; docs/02-what-it-looks-like.md:150-153 '14 processes owned by other users ... Command lines and USS are not readable, because Submeter never runs elevated.' Kernel proc doc (re-fetched): 'hidepid=invisible or hidepid=2 means hidepid=1 plus all /proc/<pid>/ will be fully invisible to other users.'
- **Impact.** On a shared Linux machine Submeter stores other accounts' and root's arguments (secrets the scrub misses included) for 48 hours, contrary to the stated behaviour; nothing leaves the machine, so fixed rule 3 holds. Command-line rules match other users' processes on Linux, so rules.toml behaves differently from the stated design. With hidepid=2 the opposite happens: those processes vanish from /proc and from Restricted, which the brief also does not cover.
- **Proposed change.** Either way: docs/10-known-limits.md:16 and docs/02-what-it-looks-like.md:152 get Linux wording ('CPU time, memory, name and command line are readable; I/O, USS, working folder and executable path are not'), and doctor reports hidepid from the /proc mount options. If (a) is chosen: psutil_provider.py and the phase-1 /proc reader leave cmdline as None when the process's real uid differs from the collector's, with a test, and docs/17 Privacy says 'Submeter keeps only the name of another user's process.'
- **Cost.** Two text cells, one sentence, and about five lines of provider code plus a test.
- **Decision.** Other users' command lines on Linux: (a) do not keep them (name only), so the store holds no other account's arguments and command-line rules behave as the brief describes; (b) keep reading them, redacted, and correct the two documents to say Linux shows them. Recommendation: (a). The brief's privacy section exists because command lines carry secrets, and the readable set here includes root's. It costs only command-line rules aimed at other users' processes, which the brief never promised.
- **Verification.** Re-read psutil_provider.py:54-101, rules.py:135-160, raw.py:150-160 and both brief passages. Re-ran the counts with my own read-only probe (counts only, no command line saved): 134 readable and 67 with arguments, matching the lead; the baseline provider hands all 134 on. Re-fetched the hidepid quote. R04 redacts secrets but does not limit whose command lines are kept, so it does not cover this. Merged the two leads; severity set between them (A: medium, D: low) at medium, because two brief passages state something false on Linux and attribution behaves differently from them. Made it needs_decision, because the lead's code change drops information the rules currently use, and the brief statement is a capability claim, not a stated policy. The hidepid=2 case (processes missing from Restricted and from reconciliation) is a second issue, quoted from the kernel doc but not tested here (no hidepid on this machine). Windows behaviour for other users' command lines was not verified.
- **Audit.** Overlap: R13 keeps the Restricted classification; both correct docs/10:16.
- **Leads.** A-linux#10, D-privacy#17. Earlier findings touched: R04


### R78. The docs/08 explorer's assumed costs are 2-21x below the measured Linux per-process cost and about 8x below the measured store cost, so its Status column is not evidence of being in budget

- **Lens** B · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/08:21 'Cost of one bulk sample, assumed: 1.5 ms plus 0.012 ms for each process'; :22 '+0.2 points for the store and the API'; :9 'assumed costs, not from measurements. Phase 0 replaces the assumptions'; :46 '1 s | 10 s | 300 | 0.7% ... | In budget'. Re-ran uv run python docs/review/probes/b-measurement/model_tables.py (arithmetic only), identical to saved: 'direct /proc, stat + statm + io 0.0517 ms = 4.3 times'; 'psutil provider, snapshot() 0.2488 ms = 20.7 times'; 'store adds about 15.9 ms per tick = 1.6 points at 1 s (assumed: 0.2 points for the store and the API)'; 'whole tick + raw store 36.0 ms ... 1s 3.60% (over)'; '10% of 300 processes hot: ... 1s 0.68%'; 'whole tick + raw store, psutil fallback 95.1 ms ... 5s 1.90% (over)'. measure/collector_footprint.out.txt: '14.1% of one core' (psutil provider, 447 processes), '120 bytes per sample row overall (brief assumes 90)'.
- **Impact.** The explorer is the only cost table the owner sees, and it understates every part. On Linux the budget at a 1 s active interval holds only if the hot set really limits per-tick reads: 3.60% when every process is handled every tick, 0.68% with a 10% hot set. The psutil fallback, measured here on Linux, is over the 1% idle limit at 5 s; Windows is unmeasured. This is not a gate failure yet: P1 replaces the psutil provider, and docs/17:230 already asks P0 to say whether the numbers are unrealistic.
- **Proposed change.** docs/08 'Sample time explorer': after the assumptions paragraph, add the measured Linux costs labelled 'Linux, baseline code, one 8-core laptop; Windows unmeasured until P0': per process 0.025 ms (stat only), 0.052 ms (stat+statm+io), 0.25 ms (psutil); Python work after the snapshot about 0.033 ms per process; raw store about 10 ms per tick at 300 processes; 120 bytes per row. State that the Status column comes from the assumed costs and is not a budget check. docs/15 P0 and docs/17:268: measure the whole tick (snapshot, deltas, attribution, store) for each provider, including the psutil fallback.
- **Cost.** About 10 lines in docs/08, one sentence in docs/15 and docs/17.
- **Verification.** Re-ran model_tables.py: output identical. Re-read docs/08:7-54, docs/17:40,71,230. Refutation: docs/08:9 and :11 already label the explorer a model with assumed costs that P0 replaces, and docs/17:230 asks P0 to report unrealistic numbers, so the design anticipates the gap. docs/17:71 says that on Linux the hot set is read at the active interval and the cold set at the cold interval. With a 10% hot set the measured whole tick plus store is 0.68% at 1 s, so the headline claim that the 'In budget' row is over budget holds only when every process is read every tick. The 14.1% figure comes from the baseline psutil provider, which P1 replaces (docs/17:40 'psutil is the fallback and the cross-check, not the hot path'), so it is not a P1 gate failure. The 15.9 ms store cost is the difference between two separate runs and is approximate. Severity downgraded from high to medium.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#4. Earlier findings touched: R02


### R79. The Rust switching rule does not say what 'one full sample' or 'holds 80 MB' measures, and its 10 ms is a third of the budget it stands in for, so it can point either way

- **Lens** B · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/11:21-22 'One full sample of 300 processes takes more than 10 ms in phase 0.' / 'The collector process holds more than 80 MB.' (the lead cites :69-70); docs/17:56 'measure one full sample. If it takes more than 10 ms at 300 processes, or the collector holds more than 80 MB, stop and tell me'; docs/11:7 'its cost is the system call, not the interpreter' (the lead cites :55). model_tables.out.txt section 4 (rerun identical): 'direct /proc, stat only 7.5 ms -> stay'; 'direct /proc, stat + statm + io 15.5 ms -> switch'; 'psutil provider, snapshot() 74.6 ms -> switch'; 'collector memory, resident 39 MB -> stay'; '10 ms per sample is 1.0% of a core at 1 s and 0.2% at 5 s; the budget itself allows 30 ms per tick at 1 s (3%) and 50 ms per tick at 5 s (1%)'. Section 3: whole tick plus store 36.0 ms against a 15.5 ms snapshot, so 57% of the tick comes after the snapshot.
- **Impact.** The result depends on choices the rule leaves open: the provider, the part of the tick (snapshot only, or with deltas, attribution and store), the memory figure and the machine. On this machine's numbers each choice changes the outcome. The rule can call for Rust while the budget holds (a 15.5 ms snapshot is 1.55% at 1 s). It can also say stay while the whole collector is over budget (a 7.5 ms snapshot inside a 36 ms tick). It is the owner's trigger ('stop and tell me'), so the owner still decides, but on a figure that may be the wrong one.
- **Proposed change.** docs/11 'When to switch the collector to Rust' and docs/17:56: replace the first two bullets with: 'In phase 0, run the collector (snapshot, deltas, attribution and raw-store writes) for 10 minutes with 300 processes on the owner's Windows machine, with the provider that will ship and with the psutil fallback. Report its process CPU time as a share of one core at the active and idle intervals, and its private bytes (Windows) or USS (Linux). Switch if the CPU share exceeds the budget (3% active, 1% idle) at the default cadence, or memory exceeds 80 MB.' Qualify docs/11:7: on Linux /proc is read per process, and Python work after the snapshot is about half the tick.
- **Cost.** About 5 lines in docs/11 and docs/17.
- **Decision.** Should the Rust trigger be the budget itself, or should the 10 ms snapshot rule stay? The budget version compares the whole collector's CPU share with 3% and 1%, and a named memory figure with 80 MB, on the owner's Windows PC. Keeping the 10 ms rule would mean defining what 'one full sample' includes. Recommendation: use the budget. The 10 ms rule stands in for it, and the measured numbers show the two disagree in both directions.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/11. The bullets are at lines 21-22, not 69-70, and the 'system call' sentence is at line 7, not 55. Re-read docs/17:56 and :230. Re-ran model_tables.py: output identical. Searched docs/ for a definition of 'full sample': the only other mention is docs/17:230 'Measure the cost of one full sample in phase 0'. Refutation: the rule is the owner's own trigger to be told, not an automatic switch, so no gate is broken. Severity downgraded from high to medium. The memory branch never decides on Linux, where the collector measured 39 MB resident.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#5. Earlier findings touched: R02


### R80. The leak acceptance ('50 MB an hour flagged within 2 hours') sits exactly on the default threshold of 50 MiB/h, its units are undefined, and the earliest possible flag comes after 2 hours

- **Lens** B, E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/01:18 'A leak of 50 MB an hour is flagged within 2 hours'; docs/17:154 'growth of 50 MB an hour must be flagged within two hours'; core/config.py:121 'min_slope: Rate = 50 * 1024**2 / 3600.0'; core/units.py:56 'Units are binary'; analysis/growth.py:73 'duration < min_duration_s', :78 'slope < min_slope_per_s'; docs/17:123 'A bucket is final 30 seconds after it ends'. uv run python docs/review/probes/verify-5/leak_checks.py: '100 MiB/h, 120 buckets spanning 119 min: not enough data yet' / '121 buckets spanning 120 min: FLAGGED'; '50 MiB/h ... noise 0 MB: 100% noise 5 MB: 45% noise 30 MB: 45%'; '50 MB/h decimal ... 0% ... 0% ... 14%'; '55 MiB/h ... 100% 100% 97%'. Re-ran b-measurement/growth_acceptance.py and e-baseline/growth_cases.py: both identical to saved.
- **Impact.** Job 3's acceptance and the P3 trigger fixture fail as written. A load generator leaking 50 MiB/h is flagged in about 45% of 2-hour windows once there is any noise. One leaking 50 decimal MB/h is never flagged without noise. Even a fast leak cannot be flagged until 2 h 01 min 30 s after it starts, when the 121st minute bucket becomes final.
- **Proposed change.** docs/01:18, the docs/15 P3 gate and docs/17:154: state the acceptance as a rate clearly above min_slope, with a reachable delay, for example 'a leak of 75 MiB an hour is flagged within 2 h 5 min'. The alternative is to lower Leak.min_slope (core/config.py:121, docs/08:88). Say in docs/12 or in docs/08 'How configuration behaves' that MB in settings and acceptance criteria means 1024 x 1024 bytes. Name the leak window precisely; docs/06:64 says only '2 to 48 h back'.
- **Cost.** About three sentences of docs, or one default in config.py.
- **Decision.** Raise the acceptance rate (for example 75 MiB/h within 2 h 5 min, with the 50 MiB/h threshold unchanged), or lower the threshold (for example 30 MiB/h, keeping 50 MiB/h in the acceptance)? Recommendation: raise the acceptance rate. A lower threshold also raises the plateau test's false flags (M4). The two leads disagree here: B recommends lowering the threshold, E recommends raising the rate.
- **Verification.** Re-read config.py:119-121, units.py:29-35 and :56, growth.py:63-84, docs/01:18 and docs/17:123,154. Wrote and ran verify-5/leak_checks.py with 200 seeds per noisy cell; results as quoted. Re-ran growth_acceptance.py and growth_cases.py: both identical. Searched the glossary and docs/08 for a definition of MB: none. Refutation: if 'within 2 hours' were measured from the first observable bucket, the timing complaint would shrink to 1.5 minutes. The threshold equality and the units problem stand on their own, so severity stays high. docs/06:64 does state a loose leak window ('2 to 48 h back'), so the claim that the window is unstated is softened to 'imprecise'.
- **Audit.** Severity changed from high to medium. Re-ran verify-5/leak_checks.py: output identical. My probe docs/review/probes/audit/audit_growth_windows.py, Part 3: 50 MiB/h is flagged 45% of the time at +/-5 MB and 44% at +/-40 MB, and 75 MiB/h is flagged 100% at both. So the core claim holds: the acceptance rate equals Leak.min_slope (config.py:121), and with any noise the result is a coin flip. It does not reach high. No number shown is wrong. Leaks clearly above the threshold are flagged. The P3 gate as written (docs/15:21, docs/17:271) names no rate. The 50 MB/2 h sentence is a requirement in docs/01:18 and docs/17:154, and a noise-free fixture passes it 100%. The 2-hour overshoot is 1 min 30 s (121st bucket, final 30 s after it ends). The fault is in the wording of the criterion, and it shows up as a flaky test rather than a silent product fault. That makes it misleading, which is medium.
- **Audit.** Correction: The 'units are undefined' sub-claim is wrong. docs/14-baseline-modules.md:121 says 'Sizes are binary: 1 GB is 1024 cubed bytes' for the module that parses '50MB/h' (units.py:56, docs/14:117). The '50 MB decimal: 0%' row therefore tests a reading the brief does not use; at most, docs/12 could repeat the convention. The proposed 75 MiB/h acceptance holds under today's plateau test, but it fails if M4's suggested fix is adopted. With a tail lower bound that must clear min_slope, a 75 MiB/h leak passes only 38% of the time at +/-40 MB and 83% at +/-20 MB (audit Part 4). M3's acceptance rate and M4's test must be set together.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#6, E-baseline#12. Earlier findings touched: R11, R34


### R81. The slow-growth check has no threshold of its own; with the only slope setting (leak.min_slope) it cannot flag any leak that the hourly check would miss

- **Lens** B · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** core/config.py:124-125 'class SlowGrowth(Record): window: Duration = 7 * 86400.0', with no slope field; docs/08:88-89 list only leak.min_slope 50MB/h and slow_growth.window 7d; docs/17:155 'fit the daily minimum of memory over at least 7 days. This catches leaks too slow for the check above'; docs/16:269 (R34 fix). verify-5/leak_checks.out.txt: "SlowGrowth fields: ['window']"; '8 d of hourly buckets from 00:00: 8 daily minimums spanning 7 d; with leak.min_slope: growth is below the threshold; with 5 MiB/h: FLAGGED'; '7 d of hourly buckets from 00:00: 7 daily minimums spanning 6 d; with leak.min_slope: not enough data yet'. b-measurement/baseline_table.py rerun identical.
- **Impact.** With the defaults, the P3 gate fixture 'a leak too slow for the hourly check' fails, so R34's fix does not work as configured. A builder has to invent a threshold that no document gives.
- **Proposed change.** core/config.py SlowGrowth: add min_slope (for example 5 MiB/h, below Leak.min_slope), and define the window as a count of daily minimums (at least 7 minima, which needs 8 days of hourly buckets) rather than a 7-day span. docs/08:89: add the min_slope row. docs/17:155 and :180: state both values. Add a test with a 10 MiB/h leak over 8 days.
- **Cost.** About 6 lines in config.py, two rows in docs/08, one test.
- **Verification.** Re-read config.py:119-137, docs/08:87-90, docs/17:155,180 and docs/14:352. Grepped docs/ for any slow-growth threshold: none. Ran leak_checks.py (section M5). Refutation: the 'span of 6 days' problem arises only if the builder passes the 7-day window as min_duration; a builder who treats 7 minima as enough avoids it, so that part is conditional. The missing threshold is not conditional. leak.min_slope is the only slope setting, and any leak it flags over 7 days the hourly check flags too, so with it the slow check adds nothing. Severity kept at high; verdict changed to 'confirmed with changes' because the span sub-claim depends on wiring.
- **Audit.** Severity changed from high to medium. Confirmed: config.py:124-125 SlowGrowth has only 'window', docs/08:88-89 lists no slow-growth slope, and the leak_checks.py rerun is identical. But no slow-growth detector exists yet. docs/17:155 says the check 'catches leaks too slow for the check above', so reusing leak.min_slope contradicts the sentence it implements, and no builder would do it. The P3 trigger fixture would fail at once and force a threshold to be chosen. The real defect is a missing default the builder has to guess. That is costly later, not a gate that fails under real conditions: medium.
- **Audit.** Correction: H7 recommends deferring slow growth and dropping 'a leak too slow for the hourly check' from the P3 gate. If H7 is accepted, M5 is moot, so H7 should be ruled before M5 is applied as 'fix now'. The window-as-span sub-claim depends on how the builder wires it, as the verifier already noted.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#8. Earlier findings touched: R34


### R82. The usual range has no default floor and an ambiguous one: an idle series gets a zero-width range, docs/07 and usual_range() put the floor on different quantities, and the statistic baselined is never named

- **Lens** B · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/07:36 'The deviation is never taken as less than 0.03 GB', a floor on the MAD (the lead cites :116). analysis/growth.py:98 'spread = max(floor, _MAD_TO_SIGMA * median(...))', a floor on the scaled spread; :91 'floor: float = 0.0'. config.py:128-131: Baseline has no floor. verify-5/config_and_baseline.out.txt: "Baseline settings: ['learning_period', 'sensitivity', 'window']"; 'idle workload, 20 minutes at 0.000 cores and one at 0.001: (0.0, 0.0, 0.0)'; 'day 8, docs/07 sentence (floor on the deviation): 3.242 to 3.598; usual_range(floor=0.03): 3.300 to 3.540; docs/07 table prints 3.25 to 3.60'. baseline_table.py rerun identical: 'floor on the scaled spread ... days flagged: [14, 23, ...]'; on minute buckets, lognormal cores: '61 of 1440 ordinary minutes fall outside'. docs/17:156 'computed from minute buckets' against docs/07:31 'daily peak memory'.
- **Impact.** R11's near-zero-spread problem returns for any idle workload: with CPU at 0, every non-zero minute is an anomaly. The two readings of the floor give half-widths of 0.178 GB and 0.120 GB, so docs/07's day 14 is inside the range under one reading and outside under the other. If single minute buckets of a skewed CPU series were compared with the range, about 4% of minutes would be flagged (this assumes a lognormal shape).
- **Proposed change.** docs/07:36 and the usual_range docstring: say which quantity the floor bounds (the scaled spread or the MAD), and give a floor per metric. core/config.py Baseline: add per-metric floors with non-zero defaults (for example 0.05 cores and 32 MiB), plus rows in docs/08. docs/17:156: name the value compared with the range (for example hourly p95 or daily peak, built from minute buckets), and require an anomaly to persist, for example for 3 consecutive values. Regenerate the docs/07 table from usual_range(), or mark it approximate.
- **Cost.** About 10 lines across docs/07, docs/08, docs/17 and config.py.
- **Verification.** Re-read growth.py:87-99, config.py:128-131, docs/07:29-74 and docs/17:156. Recomputed day 8 in config_and_baseline.py. The sentence's reading gives 3.242 to 3.598 against the table's 3.25 to 3.60, close given the rounded inputs. The code's reading gives 3.300 to 3.540, which is not close. Refutation: docs/07:5 calls the data illustrative, so the table mismatch on its own would be low. The 4% minute false-alarm figure rests on an assumed lognormal shape and on comparing single minutes, which no document specifies. The zero-width range for an idle series is shown directly. Severity kept at medium; the lead's line numbers (07:116, growth.py:203/210) are corrected here.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#9. Earlier findings touched: R11


### R83. The P1 per-process 5% gate and the P2 2% gate use the product's own source as the reference, so neither can see CPU that escapes the group or that the collector never reads

- **Lens** B, G · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/17:269 'per-process figures agree with kernel counters (performance counters on Windows, /proc on Linux) within 5%'; docs/17:71 'On Linux, read /proc directly'; docs/17:62 'The kernel's totals for the group are authoritative'; docs/17:270 and docs/15:17 'charged within 2% of the kernel's total'. learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/procthread/job-objects (re-fetched): 'After a process is associated with a job, by default any child processes it creates using CreateProcess are also associated with the job. (Child processes created using Win32_Process.Create are not associated with the job.)'. a-linux/cgroup_scope.out.txt shows an independent reference that agrees: 'payload CPU seconds, kernel: 0.646515' against 'payload CPU seconds, wait4: 0.646941'. reconcile_terms.out.txt: 'one tick is 10 ms'; 'nanosecond run time per process (schedstat) present: True'.
- **Impact.** P2's clause compares the group total with itself. A process outside the group, whether started before assignment or created through WMI on Windows, is missing from both sides, so the gate passes with a wrong tagged total (job 1). P1's Linux clause checks only parsing and delta arithmetic. Neither gate names a window or a minimum CPU level, and /proc times come in 10 ms ticks, so a per-tick comparison for a small process fails on rounding alone.
- **Proposed change.** docs/15 P1 and P2, and docs/17:269-270: use the load generator's own accounting as the reference. P1: each burner reports its own CPU seconds (getrusage RUSAGE_SELF on Linux, GetProcessTimes on Windows) at known instants. Over 10 minutes, Submeter's stored CPU for that process agrees within 5% or 0.05 s, whichever is larger, for processes above 10% of a core. P2: the generator reports its children's summed CPU (wait4 or RUSAGE_CHILDREN on Linux; GetProcessTimes on handles kept open on Windows). The run's stored CPU equals that sum plus the generator's own time within 2%, and a grandchild started in the command's first millisecond is a member of the group (IsProcessInJob, cgroup.procs). Keep the kernel and performance-counter comparison as a parsing check only.
- **Cost.** About 10 lines in docs/15 and docs/17, plus a reporting requirement on the load generator.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/17:62,71,269-270, docs/15:13,17 and R10 at docs/16:109-113, which chose kernel counters but says nothing about independence. Re-fetched the Microsoft Job Objects page; both quoted sentences are present. Read a-linux/cgroup_scope.out.txt. Refutation: the P1 per-process comparison is more than a copy test. It can catch rows suppressed by the change threshold (docs/17:111), delta errors, and stale cold-set rows read at a different instant, so G's claim that it cannot catch stale cold-set rows is overstated. What it cannot catch is CPU of processes the collector never sees (R01); the reconciliation invariant covers that instead. P2's clause is self-referential as written. G#1's high was downgraded to medium: the gate is weak, but a wrong number also needs a separate escape defect, such as the launcher's assignment race, which another lens reports. Whether the Windows PDH counters share a source with the bulk snapshot is unverified. The hot_cpu_threshold sub-claim (0.5% of a core is half a tick per second) is real but affects only how often processes move in and out of the hot set, not a number.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#13, G-buildability#1. Earlier findings touched: R10, R01, R15


### R84. The verdict rules exist in two disagreeing tables, 'signals disagree' and 'maximum frequency' are undefined, and verdict.thresholds accepts any key

- **Lens** B, F · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/02:69 'The rules are checked in this order and the first match wins'. docs/02:73-77: Thermal 'Frequency below 80% of maximum' (no load condition); Memory 'Commit above 86%. Stays until it falls below 82%'; CPU 'above 86% ... below 80%'; 'Memory tightening | Warning | Commit above 74%'; 'No bottleneck | Healthy'. brief.html:5388-5392 mock code: "if(S.freq<0.8)v='thermal'; else if(mp>0.86||(v==='mem'&&mp>0.82))v='mem'; else if(cp>0.86||(v==='cpu'&&cp>0.8))v='cpu'". docs/07:97-102: CPU 'CPU time above 85%'; Memory 'Commit above 85% of limit, page writes rising'; Thermal 'Frequency below 80% of maximum under load'; Inconclusive 'Signals disagree' (the lead cites :175-182). docs/17:157 lists six classes and no healthy result; docs/01:13 'reads Inconclusive when the signals disagree'; docs/08:94 'see verdict table'. config.py:143 'thresholds: dict[str, float]'. verify-5/config_and_baseline.out.txt: "verdict.thresholds.cpu_tyme = 0.85 accepted", while 'verdict.thresold (misspelt group) refused'; 'max 4.80 GHz, base 1.80 GHz = 37.5% of max; 80% of max is 3.84 GHz'. docs.kernel.org/admin-guide/pm/intel_pstate.html (re-fetched): 'One important property of turbo P-states is that they are not sustainable.'
- **Impact.** Job 2: a builder following docs/07 or docs/17 has no result for a healthy machine. With first-match order, Inconclusive never occurs, and 'disagree' cannot be written as a fixture. If 'maximum' is read as the turbo maximum, a laptop under sustained all-core load reads 'Thermally throttled'; this machine's base frequency is 37.5% of its maximum. A misspelt threshold key silently leaves the default in place, against docs/08:5 'unknown keys are rejected'.
- **Proposed change.** docs/07: make its table the single rule set. Add 'No bottleneck / Healthy' for when no rule matches and there are at least 60 s of data. Define maximum as the base (nominal) frequency, or as the sustained all-core frequency measured in P0, and keep 'under load'. Define 'disagree' as two or more rules matching, or a rule matching while its ruled-out condition holds, and say the verdict is then Inconclusive. Give each threshold its on and off values (hysteresis). docs/02 'How the verdict is chosen': say it is the mock's simplification and point to docs/07. docs/08:94: 'see the verdict table in docs/07'. core/config.py: Verdict.thresholds becomes a model with named fields.
- **Cost.** About 15 lines of docs and about 15 lines in config.py.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/02:56-79, docs/07:93-104, docs/17:157, docs/08:5,94 and brief.html:5386-5392. Ran config_and_baseline.py: an unknown threshold key is accepted. Read the sysfs cpufreq files (read-only). Re-fetched the intel_pstate page; the quote is present. Refutation: docs/02:5 and :79 say the mock is synthetic and that its evidence numbers follow the simulation, so docs/02's table describes the mock's code rather than a spec. Even so, docs/02:69 presents it as how the verdict is chosen, and it is the only place with a healthy result. docs/07:104 calls the thresholds starting points, which explains 85 against 86 but not different classes, order and hysteresis. The Windows meaning of 'maximum' was not verified. Severity kept at medium.
- **Audit.** Overlap: Its point that verdict.thresholds accepts any key is fixed under B4; this finding keeps the rule-table items.
- **Leads.** B-measurement#14, F-consistency#2. Earlier findings touched: R12


### R85. histogram.percentile(q=100) returns the top of the scale (256 cores) for about a quarter of time-weighted buckets

- **Lens** E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** core/histogram.py:77 'total = sum(counts)' (sum() of floats is compensated in Python 3.12 and later; pyproject requires >=3.12), :80 'target = total * min(max(q, 0.0), 100.0) / 100.0', :85 'if running + count >= target', :91 'running += count' (plain addition), :92 'return scale.high'. uv run python docs/review/probes/verify-5/rollup_and_histogram.py: 'q=100 returned 256 cores (top of scale) in 276' of 1000 buckets with weights of 0.995 to 1.005 s; 'control, integer weights of exactly 1.0 s: q=100 gives 4.14 cores'; 'fsum vs plain loop on 0.1 x 10: 1.0, (0.9999999999999999, 1.0)'. e-baseline/rollup_cases.py rerun identical: '469 report a maximum of 256 cores'.
- **Impact.** Bucket (core/models.py:150-175) has no CPU maximum field, so the CPU minimum-to-maximum envelope that docs/17:132 requires can come only from cores_percentile(q=0 and q=100). About a quarter of buckets would show 256 cores. There is no caller yet. Secondary problem: values below the scale's low edge are read by interpolating within [0, low), so a steady 1 MiB and a steady 15 MiB both read p50 = 8.0 MiB, and 0.002 cores reads 0.005. That contradicts histogram.py:8-9 'within one bin of the true value: a factor of about 1.17'.
- **Proposed change.** core/histogram.py percentile: when the loop ends without a match, or when q >= 100, return the upper edge of the last non-empty bin; add a test with float weights. docs/14 histogram entry: say that a value below the low edge reads as somewhere in [0, low), and that the 1.17 factor holds only inside the scale.
- **Cost.** About 3 lines plus one test.
- **Verification.** Re-read histogram.py:73-92. Wrote my own probe with float weights of 0.995 to 1.005 s, as a monotonic-clock interval would give: 276 of 1000 buckets return 256 cores, and none do with exact 1.0 weights. That confirms float rounding as the cause: compensated sum() against a plain loop, plus target computed as total*100/100. Grepped src for callers: only cores_percentile and memory_percentile (rollup.py:164,168), which only tests use. Severity kept at medium: the bug is latent with no caller, though the CPU envelope makes a caller likely.
- **Leads.** E-baseline#19. Earlier findings touched: R27


### R86. Baselines must be kept 'separately for idle and active states', but no document defines idle or active for a workload, and the usual-range window is stated both as 14 values and as 14 days

- **Lens** F · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/17:156 'keep baselines separately for idle and active states'; docs/16:121 (R11 fix); docs/04:30 'Baselines learned separately for idle and active use'; docs/06:39 'Active seconds, so idle and busy periods can be compared separately'. Three unrelated candidates: docs/08:64 idle_after (machine level), docs/08:65 hot_cpu_threshold 0.5% (per process), store/rollup.py:37,68 active_share = 0.005 (per series, tied to no setting). Window: docs/07:36 'the median of the last 14 values that were not flagged'; docs/06:65 '14 d back'; docs/17:156 'over a trailing 14 days'; docs/08:91 baseline.window 14d. A grep of docs/ for idle or active definitions, excluding interval names, finds only these lines; the glossary (docs/12:9) defines only hot and cold sets.
- **Impact.** Two builders will split idle and active differently, so no fixture can specify the baseline anomaly detector (job 3), and R11's false-alarm fix depends on that split. On minute buckets, '14 values' and '14 days' differ by a factor of 1440.
- **Proposed change.** docs/07 baseline section: 'a minute bucket is active when active_s is at least half of covered_s, where a sample is active when the workload uses at least hot_cpu_threshold of one core; idle and active baselines use only buckets of their own state'. Tie rollup.py's active_share to sampling.hot_cpu_threshold, and note it in docs/14. State one window rule, for example the last 14 days of unflagged values of the statistic named in M6.
- **Cost.** Four sentences in docs/07, one line in rollup.py.
- **Verification.** Grepped docs/ for idle and active definitions: only the lines cited. Re-read rollup.py:37,68 and growth.py:87-99. Refutation: the lead's sub-claim that min_values=5 conflicts with the 7-day learning period does not hold, because the detector can enforce learning_period separately and usual_range's min_values is only a floor beneath it; that sub-claim is dropped. The rest stands.
- **Leads.** F-consistency#10. Earlier findings touched: R11


### R87. The overhead budget covers the collector in docs/08 and 'the monitor' in docs/17, the 150 MB memory budget exists only in docs/17, and the 'submeter' workload holds one pid

- **Lens** F · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/08:140 overhead_fraction 'The collector's CPU time as a share of one core'; docs/08:33 'until the collector is back under 3%'; docs/08:22 'plus 0.2 points for the store and the API' (the API runs in the server, docs/17:53); docs/17:230 'The monitor must not become the problem ... under 1% of one core at idle cadence, under 3% at active cadence, and under 150 MB of memory across collector and server'; docs/15:13 'stays inside the budget' names no budget; docs/11:22's 80 MB is the Rust trigger. attribution/rules.py:124,174 take a single self_pid. verify-5/config_and_baseline.out.txt: 'pid 100: submeter (submeter itself)' / 'pid 200: unattributed (no rule matched)' for a submeter-server process. docs/04:17 'Submeter itself always shown'. No shipped rule claims the server (docs/08:219-252).
- **Impact.** The P1 soak gate cannot be judged without knowing which processes and which memory figure count. The server's rollups, analysis and API land in Unattributed, so Submeter under-reports its own cost, and the built-in 'submeter over budget' alert (docs/08:150) ignores the server.
- **Proposed change.** docs/08 after overhead_limit, docs/08:140 and docs/15:13: state that the budget covers every Submeter process (collector, server and launcher holders). Their summed CPU stays under 1% of one core at idle cadence and 3% at active cadence, and memory stays under 150 MB across collector and server, with the figure named (private bytes on Windows, USS on Linux). The overhead guard acts on the collector; overhead_fraction reports the total. docs/14 attribution entry and rules.py: the submeter workload claims every Submeter process (a set of pids or an executable match), not one pid.
- **Cost.** About three sentences in docs/08 and docs/15, one in docs/14; a few lines in rules.py.
- **Decision.** Does the overhead budget cover only the collector, or every Submeter process (collector plus server plus launcher holders)? Recommendation: every Submeter process, because docs/17:230 says 'the monitor' and the server's work is real overhead on the machine.
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/08:22,33,72,140,150, docs/17:53,230, docs/15:13 and rules.py:118-128,168-176. Ran the Attributor with self_pid on two Submeter processes: the server process is unattributed. Searched docs/ for a memory budget: only docs/17:230; docs/11:22's 80 MB is a switching rule. Refutation: 'across collector and server' in docs/17:230 can be read as qualifying memory only, and that ambiguity is the finding itself. A user rule in rules.toml could claim the server, but no shipped rule does.
- **Leads.** F-consistency#9. Earlier findings touched: R02


### R88. Nothing records which raw days have been rolled up and no order of catch-up and retention is stated, so raw days can be deleted before roll-up; retention stops while the server is down

- **Lens** C · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** files.py:121-131: expired_raw tests file age only. verify-6/v6_tiers.out.txt: 'expired_raw(self, now: 'float', retention_s: 'float') -> 'list[Path]''. The lead's retention_calendar.py re-ran identically: 'server back at 09:00 on the 15th after five days away: expired_raw offers ['2026-11-10.db', '2026-11-11.db', '2026-11-12.db'] for deletion. FileMap members that record what has been rolled up: none'. docs/17:52: the collector 'writes the raw files and nothing else'. docs/17:53: the server does 'rollups, retention'. Grepping docs for catch-up, rolled-up and watermark finds no rule.
- **Impact.** Suppose the server is down longer than retention.raw (48 h) while the collector keeps writing. A retention pass that runs before catch-up then deletes days whose minute and hourly buckets were never built, and that trend history is lost for good. While the server is down, raw files also grow with no budget enforced, roughly 0.4 to 0.7 GB a day.
- **Proposed change.** docs/06-storage.md and docs/17-build-prompt.md Store: 'The server keeps a rolled-up-until mark in the history file. At start it rolls up every raw day it holds, oldest first, before any retention pass, and deletes a raw file only after the mark has passed that day's end.' src/submeter/store/files.py expired_raw: take the mark as an argument and never return a day after it.
- **Cost.** Two sentences in each doc and about 5 lines in files.py.
- **Decision.** Who deletes raw files? Options: (a) only the server, gated on the rolled-up-until mark; (b) the server as in (a), plus a collector-side safety cap (for example, delete raw files older than 7 days, with a logged finding) so that a dead server cannot fill the disk. Recommendation: (b).
- **Verification.** I re-ran retention_calendar.py and got identical output, and printed expired_raw's signature, which has no roll-up input. I searched docs/ and found no catch-up or ordering rule. R13 ('while those still exist'), R23 and R26 do not cover ordering. I downgraded from high to medium: the loss needs both more than 48 h of server downtime with the collector running and an implementation that runs retention before catch-up. Normal running loses nothing.
- **Leads.** C-storage#5. Earlier findings touched: R13, R23, R26


### R89. synchronous=NORMAL does not deliver 'a crash or power loss may lose up to 5 seconds', and the kill test cannot detect the gap

- **Lens** C · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** raw.py:70 'PRAGMA synchronous=NORMAL'; docs/17:138 'Durability: synchronous=NORMAL. A crash or power loss may lose up to 5 seconds of samples'. Re-fetched https://www.sqlite.org/pragma.html: 'A transaction committed in WAL mode with synchronous=NORMAL might roll back following a power loss or system crash. Transactions are durable across application crashes regardless of the synchronous setting or journal mode.' and 'In WAL mode when synchronous is NORMAL (1), the WAL file is synchronized before each checkpoint ... but no sync operations occur during most transactions.' Lead's wal_readers.out.txt: 'the log reached 3.9 MiB and was reset after 149 ticks. At a 1 s interval that is one log sync every 2.5 minutes'. The docs/15:13 gate tests only 'Killing the collector'.
- **Impact.** On a power loss or OS crash, SQLite guarantees only the samples up to the last checkpoint: minutes, not 5 s, and longer at the idle cadence. The gate passes by construction, because a killed process loses nothing at any setting. There is no corruption risk.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:138 and R36: pick one option. (a) Run a PASSIVE checkpoint every 5 s, which syncs the log. (b) Use synchronous=FULL for raw files. (c) Restate the promise: 'a program crash loses nothing committed; a power loss or OS crash may lose the samples since the last checkpoint, up to a few minutes'. Either way, state that the P1 gate does not test power loss.
- **Cost.** One sentence, plus one timer line in the collector loop if (a).
- **Decision.** Should power-loss loss be bounded at 5 s, by (a) a PASSIVE checkpoint every 5 s (about 0.4 syncs a second) or (b) synchronous=FULL (about 1 sync per tick), or should the promise be relaxed (c) to 'since the last checkpoint'? Recommendation: (c). A few minutes lost at a power cut is a short gap that covered seconds already handle. Choose (a), measured in P1, only if the 5 s bound matters to you.
- **Verification.** I re-fetched pragma.html; both quotes are present and mean what the lead says. I read raw.py:68-71. I did not re-run the strace part of wal_readers.py, which is heavy I/O while other verifiers are running; the vendor text and the code are enough. R36 states the promise but not the mechanism. I kept medium: the brief's promise is misleading, but only a few minutes at a power loss are affected.
- **Leads.** C-storage#6. Earlier findings touched: R36


### R90. A quiet process's CPU is held in memory until a keyframe: a crash loses up to 10 minutes of it, and in normal running it is stamped up to 10 minutes late

- **Lens** C, E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** raw.py:138 writes a row only if 'full or moved or pending >= self._cpu_share * waited'. A process holding steady below 0.5% of a core never meets that. config.py:64-66 allows a keyframe interval of 1m to 1h. verify-6/v6_held_cpu.out.txt: '40 processes at 0.4% of a core for 900 s from 10:55: workload_raw holds 144.00 CPU-s, process_raw holds 96.16; a crash now loses 47.84 CPU-s'; 'process rows were written: [1, 601]'; '10:00 hour: these processes used 48.00 CPU-s; process rows stamped in that hour hold 0.16'. The lead's raw_store_cases.py re-ran identically: 'process_raw holds 1.2 in 300 rows; workload_raw holds 648.0'.
- **Impact.** This contradicts raw.py:5 and docs/14 ('per-process sums stay exact') and the 5 s promise in docs/17:138. After any collector crash, process-level views undercount quiet processes by up to one keyframe interval. In normal running, every hour, quiet processes' CPU lands in the wrong hour of the hourly top-ten executables (R28). Workload totals are unaffected.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/store/raw.py:138: also write the row when waited >= 60 s and when the batch crosses a UTC hour boundary. docs/14-baseline-modules.md store.raw: 'a crash loses at most 60 s of a quiet process's CPU from process rows; workload rows lose nothing committed'.
- **Cost.** About 4 lines and one test. The lead estimates about 10% more process rows.
- **Verification.** I re-ran raw_store_cases.py and got identical output. I wrote v6_held_cpu.py, which writes through RawStore and reads with a second read-only connection standing in for a crash; it confirmed both the loss and the hour shift. The flush in close() (raw.py:227-232) runs only on a clean close. R31's keyframes bound how far back a reader must look, not what a crash loses, and R36 promises 5 s. I did not re-run the 12-kill crash_kill.py loop; my probe reproduces the mechanism.
- **Leads.** C-storage#7, E-baseline#16. Earlier findings touched: R28, R31, R36


### R91. The baseline's only raw-file reader is the writer class: reading creates files, takes the write lock and checkpoints the collector's log

- **Lens** C · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** raw.py:66-76: RawFile.__init__ runs mkdir, opens read-write, sets the journal mode, runs CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS and does INSERT OR IGNORE into meta. raw.py:233: close() runs wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE). workload_samples exists only on RawFile (raw.py:195). verify-6/v6_read_path.out.txt: '1. reading a day with no file returned 0 rows and left: 2026-01-01.db 40960 B'; '2. RawFile opened while the collector holds the write lock: refused: database is locked'; '3. ... 2026-11-19.db 4096 B, ... -wal 1075352 B / RawFile read 120 rows and on close the .db went from 4096 B to 49152 B'. AGENTS.md: 'Each data file has one writer: the collector writes raw files'. Re-fetched https://www.sqlite.org/wal.html: 'If a database file is separated from its WAL file, then transactions that were previously committed to the database might be lost'.
- **Impact.** A server that reads through RawFile becomes a second writer of the collector's files, which breaks the one-writer rule. It also creates files for days the collector never wrote, competes for the collector's write lock (with a 5 s busy wait), and checkpoints crashed files. Separately, a quarantine that moves only the .db (docs/17:139) loses a crashed day whose data is still in its -wal.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/store/raw.py: add a read-only opener (sqlite3.connect('file:PATH?mode=ro', uri=True), never immutable=1) that raises if the file is missing, and move workload_samples onto it. docs/17-build-prompt.md:139: 'quarantine moves the .db with its -wal and -shm'.
- **Cost.** About 20 lines in raw.py, a test, and one doc clause.
- **Verification.** I wrote v6_read_path.py and confirmed all three writes. I re-ran side_files.py: immutable=1 fails on a crashed file and mode=ro reads all 120 ticks. docs/17:112 says 'Open closed files only for the length of a query' but never says read-only. The cluster's second problem, quarantine moving only the .db, is kept as a doc clause. Severity stays medium: one writer per file is a design rule from AGENTS.md, not one of the three fixed rules.
- **Leads.** C-storage#10. Earlier findings touched: R23, R36


### R92. The 0.1 GB minute-tier estimate assumes an unstated compact bucket encoding and only 9 series, and the deletion order cannot keep the promised 'exactly enforced' 2 GB cap

- **Lens** C · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** histogram.py:26-30,53-54: two scales of 64 bins plus 2, so 132 values a bucket. docs/06:111: '9 series, 1,440 buckets a day, 250 bytes each'. The lead's row_sizes.py re-ran identically: 'dense 8-byte floats, rowid table 1403 B a bucket; 9 series: 0.55 to 0.67 GB for 30 to 37 days; 26 series: 1.94 GB; 200 series: 14.95 GB'; 'filled bins only, plus 18 catalogue fields 311 B a bucket; ... 200 series: 3.31 GB'. The same probe measures raw rows at 48 B against the brief's 90 B. docs/04:22 'a 2 GB cap that is enforced exactly'. docs/17:141 and docs/06:117 delete raw down to a 24 h floor, then backups, then 'raise a finding'; minute files are never deleted. docs/08:32,34,38,54 promise 'raw retention about 13 h', below the 24 h floor.
- **Impact.** A dense encoding makes the minute tier 5 to 6 times its estimate. With more series (doctor warns only at 200), minute files alone can exceed the budget, and the deletion order cannot reduce them, so 'enforced exactly' is false. The docs/08 status lines promise raw retention below the floor.
- **Proposed change.** docs/06-storage.md 'Disk use after a year': state the bucket encoding (sparse filled bins, about 300 B a bucket) and give the minute size per series, at 50 series and at 200. docs/04-features.md:22: drop 'enforced exactly', or add minute files to the deletion order (decision). docs/08-configuration.md:32,34,38: replace 'about N hours' with 'raw drops toward its 24 h floor; below that a finding is raised'.
- **Cost.** About 10 changed lines across three docs.
- **Decision.** When raw is at its 24 h floor, backups are down to one and the store is still over budget, should the oldest minute files be deleted (down to, say, 7 days), or is the budget a warning only? Recommendation: delete minute files oldest first down to 7 days, then raise a finding; never delete history.
- **Verification.** I re-ran row_sizes.py and budget_arithmetic.py and got identical output, and recomputed the histogram size from the code. I rejected two sub-points. (1) That 72 h of raw plus 0.55 GB of other files puts the 500 ms row over budget: docs/06:110 already gives '2 to 3 daily files, 0.7 to 1.1 GB', and the lead's own measurement is 48 B a raw row rather than 90 B, so docs/08 overstates raw and its status lines are not shown wrong. (2) Binary versus decimal GB: parse_size('2GB') is 2.147e9 B, larger than the decimal figures, so no status changes. At the default 9 series even a dense encoding totals about 1.7 GB with measured raw sizes, so the risk is many series, not the defaults.
- **Leads.** C-storage#11. Earlier findings touched: R03, R23, R29


### R93. The raw schema lacks columns for several catalogue metrics and never reads back its schema version, so the first schema change breaks the day's open file

- **Lens** C · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `plan` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** raw.py:41-52: workload_raw has one memory_bytes; process_raw has no I/O or handle columns; there is no per-core table. docs/06:82-83,86,90,120 promise two memory metrics with the age of the reading, per-process I/O and handles, and per-core load. SampleBatch (models.py:122-131) has no memory_total_bytes, although docs/17:121 says buckets record memory capacity. raw.py:72-76 uses CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS plus 'INSERT OR IGNORE INTO meta ... schema_version'. A grep shows SCHEMA_VERSION is only ever written (raw.py:31,75). The lead's schema_gaps.py re-ran identically: 'a version 2 collector reopened the version 1 file without an error. The file still says schema_version 1 ... Its 11-value insert then fails'.
- **Impact.** Each missing metric forces a schema change. The first change that ships during a day makes every collector write fail until UTC midnight. Minute files live 30 to 37 days, so two changes in that window break docs/17:140's rule that 'readers accept the current and previous versions'.
- **Proposed change.** Plan, as a P1 task: put every catalogue column into schema version 1 of the raw, minute and history tables, nullable, with NULL meaning unavailable. Now: in src/submeter/store/raw.py, read schema_version on open; if it differs, start a new file for the rest of the day under a name that files.py parses, and teach expired_raw that name (files.py:126-128 skips any non-ISO name today). docs/17:140: 'readers accept every version still on disk'.
- **Cost.** About 10 lines and two doc sentences now; the schema columns are a P1 task.
- **Verification.** I re-ran schema_gaps.py and got identical output, read raw.py and models.py, and grepped for schema_version. The lead's third point, that rules.py:208-209 sums unreadable I/O as 0, is a distinct problem already carried by E-baseline#3 and F-consistency#13 in other clusters, so I left it out of this finding and its severity. Medium: nothing fails until the first schema change, but every missing column guarantees one.
- **Leads.** C-storage#12. Earlier findings touched: R36


### R94. What starts an epoch is stated five ways and never settles adaptive cadence; a bucket spanning an epoch or core-count change keeps only the latest values

- **Lens** C, F · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/04:24 'Every change of settings, rules or hardware'; docs/08:5 'every change'; docs/08:116 'Any change to sampling, rules or providers'; docs/06:98 and docs/17:130 'the sampling intervals, the rules file, each provider's status, the hardware and the Submeter version'; docs/17:189 'Every effective change'; raw.py:191 'an interval change, a provider falling back'. Adaptive switching is at docs/08:60, the overhead guard at docs/17:191 and the session interval override at docs/08:74. rollup.py:105,118 apply one epoch and one logical_cores per roll_up call; rollup.py:158-159: merge keeps latest.logical_cores and latest.epoch. The lead's merge_capacity.py re-ran identically: 'CPU 43200 s of 43200 core-seconds of capacity: 100% busy'; 'the merged hour keeps epoch 4 and logical_cores 16 ... = 75%'.
- **Impact.** If adaptive switches count as epochs, which is how 'sampling intervals' and raw.py:191 read, a new epoch starts each time the machine goes quiet (idle_after 60 s), and R32's flag fires on almost every comparison. If they do not count, buckets mix 1 s and 5 s maxima with no record. A bucket that spans a hardware change shows a wrong percentage (75% against a true 100%), breaking docs/06:101.
- **Proposed change.** After the decision, write one definition in docs/06-storage.md:98 and copy it to docs/04:24, docs/08:5, docs/08:116, docs/17:130 and docs/17:189; align the raw.py:191 docstring. src/submeter/store/rollup.py: either make merge refuse mixed epochs, or give Bucket the minimum and maximum interval_s it contains and store capacity as core-seconds (the sum of covered_s x logical_cores), so a merged percentage stays right.
- **Cost.** One paragraph and four one-line doc edits; about 10 lines in rollup.py and two Bucket fields.
- **Decision.** Do changes the collector makes itself start an epoch: adaptive idle/active switching, the overhead guard lengthening intervals, a session's interval override? Options: (a) no; epochs track configured sampling settings, the rules file, provider status, hardware and version, and each bucket records the minimum and maximum interval it contains; (b) yes, every change in the interval actually used. And do alert, UI or verdict-setting edits start an epoch? Recommendation: (a), and no for alert, UI and verdict edits.
- **Verification.** I re-ran merge_capacity.py and got identical output. Grepping docs/ for 'epoch' finds exactly the formulations above, and no sentence covers adaptive mode. R32 introduced epochs without addressing adaptive mode. I kept medium: a core-count change is rare (VM resize, CPU hot-plug), and the ambiguity in the definition corrupts no number until the epoch writer is built, but it decides whether R32's flags mean anything.
- **Leads.** C-storage#13, F-consistency#4. Earlier findings touched: R32, R08


### R95. choose_tier falls back to the minute tier without checking its retention, so it can answer from data that was already deleted

- **Lens** E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** query.py:40-50: the tier list depends only on the window's age against raw retention, and the point-budget fallback never checks the next tier's retention. config.py Retention has raw and minute durations with no ordering check. verify-6/v6_tiers.out.txt: '1. Retention(raw='60d', minute='30d') accepted'; '45 d back, 12 h -> minute step 60 s; covered: False'; '45 d back, 1 h -> minute step 60 s; covered: False'; '59 d back, 30 h -> minute step 60 s; covered: False'. docs/14:332 'Picks the finest tier whose retention still covers the start of the window.' docs/08:80 suggests raising retention.raw to 'Keep full detail longer'.
- **Impact.** With raw retention set above minute retention, history windows 30 to 60 days back come back empty although the raw data exists. Default settings are not affected.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/store/query.py: drop any tier whose retention does not cover the window start; if only raw qualifies, return raw with a wider step. Alternatively, refuse retention.raw > retention.minute in config.py with the allowed range in the message. Add a test.
- **Cost.** About 5 lines and a test.
- **Verification.** I re-ran interface_cases.py and got identical output. I wrote v6_tiers.py, which confirms the settings model accepts raw retention longer than minute retention and shows three uncovered answers. R33 sets the rule and docs/14 restates it; the code's fallback breaks it. Medium because it needs non-default settings, though docs/08 invites them.
- **Audit.** Overlap: See also N29, which the coverage check added: choose_tier takes raw coverage from the retention setting, not from the files on disk.
- **Leads.** E-baseline#18. Earlier findings touched: R33


### R96. Scrub mode, the default, misses the most common ways credentials are passed on a command line

- **Lens** D · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** uv run python docs/review/probes/verify-7/privacy_recheck.py (out: privacy_recheck.out.txt): 'LEAK curl bearer header -> curl -H Authorization: Bearer FAKEtok...', 'LEAK mysql attached -p -> mysql -uroot -pFAKEpw-7x db', 'LEAK curl -u user:pass', 'LEAK bash -c wrapper -> bash -c tool --api-key FAKEtok...', 'LEAK docker --env=NAME=value', 'LEAK conn string ...Password=FAKEpw-7x;'; controls hidden ('mysql --password [redacted]', '-e API_TOKEN=[redacted]', 'https://alice:[redacted]@'). Re-ran d-privacy/redact_leaks.py: output identical, '84 cases, 71 of them not controls; 70 leaked in all'. Cause: redact.py:74-79 hides by name only for '-flag value' or NAME=value with the name before the first '='; everything else goes to redact_text (redact.py:47-52: 5 token shapes + scheme://user:pw@). argv[0] is never scrubbed (redact.py:67). Promise: docs/04-features.md:43 'Command lines are scrubbed of secrets before they are stored'; docs/14:192 'Errs towards hiding too much.' Attribution uses the live argv (rules.py:84-86), so the stored form does not affect attribution numbers.
- **Impact.** Bearer headers, attached -p passwords, user:pass, wrapped shells and connection strings reach raw/*.db (48 h) and the process drill-down API. The R04 fix does not hold for these forms, and docs/04:43 and docs/14:192 state the opposite.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md:146, docs/08-configuration.md:104, docs/04-features.md:43: make exe_only the default; describe scrub as opt-in and best-effort ('hides known shapes; it cannot recognise every credential'). docs/14:192: drop 'Errs towards hiding too much' or qualify it. src/submeter/core/config.py:186 and raw.py:64,247: default 'exe_only'. Add the redact_leaks cases as a test that records which forms scrub misses.
- **Cost.** Three sentences, a default in three places, one test
- **Decision.** Default redaction: keep scrub (best-effort, misses common forms) or switch to exe_only (no arguments stored; loses detail in the process drill-down, not attribution numbers)? Recommendation: exe_only by default, scrub opt-in and documented as best-effort.
- **Verification.** Re-ran redact_leaks.py (identical counts) and wrote my own probe with six common forms plus controls; all six leaked, controls hidden, so the probe is wired correctly. Read redact.py:62-83 and tests/test_baseline.py:171-194 (tests only the handled forms). Searched docs for a stated limit on scrub: none; docs/14:192 claims the reverse. Changed the framing: '70 of 71' comes from a corpus built around the redactor's blind spots, so it is not a prevalence figure; the title now says what is verified. Severity kept at high because the fix for a critical prior finding (R04) fails under real conditions; no fixed rule is broken since nothing leaves the machine.
- **Audit.** Severity changed from high to medium. Re-ran verify-7/privacy_recheck.py. The output is identical except for a live count (290 -> 289 readable command lines). All six common forms leak (bearer header, -pPASS, -u user:pass, bash -c, --env=NAME=value, connection string) and the controls are hidden. The cause is in redact.py:74-82: names are only checked in '-flag value' and NAME=value, and anything else is caught only by token shape or the whole-argument opaque test. The leaked secrets stay in local files and behind the token-protected loopback API. None of the three fixed rules covers that (AGENTS.md:9-11 name observe-only, no elevation, and no upload, webhook, telemetry or update check). Attribution uses the live argv (rules.py:84-86), and no phase gate depends on redaction; the P6 gate concerns MCP responses only. 'A prior critical fix fails' is not one of the severity definitions. What remains is misleading, because docs/04:43 and redact.py:6 ('errs towards hiding too much') promise the opposite: medium.
- **Audit.** Correction: Switching the default to exe_only needs P2's fix first. exe_only returns args[0] (redact.py:64-65), which is a whole process title when a program rewrote it: privacy_recheck P2 found 12 local cases, for example 'postgres: alice appdb 10.0.0.5(5432) idle'. Without P2, the new default would still store user names and addresses. P7's 0644 files make these secrets readable by other local users on Linux; that is still on the machine, so still medium.
- **Leads.** D-privacy#1. Earlier findings touched: R04


### R97. exe_only keeps argv[0], which is a whole process title when a process rewrote it

- **Lens** D · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** redact.py:64-65 'if mode == "exe_only": return (args[0],)'. privacy_recheck.out.txt P2: 'readable non-empty command lines: 290 / NUL split gives one string with spaces (a direct /proc reader): 64 / psutil argv[0] contains a space, so exe_only stores it whole: 12 / exe_only on a one-item argv: ('postgres: alice appdb 10.0.0.5(5432) idle',)'. psutil splits titles without a trailing NUL on spaces (.venv psutil/_pslinux.py:1763-1771), so through the baseline provider (psutil_provider.py:69) only NUL-padded titles stay whole. docs/17:71 'On Linux, read /proc directly' means the planned provider would see all 64 unless it copies psutil's split. scrub mode also keeps argv[0] unscrubbed (redact.py:67).
- **Impact.** The strict mode, documented as 'stores the executable only' (docs/17:146, docs/08:104), stores whole titles (user, database, client address for postgres) for 12 processes here via psutil and 64 via a NUL-only reader. No secret was shown in such titles, so this breaks the promise rather than leaking credentials.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/privacy/redact.py (or raw.py:153-154): in exe_only store sample.exe, the executable name, never args[0]. In scrub mode, split an argv[0] that contains spaces and scrub it like the rest. Test with ('postgres: alice appdb 10.0.0.5(5432) idle',). docs/17 Collection: the /proc provider splits a one-string cmdline as psutil does.
- **Cost.** About 5 lines plus one test and one sentence
- **Verification.** Wrote my own count probe comparing a NUL-only split with psutil.cmdline() on live processes (counts only). The lead's 64 holds for a NUL-only reader but the baseline provider (psutil) stores the whole title in only 12 cases, because psutil splits on spaces when the data has no trailing NUL; corrected the claim that psutil passes titles through unsplit. Downgraded high to medium: the evidence shows identifying titles, not secrets, and no job or gate fails. Added that scrub mode never scrubs argv[0] (redact.py:67). Windows not checked (psutil splits there in C).
- **Leads.** D-privacy#2. Earlier findings touched: R04


### R98. Logs, validation errors, reprs and event rows bypass the store's redaction

- **Lens** D · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** privacy_recheck.out.txt P5: 'repr(ProcessCounters) contains the secret: True' / 'str(ValidationError) contains the secret: True' (cmdline given as one string) / 'redact_text on a log line keeps the secret: True' ('launch failed: tool --api-key FAKEtok...'). models.py:19 sets only extra and frozen; cmdline and cwd at models.py:77-78 and 111-112 have no repr=False. raw.py:190-193 record_event stores detail as given. Promise: docs/17:137 and docs/06-storage.md:19 'with the same redaction as the store'.
- **Impact.** A log line built from a record, an exception or a provider error can carry the unredacted command line into logs/*.log (25 MB kept) or event rows, in either redaction mode.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/core/models.py:19 add hide_input_in_errors=True; Field(repr=False) on cmdline and cwd at models.py:77-78 and 111-112. docs/17:137 and docs/06:19: 'Logs never contain command lines, working folders or the token: they name a process by pid and executable, and validation errors are logged without input values.' Event details are structured fields, never free text taken from a process.
- **Cost.** About 6 lines and two sentences
- **Verification.** Reproduced (a), (b) and (c) with my own probe; read raw.py:190-193 for (d). Corrected the lead's line numbers (models.py:17 -> 19, 75-76 -> 77-78, 109-110 -> 111-112). Did not re-fetch the Pydantic config page (medium finding); behaviour reproduced locally instead. Searched docs for a rule keeping command lines out of logs: none.
- **Leads.** D-privacy#5. Earlier findings touched: R04, R30


### R99. Scrub hides harmless arguments, and cmdline rules match text the user never sees

- **Lens** D, attribution · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** privacy_recheck.out.txt P6: 'python [redacted]' (script path, no extension), 'optimizer --passes [redacted] model.onnx', 'trainer --tokenizer [redacted]', 'git -C [redacted] status', 'claude --resume [redacted]' (UUID); control 'tool --max-tokens 4096' kept. Causes: _OPAQUE (redact.py:36) includes '/' and '-' so long POSIX paths and UUIDs qualify; _SECRET_NAME (redact.py:21-25) is an unanchored search, so 'pass' hits --passes and 'token(?!s)' hits --tokenizer. Rules match the live argv (rules.py:84-86), so a rule copied from the stored text can miss.
- **Impact.** The process drill-down loses the arguments that tell agent sessions, checkouts and scripts apart (live diagnosis). A cmdline rule written from the shown text may never match, leaving CPU Unattributed.
- **Proposed change.** If scrub stays (see P1), src/submeter/privacy/redact.py: anchor _SECRET_NAME to whole words of the flag (bounded by start, '-', '_' or end); exempt strings with a path separator or UUID shape from _OPAQUE; add tests. docs/17 Interfaces: cmdline rules match the unredacted live command line, and `submeter rules test` reads the live process and marks which arguments the store hides.
- **Cost.** About 6 lines plus tests, one sentence
- **Verification.** Reproduced five of the lead's cases with my own probe. docs/14:192 accepts over-hiding of commit hashes only. Severity stays medium; it falls to low if P1 makes exe_only the default, because scrub then becomes opt-in.
- **Leads.** D-privacy#6. Earlier findings touched: R04


### R100. Data folders and files are created 0755/0644, readable by other local users

- **Lens** D, E · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** privacy_recheck.out.txt P7 (umask 0022, temporary SUBMETER_HOME): 'dir 0o755 home/data', 'home/data/raw', 'home/state'; 'file 0o644 .../2026-11-20.db', '-shm', '-wal'; 'file 0o644 home/state/collector.pid'; only 'collector.lock' is 0o600 (files.py:222). Code: files.py:119 mkdir(parents=True), raw.py:66-68 sqlite3.connect default mode, files.py:230 write_text. Rule: docs/17:146 'Restrict every data file and the API token to the current user.' Fix works on POSIX (d-privacy/private_files.out.txt: dirs 0700, db/-wal/-shm 0600). Python docs (re-fetched https://docs.python.org/3.12/library/os.html#os.mkdir): 'On Windows, a mode of 0o700 is specifically handled to apply access control to the new directory such that only the current user and administrators have access. Other values of mode are ignored.' and 'Changed in version 3.12.4: Windows now handles a mode of 0o700.' pyproject.toml:5 requires-python '>=3.12'.
- **Impact.** On a shared Linux machine, or with SUBMETER_HOME outside a private folder, other users can read process names, workloads and stored command lines. A token file written the same way would expose the whole API.
- **Proposed change.** src/submeter/store/files.py ensure(): create each level under the Submeter root with os.mkdir(path, 0o700) and chmod existing levels on POSIX. src/submeter/store/raw.py: os.open(path, O_CREAT|O_RDWR, 0o600) before sqlite3.connect. Pid file and token: os.open(..., 0o600). Backups into a file made 0600 first. pyproject.toml: requires-python '>=3.12.4'. doctor flags any Submeter path readable by others. Tests under umask 0022.
- **Cost.** About 25 lines in files.py and raw.py, tests, one line in pyproject
- **Verification.** Re-ran the modes with my own probe under umask 0022 and re-fetched the Python os.mkdir docs. Mitigation: Ubuntu's default home folder is 0750 and %LOCALAPPDATA% sits under the per-user profile, so the default paths are partly protected; SUBMETER_HOME (files.py:48-51) can point anywhere. The two leads state the same problem; kept D-privacy#7's wider evidence and fix.
- **Leads.** D-privacy#7, E-baseline#21. Earlier findings touched: R30


### R101. The brief does not say how each client carries the token, and the obvious browser designs put it in URLs, logs or cookies

- **Lens** D · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/17:148 'Require a token on every HTTP and WebSocket request ... and check Origin on the WebSocket'; grep finds no transport, cookie or CSP rule in docs/. docs/05:37-39: dashboard, CLI and agents all reach the API with the token. MDN (re-fetched https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/WebSocket/WebSocket): syntax is only 'new WebSocket(url)' and 'new WebSocket(url, protocols)'. RFC 6265 (re-fetched) section 8.5: 'Cookies do not provide isolation by port.' Origin scope disagrees: docs/17:148 (WebSocket only) vs docs/17:272 and docs/15:25 ('requests ... with a foreign Origin are refused'). redact_leaks.out.txt: a '?range=1h&token=' URL survives scrub.
- **Impact.** A browser cannot send an Authorization header on page load or on a WebSocket, so a builder falls back to a query-string token (access logs, history, the browser's command line) or a cookie sent to every localhost port. The phase-4 gate passes for each of these designs.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17-build-prompt.md 'Privacy and local security': CLI and MCP send the token in an Authorization header read from the token file. The dashboard shell is served without a token and never embeds one; the CLI opens it with the token in the URL fragment, which the page moves to sessionStorage and strips. API calls use the header; the WebSocket sends the token as its first message and is closed without it within 2 s. No cookies; a query-string token is refused. Host and Origin are checked on every HTTP, WebSocket and MCP request. Access logs omit query strings. rotate-token closes open WebSockets. Phase-4 gate: logs and raw files from the test run do not contain the token.
- **Cost.** One paragraph and one gate clause
- **Verification.** Re-read docs/17:148, 272, docs/15:25 and docs/05:37-39; grepped docs for a token transport (none). Re-fetched MDN and RFC 6265 and found both quoted sentences. Did not re-fetch the uvicorn source for the access-log format (medium finding). R04's fix names the token and the Host and Origin checks only, so it does not cover transport.
- **Leads.** D-privacy#8. Earlier findings touched: R04


### R102. Clients send the token to whatever holds the port, and the default 8787 is Wrangler's dev-server port

- **Lens** D · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** Re-ran d-privacy/port_first.py (loopback only): 'the stand-in, which is not Submeter, received: ['Authorization: Bearer FAKE-api-token']' / 'a server starting afterwards cannot bind the port: Address already in use'. config.py:197 and docs/08:107 default port 8787. Cloudflare (re-fetched https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/wrangler/configuration/): 'port: Port for the local dev server to listen on. Defaults to 8787.' docs/05:37-39: CLI and agents use the API with the token. No document covers port-in-use handling.
- **Impact.** With `wrangler dev` running, Submeter's server cannot start and the CLI and MCP send the token to the Worker and show its answers. On a multi-user machine another user can hold the port while the server is down and collect the token.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17 'Privacy and local security': after binding, the server writes state/api.json (port, pid, start time, 0600); clients read the port from it, connect to literal 127.0.0.1, and confirm the listener before sending the token (pid check, or an HMAC(token, nonce) challenge); if the port is taken, the server refuses to start and names the holder. docs/08:107 and config.py:197: a default not used by common dev servers, or 0 written to api.json.
- **Cost.** Three sentences and one default value
- **Verification.** Re-ran the port probe and re-fetched the Wrangler docs. A same-user squatter gains nothing because it can read the token file, and Windows 11 work machines are usually single-user, so the realistic case is a collision with a developer tool. Severity stays medium (cheap to fix, and a server that cannot start leaves live diagnosis unavailable). Windows SO_EXCLUSIVEADDRUSE and localhost resolution order were not checked.
- **Leads.** D-privacy#9. Earlier findings touched: R04


### R103. Documents disagree on what MCP withholds, and findings may carry command lines into history and MCP

- **Lens** D, F · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** no
- **Evidence.** docs/17:148 'The MCP server must never return command lines or working directories'; docs/17:193, docs/08-configuration.md:125 and docs/05-data-flow.md:50 name command lines only; gate docs/17:274 'no MCP response contains a command line'. AGENTS.md:41 'A command line is redacted before it is stored, logged, put in a finding ...', so findings may hold (scrubbed) command lines; findings live in history.db, 'Kept' (docs/06:15), and docs/17:152 gives findings 'the evidence that triggered them' with no restriction.
- **Impact.** Working folders can reach MCP with no gate testing for them. A command line put in a finding outlives the 48 h raw retention and is served to agents through findings, against the MCP rule; with scrub's misses (P1) that can include credentials.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17:148, docs/17:193 and docs/08:125: 'The MCP server returns workload names, numbers, findings and verdicts only: never command lines, working folders, executable paths or the token. This limits what reaches an agent's context; it does not stop an agent running as the same user from reading the data files.' docs/17 Analysis: 'Evidence names workloads, executables and metrics, never a command line.' AGENTS.md:41 (owner approval): drop 'put in a finding'. Gate docs/17:274: 'no MCP response contains a stored command line, a working folder or the token'.
- **Cost.** Four sentences
- **Verification.** Re-read all five passages and AGENTS.md:41. Changed the emphasis: the 'courtesy, not boundary' point is documentation clarity (low); the verified problem is the inconsistent lists plus AGENTS.md:41 allowing command lines in findings, which are kept forever and readable through MCP. The claim that a hosted agent sends tool results to its model provider is inference and was not used for severity.
- **Leads.** D-privacy#10. Earlier findings touched: R04, R25


### R104. The WSL guest collector's 'ingest API' conflicts with the loopback-only, read-only API, and default WSL networking cannot reach host loopback

- **Lens** D, fixed rule 3 · **Severity** medium · **Status** verified · **Disposition** `fix now` · **Needs your decision** yes
- **Evidence.** docs/16-review-log.md:81 (R05 fix) 'The ingest API accepts a second collector'; docs/17:79 'Design the ingest path so a second collector running inside the guest can report to the host'; docs/17:254 API 'read-only except session markers'; docs/17:50 programs 'share nothing but files on disk'. Microsoft (re-fetched https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/wsl/networking): 'By default, WSL uses a NAT (Network Address Translation) based architecture'; 'If you want to access a networking app running on Windows ... from your Linux distribution (ie Ubuntu), then you need to use the IP address of your host machine.'; 'you may need to bind your application to 0.0.0.0 instead of 127.0.0.1'; mirrored mode needs 'Windows 11 22H2 and higher' and a .wslconfig setting, then 'Connect to Windows servers from within Linux using the localhost address 127.0.0.1'.
- **Impact.** Phase 7, which can move to right after phase 2 (docs/17:264), either binds beyond loopback under default WSL (breaks fixed rule 3), depends on the user opting into mirrored mode, or adds a write route and hands the token to a guest VM.
- **Proposed change.** docs/17:79 and the R05 fix text in docs/16:81: name a transport with no network path and no ingest route.
